./tests/run_credit.sh      # stdlib/credit.bas -- credit analytics over a PORTFOLIO (docs/credit_analytics_design.md), Phase 3's second increment and the item lending_design §8 deferred until fake.plant existed. `lending` answers questions about ONE loan; this answers questions about a BOOK -- vintage curves, roll rates, migration, charge-off -- none of which is derivable from a single loan or is arithmetic about balances: they are questions about STATES OVER TIME. THE INPUT IS A STATUS TABLE, NOT A LIST OF LOANS, and everything follows from that: `apply` is a fold, so a 5,000-loan book over 36 month-ends is 180,000 folds, and real portfolio data arrives as a monthly performance record per loan (the shape published loan-level datasets and any servicer extract take) -- a library that could only read our own loans could not be pointed at a real book. `credit.observe` is the BRIDGE, so our machinery is a producer rather than a special case. DELINQUENCY IS DECLARED AND THE INDUSTRY DISAGREES: mba counts PAYMENTS MISSED (30 days delinquent the day after one is missed), ots counts DAYS PAST DUE, and on a monthly loan they run a month apart for the whole life of a delinquency -- asserted as a DIFFERENCE (mba harsher on every row where they differ), never as a value. ATTRITION IS A STATE, NOT A HOLE: paid_off and charged_off are buckets nothing rolls out of, and a loan present at t and missing at t+1 is reported `unobserved` rather than dropped -- dropping it makes a book look like it is CURING, an ordinary-looking percentage with nothing raised. That gives the load-bearing invariant, the counterpart of the accounting equation: EVERY LOAN OBSERVED AT t IS ACCOUNTED FOR AT t+1, row totals plus unobserved equalling the starting bucket. Never enforced anywhere -- it falls out of correct bucketing, which is what makes it a good test. AND THE TIER THAT KEEPS IT FROM BEING VACUOUS is the one where four accounts GO SILENT: `observe` emits a row for every date once a loan is written, so nothing ever disappears from a table it produced and a library that never counted attrition would reconcile perfectly (measured: with the unobserved count removed, the main book reports ZERO mismatches and only the silent tier goes red). The rate form needs its OWN check for the same reason, since reconciliation runs on counts. VINTAGE IS MONTHS ON BOOK, NEVER CALENDAR MONTH -- indexed by calendar every cohort's curve starts at a different age and the result is a smooth meaningless line that does not look like a mistake; a cohort's curve STOPS at the age it has reached, so an unlived age is absent rather than 0% ("no losses" where the truth is "no data"). basis original/outstanding is REQUIRED, and the outstanding denominator counts a delinquent loan ONCE (`is_bad or still_alive`, not the sum). A charge-off is READ from the record, never inferred from days past due, and gross/recoveries are reported separately. THE PLANTED-PORTFOLIO TIER is what §8 asked for: four of twenty accounts in the middle cohort stop paying, chosen by `fake.sample` with NO marker in the data, and vintage must report exactly 4/20 for that cohort and 0 for its neighbours. Self-checking not golden, forced: every defect here produces an ordinary-looking PERCENTAGE. Eight perturbations proven red, each caught by the tier written for it. Fixture is ~12s. Headless, GI-independent, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_scoring.sh      # stdlib/scoring.bas -- credit scorecards (docs/scoring_design.md), the first of the two items credit_analytics_design §9 deferred until the measurement layer and the population existed. `credit` measures what a book has DONE; this turns a population into a model that RANKS risk and then into the artefact a credit committee approves. DELIBERATELY THE CREDIT-SPECIFIC HALF ONLY -- stats.logistic_regression is the engine and is NOT reimplemented; what stats has no reason to carry is WOE/IV binning, discrimination the way lenders measure it, calibration onto a point scale, and PSI. SELF-CHECKING AND FORCED: every defect here is a PLAUSIBLE NUMBER -- a WOE orientation that flips every sign yields a scorecard with the SAME discrimination pointed at the people who will not pay; an AUC below 0.5 quietly turned the right way up converts the field's most consequential error into a mediocre-looking result that gets deployed. EXPECTED FIGURES COMPUTED IN PYTHON from the bin counts, since a fixture asserting what the library said is a transcript. TWO TIERS CARRY THE LOAD AND NEITHER IS A VALUE COMPARISON: ORIENTATION IS A DIFFERENCE (the two conventions must give WOE of equal magnitude and OPPOSITE SIGN -- every value check in the file passes on a library that hardcodes one convention), and DIRECTION runs the whole pipeline end to end (bin, fit a real logistic regression on the WOE column, scale to points, require the KNOWN BADS to score BELOW the known goods by a material margin -- every other tier checks a component, this one checks they are wired the right way up). AUC is checked TWICE OVER: against the external value AND against a BRUTE-FORCE PAIR COUNT written in the fixture, which is a second implementation rather than the same rank identity called twice. Refusals each beside their nearest LEGAL neighbour -- the empty-bin refusal's control is the SAME bin accepted once a smoothing is DECLARED, since smoothing invents evidence in proportion to how small the bin is and the output cannot show that. Five perturbations proven red, each caught by the tier written for it: orientation ignored, AUC silently flipped, points reversed, empty cell smoothed by default, ties given full credit. NAMED `scoring` NOT `scorecard` because examples/edgar/scorecard.bas already exists and `forensics` already calls its output scorecard frames -- the collision also tripped run_stdlib_docs, and an IGNORE entry there would have weakened a real check. Headless, GI-independent, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_deposits.sh    # stdlib/deposits.bas -- deposit interest, crediting and certificates (docs/lending_design.md §7). A SEPARATE LIBRARY FROM LENDING ON PURPOSE: `waterfall`, `delinquency` and per-diem mean nothing to a savings account, and `tier`, crediting schedule and early-withdrawal penalty mean nothing to a mortgage; what they genuinely share is simple interest over a day count, and that was pushed DOWN into `finance.accrue` once lending had proved what it is -- a deposit does not borrow, so lending must not own it. THE BALANCE-METHOD TIER IS A DIFFERENCE, like lending's accrual basis: a large late withdrawal earns 28.77 under `daily` and 4.11 under `minimum`, which is why banks name the method in the account terms. AND ONE PAIR THAT DOES *NOT* DIFFER, ASSERTED AS EQUAL AND EXPLAINED: simple interest is LINEAR in the balance, so the average balance earns exactly what each day's balance earns -- `daily` and `average_daily` agree at a constant rate and part company only under tiers. Asserting they differ would assert something false, and this is the tier that says so, which matters because the neighbouring lending suite exists to catch exactly the opposite mistake. COMPOUNDING IS NOT CREDITING (the common error): interest is computed per crediting period and added at the end of it, and interest since the last crediting date is reported SEPARATELY, because the holder has earned it and not been paid it. THE PENALTY MAY EXCEED THE INTEREST and then reduces PRINCIPAL -- 90 days of penalty against 31 days earned -- and clamping at zero would report proceeds the holder will not receive; the maturity case is the control, without which a library that always penalised would pass every case above. A defect found by the fixture and not by reading: `_runs` applied EVERY event to its running balance, including ones before the window the caller had already rolled in, so a withdrawal in an earlier crediting period was replayed in every later one and raised "exceeds the balance" against a balance the first replay had already reduced
./tests/run_lending.sh     # stdlib/lending.bas -- loans, servicing and payoff (docs/lending_design.md), Phase 3 first increment. PHASE 1 ANSWERS WHAT THE PAYMENT IS; this answers WHAT HAPPENS NEXT, which is not a formula -- pay late, partly or extra, change the rate, pay off on the 14th -- so the conventions are the subject and the arithmetic is small. The loan DECLARES basis, waterfall and day count and the library never assumes, the same rule finance set for period rates. SELF-CHECKING AND FORCED: every defect here produces an ORDINARY-LOOKING BALANCE, and TWO OF THREE HAPPENED WHILE WRITING IT, both caught by the tests the design asked for rather than by reading. (1) The first `_accrue` PRORATED the amortized basis by days, which makes it ALGEBRAICALLY IDENTICAL to daily simple -- the two bases agreed to the cent and the declaration was decorative; the design's "the difference must be the days" test is what found it. (2) The second counted whole periods WITHIN each gap, so events 14 and 17 days apart each rounded to zero and NO INTEREST EVER ACCRUED -- a weekly-payment loan would run free forever, and a single-payment test cannot see it. THE DIFFERENCE TIERS ARE THE POINT: basis and waterfall are asserted as DIFFERENCES rather than values, since the same partial payment under two waterfalls has equal TOTALS by construction and only the components move. THE LEDGER TIER PROVES THE REST (design §5): a loan's whole life posted to a real accounting ledger must balance with receivables equal to the servicing balance and interest income equal to interest collected -- an unbalanced entry or phantom account is refused where posted, so a loan that posts cleanly has DEMONSTRATED its arithmetic. `apply` is a FOLD, chosen for auditability: state is derived from the record of what happened, so "why is this balance what it is" is answerable by replay, where stored incremental state makes the number itself the answer with nothing to reconstruct it from. `history` is opt-in so a portfolio scan is not O(n) in memory per loan. Underwriting returns `unknown` for a missing input, NEVER zero, since an absent income that became zero makes every ratio look perfect. KNOWN COST recorded rather than optimised: `entries` folds each prefix, so it is O(n^2) in events -- nothing at a loan's scale, and the remedy is the checkpoint the design already names
./tests/run_accounting.sh  # double-entry accounting (stdlib/accounting.bas, docs/accounting_design.md) -- Phase 2 of the finance platform, MOVED AHEAD of lending because double-entry is the substrate lending events post INTO. SELF-CHECKING rather than golden, and here that is forced rather than preferred: EVERY failure mode double-entry exists to prevent leaves a BALANCED ledger and a plausible statement -- right amounts on the WRONG SIDE, a close run twice, an account absent from the chart -- so a golden would record the damaged figures as expected and none of those defects would move a line of it a reader would question. The load-bearing assertion is ARITHMETIC: assets = liabilities + equity + earnings must hold after every posting AND after closing, which states what must be TRUE rather than what the library said; the balance-sheet identity is never ENFORCED anywhere, it falls out of the one invariant plus correct account typing, which is exactly what makes it a good test. TWO gBASIC PROPERTIES DO MOST OF THE DEFENSIVE WORK and were measured before being relied on: `money` REFUSES to add different currencies, so the commonest accounting bug raises instead of totalling nonsense, and money is an exact integer so a ledger that balances stays balanced with no float to sweep into a rounding account. A line carries `debit` OR `credit`, never a signed amount -- "is a negative debit a credit" is a question that yields statements which balance and are backwards. THE SIGN TIER exists because a normal-side error is INVISIBLE to the trial balance (debits still equal credits when both are on the wrong side), so there is one assertion per account kind and no untested quadrant. CLOSING IS REFUSED on an already-closed period rather than made idempotent, since running it twice doubles equity while leaving everything balanced. 7 refusals each beside their nearest LEGAL neighbour, and one of those controls -- "a later entry still posts after a close" -- is what distinguishes sealing a PERIOD from sealing the ledger. `post` RETURNS the new ledger because inside a function `append` mutates a local copy, so a mutate-in-place API would silently do nothing to the caller's value. Building it found that MONEY COULD NOT REPORT ITS OWN CURRENCY at all, which blocked per-currency balancing outright; `money.currency(amount)` was added (DOGFOOD 2026-08-30). Headless, GI-independent, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_exact.sh       # EXACT INTEGERS -- exactness as a property of a `number`, not a second type (docs/exact_number_design.md). THE DEFECT: `number` was the only numeric kind and it was a double, so integer arithmetic went silently wrong above 2^53 -- and the inconsistency that made it a DEFECT rather than a limit is INTERNAL, because run_odbc's exactness tier inserts 2^53+1 and reads it back as a STRING precisely so the digits are not lost. gBASIC refused to lose precision crossing a driver and lost it in its own arithmetic; one of those two positions was wrong. ONE NUMERIC KIND, which is Scheme's model and Lua 5.3's: `type()` still answers `number`, so there is no 28th kind and NO PROMOTION MATRIX TO GET WRONG -- and that was the load-bearing rejection rather than a cost saving, because an `integer` and a `number` are both numbers, so every mixed expression would need a promotion rule and a wrong one is a silent wrong answer IN PRECISELY THE CLASS BEING CLOSED (PLAT-EQ needed three instalments to get comparison right across kinds that already existed). SELF-CHECKING NOT GOLDEN AND FORCED: the defect produces a PLAUSIBLE NUMBER ONE DIGIT OUT, which is exactly how it survived from the type's introduction. THE ORACLE IS python3's ARBITRARY-PRECISION INTS recomputing all 407 values, which is what catches an implementation that is self-consistently wrong where tier 1 states values written by hand; it carries its own can-disagree check (7^19 differs in double arithmetic) so it cannot pass by comparing nothing. COMPARISON IS WHERE A MISTAKE WOULD BE INVISIBLE and is asserted both ways: the exact side is moved into the INTEGER domain rather than the double side being widened, because the obvious implementation converts the exact value to a double first and answers `9007199254740993 = 9007199254740992.0` TRUE, reintroducing the defect inside the comparison. OVERFLOW DEGRADES RATHER THAN REFUSING (warning 2112), deliberately: refusing would stop a program that answers today, and the answer is the one it always got -- what is new is that the loss is loud. `number("...")` BECAME A SOURCE OF EXACTNESS 2026-10-12 AND THE GAP IS THE INTERESTING PART: the design's "Where exactness comes from" lists four sources and increment 1 shipped ONE, so the settled ruling on `1e20` -- which names `number("...")` as THE way to ask for an exact integer -- pointed at a remedy that silently handed back an inexact number. This fixture STATED that remedy in a comment and asserted nothing about it. Two things hid it, both recorded elsewhere as lessons: BELOW 2^53 IT IS INVISIBLE (an exact and an inexact integer render identically through `string` and `encode`, measured, so every value check passes on the broken build and only `+ 1` separates them -- the tier asserts ARITHMETIC for that reason), and THERE WERE TWO DOORS (the `number()` call and the `{number}` modifier were two independent copies of `strtod`, so they share one helper now and a check asserts the modifier agrees -- a fix reaching one door is the shape webclient's read and write halves had). Found by checking a BOOK CHAPTER's claim against the tree, not by any gate. FOUR CONTROLS beside it, each a settled decision rather than caution: exponent TEXT is inexact like an exponent LITERAL (the notation means "approximately this magnitude" whichever door it arrives through), a fraction is inexact, hex text is inexact although the hex LITERAL is exact (the lexer has a token to inspect; `number("0x10")` would rest on a strtod quirk nothing documents), and past int64 degrades rather than raising. AND IT MOVED TWO ASSERTIONS THAT ENCODED THE OLD WORLD, which is the negative-control pattern working: run_odbc asserted that `number(a_bigint_string)` LOSES the digit, and odbc_cookbook recipe 4 printed "<- one less, silently" into its golden while its prose still said "a gBASIC number is a double" -- stale since 0.6.0 and invisible because the one conversion it demonstrated was the one increment 1 missed. Both now assert the narrower truth, with the control moved to the FRACTIONAL decimal, which is where the loss that forces a text return still lives. INCREMENT 5 SHIPPED 2026-10-13 AND IT WAS WIDER THAN THE INCREMENT, which is the part to read: the design named THREE things (`pow`, `mod`, the counting builtins) and MEASURING with b = 2^53+1 found NINE more wrong -- `min max median percentile quantile sum abs floor ceil round` all collapsed the digit. WHAT FOUND THEM WAS A RULE RATHER THAN A LIST, supplied by the books session: a function that PICKS a value preserves exactness, one that CALCULATES does not. The design's propagation table was an ENUMERATION OF OPERATIONS, and an enumeration is a list a new builtin silently stays off -- which is precisely what had happened to the five selectors; it is rewritten as that rule's consequences. `mod(9007199254740993, 2)` answered 0, a WRONG ANSWER rather than lost precision, and a parity test is the commonest thing anyone asks `mod`. `min`/`max` WERE A DEFECT RATHER THAN A DECISION AND THE PROOF IS THE NEIGHBOUR: over text they already returned the ELEMENT and only the numeric path recomputed a double, so two halves of one builtin disagreed about what it does. AND `sort` HAD NEVER HEARD OF EXACTNESS, which nothing had filed: increment 2 wrote the mathematical comparison into eval_comparison while sort_value_compare compared `as.num.value`, so two exact integers rounding to the same double TIED and a stable sort left them alone -- `b > b - 1` answered correctly while `sort` put `b` FIRST, two routes to one question disagreeing, the shape PLAT-EQ needed three instalments to close for the value KINDS happening once more for EXACTNESS. One comparison now, shared, which also fixed `sort_by` and the element a median or percentile picks. THE COUNTING LINE IS STATED RATHER THAN INFERRED: 86 `value_number((double)...)` sites exist and FIVE are exact -- the line is quantities a program does ARITHMETIC with (a length, a count, an index, an epoch) against identifiers and coordinates (a handle, an HTTP status, a diagnostic column), because a blanket sweep makes 86 new claims for no measured need. TEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED and TWO OF THEM FOUND WEAK ASSERTIONS OF MINE, both the same shape: the counting tier used THREE-element lists and above 2^53 doubles step in TWOS, so `len([1,2,3]) + b` lands on the same answer either way and reverting `len` to a double left every check green -- and the comment directly above that check ALREADY SAID `len([1,2,3]) + b` read correctly by luck while `find` did not, so the trap was documented and then written into. Counts of one and two now. Second: `epoch` has THREE return paths (clock, local datetime, named zone) and only one was asserted; the two that cannot be pinned by value are asserted as a DIFFERENCE with the operand DOUBLED first, since an even operand is representable above 2^53 where a bare `epoch()` would make the check depend on the parity of the current second. INCREMENT 4 SHIPPED 2026-10-23 as `serialize` version 3 (run_serialize.sh owns it), so every numbered increment is in. Headless, no network, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_serialize.sh    # `serialize` VERSION 3 -- the PORTABLE value encoding G-SQL's rows and client protocol share with the public builtin (docs/gsql_storage_design.md §3.3), and the close of exact_number_design.md's increment 4. Versions 1 and 2 copied integers in the WRITING MACHINE's byte order, justified by "round-trips are always same-binary" -- true for an actor and false for a payload in a file, which is copied between machines and outlives the binary that wrote it -- and a number carried only its double, so 2^53 + 1 came back as 2^53. v3 writes every fixed field LITTLE-ENDIAN by shifting (never memcpy), every length and count as unsigned LEB128, a number as an EXACTNESS BYTE then its int64 or its double's bits, and dates/durations FIELD BY FIELD (v2 wrote the C struct, padding included). The reader still takes 1 and 2. SELF-CHECKING NOT GOLDEN, forced: every defect is a plausible value one digit out. THE ORACLE TIER IS THE ONE TO READ: every other check reads back through our own `deserialize`, so a writer and reader agreeing on a WRONG layout pass all of them -- PROVEN, flipping the byte order on BOTH sides left every round trip green -- so tests/serialize/decode_v3.py decodes the dump with Python's `struct` from the written layout and compares against values written by hand, and the PINNED-BYTES tier is the same claim in the fixture. THE OLD-VERSION TIER READS A v2 PAYLOAD THE PRE-v3 BINARY REALLY WROTE (built from 2f77bb9 in a scratch worktree), because a fixture this binary wrote would test v3 against itself. MONEY CARRIES ITS ALPHABETIC CODE NOW, which the design did not have: a REGISTERED currency's number is handed out in registration order, so the same number names different currencies in two programs, asserted across two processes that register in different orders. CORRUPTION: every prefix of a payload holding every kind is refused, with the control that the whole one is accepted, plus a LEB128 length that WRAPS past 64 bits to a plausible 2 with two bytes after it -- the first draft's overflow case was ALSO too long for the payload, so the length check refused it anyway and removing the tenth-byte guard left it green. AND A CONSUMER MOVED: `frame` groups by comparing serializations, and an exact 3 and a computed 3.0 now differ while `=` calls them equal, so `frame._sig` signs a number by its double whenever the double holds it without loss (asserted: 3, 6/2, 3 are one group; 2^53+1 and 2^53 are two). Eight perturbations proven red. Headless; the oracle skips without python3, saying the layout was cross-checked by nothing but our own reader
./tests/run_gsql_key.sh     # G-SQL's INDEX KEY ENCODING (src/gsql/key.c, docs/gsql_storage_design.md §3.2) -- a value becomes bytes whose MEMCMP order is gBASIC's own order, Phase 0a's first artefact and needed whichever storage candidate wins. ITS INPUT IS A `serialize` v3 VALUE, which is what a G-SQL row holds, so it needs no interpreter and is compiled here alone; the tags come from include/serialize_tags.h, MOVED there out of src/eval.c so the reader and the writer cannot hold two copies. THE ORACLE IS gBASIC: tests/gsql/key_values.bas serializes 160 values and writes its OWN answer for ~14,800 pairs it will order (absence placement decided by its own `sort`; different `type()` never asked; two currencies written as refused), and tests/gsql/key_check.c requires memcmp to agree ascending, DESCENDING, as typed money and over 200,000 composite (ASC, DESC) keys, with every truncation of every value refused and the unindexable kinds refused rather than keyed. NaN is held to the stated position (after +inf, equal to itself, below absence). BUILDING IT CORRECTED THE DESIGN: a date against a datetime IS a total order (time-only, date, time, then PRECISION, which equality includes), so one tag covers all three and "a column holds one temporal kind" stopped being an index limit; durations get NO key (they order by total seconds and are equal field by field, which no single byte order can match). A PINNED-KEY TIER EXISTS BECAUSE ORDER WAS NOT ENOUGH: shifting every subnormal's exponent by one kept all 229,634 comparisons and changed the bytes, and keys are on disk -- 13 keys worked out BY HAND from the spec, which also matched the encoder independently. AND A HANG IS NOT A FAILURE: a perturbation sending -0 down the magnitude path looped forever on the top-bit search, so the checker is `timeout`-bounded and valgrind runs only over an encoder that passed (it cannot wrap `vg_run`, a shell function). Ten perturbations proven red. Skips without a C compiler, saying the key encoding was NOT measured
./tests/run_money.sh        # PLAT-MONEY phase 0: exact construction (docs/money_design.md). money was ALWAYS an exact int64 of cents -- 0.01 accumulated 1000 times is exactly 10.00 -- and NOTHING COULD PUT AN EXACT VALUE IN: `{USD}` took a number, already a double when the modifier saw it, and refused text, so 92233720368547.75 became ...76 and the type's own int64 range was unreachable through its own constructor. `{USD}` is now REFLECTIVE: text is parsed directly, a number is first rendered to its SHORTEST round-trip decimal (PLAT-NUMFMT) and then parsed, so both routes end in one exact integer parse and an ordinary literal is exact without touching the parser. THE EXCESS-PRECISION RULE SPLITS BY ORIGIN and that split is what keeps the type usable: authored text ("1.23456789") is REFUSED because the author wrote what money cannot hold, while a computed value ROUNDS because `price * 1.08` carries seventeen digits as a matter of course. BOTH ROUTES STOP AT THE STORAGE SCALE, which is a correction made 2026-08-29 after gdash reported it: phase 2 capped AUTHORED text at the MINOR UNIT instead, on the argument that guard digits are internal headroom rather than precision an author may claim -- wrong three ways, and the acceptance tier now pins each. Sub-cent authored prices are ORDINARY rather than exotic (fuel is posted at $3.459 a gallon, electricity quoted at $0.10432 a kWh), so the narrow rule refused values the type holds EXACTLY while reporting that the currency did not allow them, which was false; it refused "0.030000", which claims no extra precision at all, being 0.03 with trailing zeros; and it defeated the requirement that MOTIVATED guard digits, representing three cents as 3.0000. RETENTION IS THE TIER DISPLAY CANNOT SHOW -- 0.10432 displays as 0.10 either way, so only `t * 1000` = 104.32 distinguishes a value stored exactly from one rounded at the door, and a fixture asserting the displayed form would have passed on both binaries. The refusal message now NAMES the currency's own limit, because "how many places DOES it store" is the author's next question and the answer varies (6 USD, 7 KWD, 4 JPY). FIXING THE ENTRANCE EXPOSED THE EXIT: `string(m)` renders at the MINOR UNIT and `number(m)` refuses money outright, so an exact sub-cent value could be put in and NEVER GOT OUT -- the identical too-narrow-doorway defect one level along, and money_render had carried an unused lossless path plus a comment claiming it was "what a round-trip through text needs" since phase 2. `money.text(amount [, places])` is that exit, defaulting to the storage scale so the text reads back through `{USD}=` as the same value. Its fixture's load-bearing tier is the CONTROL, not the round trip: every other check is also satisfied by a money.text that merely called string(), and the round trips pass too for any value landing on whole cents -- so the tier that separates them asserts the two must DIFFER on 0.10432. DISSOLVED A FOURTH DEFECT NOBODY HAD FILED: rounding at .5 was undefined -- 0.125 gave 0.13 while 0.145 gave 0.14, which LOOKS like banker's rounding and was not, since round_to_cents was half-away-from-zero applied to a DOUBLE and 0.145 as a double is 0.14499999999999999001; parsing decimal text leaves no binary representation to be ambiguous about, so ties are half-even every time. FIXTURES ARE SELF-CHECKING not golden, because every one of these defects produced a PLAUSIBLE NUMBER and a golden would have recorded the damaged value as expected -- which is how they survived from the type's introduction until gdash became its first consumer. The refusal fixture CARRIES ITS OWN CONTROL (each refusal beside its nearest legal neighbour: authored "1.234" refused, computed 1.234 accepted), since a refusal tier alone is satisfied by a modifier that rejects everything. Two bugs the fixtures caught and reading had not, both from int64's ASYMMETRIC range (min is one greater in magnitude than max): the parser accumulated signed and negated, refusing -92233720368547758.08; and format_money printed it as `--92233720368547758.-8` because negating LLONG_MIN overflows -- pre-existing, and unreachable until exact construction made the value constructible. Red-proofed on the NUMBER path, where the pre-change binary answers WRONGLY rather than crashing (...76 vs ...75, 0.13 vs 0.12). Headless, never skips (bar valgrind). PHASE 2 added currency identity: `{USD}` was one hardcoded strcmp and cents were hardcoded everywhere else, so JPY (no minor unit) and KWD (three) had NO correct representation; it is a table lookup now and all 178 ISO 4217 currencies arrived at once, from a COMMITTED src/currency_table.h generated by tools/make_currency_table.py -- committed because a currency appearing or vanishing with the build machine would be a correctness problem, not a packaging one. Storage carries FOUR GUARD DIGITS below the minor unit so intermediates survive and round once at display ((100/3)*3 is 100.00 where cents gave 99.99 -- which CLOSED a gap phase 1 had pinned as open, and the phase-1 fixture was updated). THE COST IS RANGE AND IT IS REAL: USD's ceiling fell 10,000x from ~$92 quadrillion to ~$9.22 trillion, which BROKE the phase 0 and 1 fixtures and is the most concrete demonstration of the trade available. Currency mismatch splits by operation, following PLAT-EQ: + and ordering RAISE, equality ANSWERS false. The built-in table is the CURRENT ISO list so ITL/DEM/FRF are absent -- found by writing a test that assumed otherwise -- which is the clearest justification for money.register/retire/currencies; retire marks HISTORICAL (new values refused, old ones still read) rather than removing, since removing does not unmake values that exist. SER_VERSION 2 carries units, currency AND exponent so a value is self-describing (actors are fork+exec, so a parent's registration is not the child's); the v1 tier reads a payload THE PHASE-1 BINARY ACTUALLY WROTE, committed as hex because -- a correction to the design doc, recorded in DOGFOOD -- gBASIC has NO binary-safe file read at all: read() truncates at NUL and bytes() returns a file's SIZE (RESOLVED same day -- one word, value_string -> value_string_n). PHASE 3 added dated FX: money.rate/convert/rate_on, where the rate EFFECTIVE on a date is the latest whose as-of is on or before it, so a report run for March sees March's rate rather than today's -- converting without a date gives a number nobody can reproduce, which is an AUDIT problem not an arithmetic one. The rate is decimal TEXT (eight significant figures is ordinary for FX and a double would round them) and conversion crosses storage scales in ONE integer operation. INVERSION IS REFUSED because the two sides of a quote differ by a spread and inverting would invent money -- the message names the rate that DOES exist so the author decides. Same-currency conversion is identity needing no rate, which is the CONTROL tier: without it a refusal suite is satisfied by refusing everything, and generic code converting a mixed list to one reporting currency would fail on the entries already in it. Expected values computed in exact decimal arithmetic OUTSIDE gBASIC. PHASE 4 added money.allocate and stdlib/finance.bas. ALLOCATION IS A DIFFERENT PROBLEM FROM DIVISION and guard digits only solved the latter: (100/3)*3 comes back whole because a third of a dollar has somewhere to live, but three PAYMENTS cannot each be 33.3333 -- an invoice or payroll line is whole minor units -- so allocate works at the MINOR UNIT and distributes the remainder one unit at a time, giving [33.34,33.33,33.33] that sums back EXACTLY; never three of 33.33 (loses a cent) nor three of 33.34 (invents one), both of which look perfectly reasonable. finance.bas is the TIME VALUE OF MONEY (pmt/pv/fv/nper/npv/irr/schedule/sln/syd/ddb), the half stats.bas never had -- it covers securities analytics, this covers what a loan payment is. Rates are PER PERIOD (0.06/12 is the caller's arithmetic) because compounding conventions vary by product and jurisdiction and a guess would be silently wrong somewhere. The schedule ADJUSTS ITS FINAL PAYMENT so the balance lands exactly on zero, as lenders do, asserted ARITHMETICALLY (principal parts sum to the loan) rather than as a golden. Expected values are EXTERNAL -- pmt 250k at 0.5%/month over 360 is -1498.88 in Excel and LibreOffice. Two bugs the fixtures caught: irr OVERFLOWED MONEY because bisection visits rates near -100% where the discount factor is 1e-12 (the rate search now works in plain numbers, which is right anyway -- a rate is a ratio), and a test of mine assumed a single cash flow cannot break even when it can at -59.8%, now a CONTROL beside the genuine refusal. NOTE FINANCE PHASE 1 (2026-08-29/30) MOVED THE ARGUMENT ORDER: pmt/pv/fv/nper/schedule take EXCEL'S order now (rate, periods, amount), `fv` changed SHAPE AND SIGN to Excel's annuity form, `irr` takes one array with the outlay first and negative, and `rate`/`xnpv`/`xirr`/`year_fraction`/the rate conversions are new -- see docs/finance_design.md and run_finance_cookbook.sh. The -1498.88 figure above still holds; the call that produces it does not
./tests/run_estate.sh      # `estate` -- a fabricated business estate whose TRUTH IS WRITTEN DOWN (stdlib/estate.bas, docs/estate_design.md), and `discovery` traced across it. WHY: "did the tool find the right relationship" has no answer unless the right answer is KNOWN, and "DID IT INVENT ONE WHERE THERE IS NONE" has no answer at all without a region whose truth is NOTHING -- a real customer database supplies neither. R1 IS LOAD-BEARING: ONE DECLARATION PRODUCES BOTH THE DATABASE AND THE TRUTH, since a hand-written answer key drifts the first time either side changes and A FIXTURE WHOSE ANSWER KEY IS WRONG TEACHES THE TOOL TO BE WRONG AND THEN CERTIFIES IT. THE OFFLINE TIER NEEDS NO DATABASE, which is what keeps this gate from going quiet: spec/ddl/truth are pure and discovery.references is a pure function of a string, so the estate DECLARES what each module touches and the scanner DERIVES it from the SQL WITHOUT SEEING THE DECLARATION -- agreement between two independently written statements is evidence where a golden of either alone is a transcript. VOCABULARY IS REAL, from the Enron corpus (counterparty, enron_entity, gross_vol_mmbtu, pvr_pct, ctp) because an author's naming imagination is a stated blind spot. THE LIVE TIER FOUND FOUR DEFECTS IN `discovery` WITHIN MINUTES, none visible hermetically: a view reading ANOTHER VIEW could not be resolved at all (only base tables were resolution targets, so views-on-views simply did not work); keeping views then swept in every sys.*/INFORMATION_SCHEMA.* object, 23 -> 656 of which 17 were the estate; FreeTDS REFUSES odbc.columns with no table pattern; and psqlODBC with no SCHEMA pattern SILENTLY RETURNS ONLY THE SEARCH_PATH, so an estate in trading/finance/warehouse came back as 17 `public` tables WITH NO ERROR -- the worst of the four, because a complete-looking answer missing most of the database is exactly the failure this library exists not to produce. Traced four hops across three schemas with fan-out at the end. A FALSE POSITIVE IN THE FIXTURE'S OWN FIRST DRAFT worth remembering: the unenforced-relationship check matched `references trading.counterparty(id)` ANYWHERE in the DDL, but the ENFORCED contract.counterparty_id emits that same text -- it checks the SOURCE side inside that table's own create statement now. COLUMN LINEAGE IS DECLARED, NOT DERIVED (2026-09-09): the module bodies are the SQL and `spec().column_lineage` is what that SQL MEANS, written by hand when the estate was designed, so `discovery.lineage` deriving the same answer from the bodies alone is evidence -- DERIVING THE ANSWER KEY FROM THE BODIES WITH THE SAME PARSER WOULD MAKE IT A COPY OF THE ANSWER and the comparison would pass on any parser that is consistently wrong. Same rule for a view's OUTPUT COLUMNS, declared beside its body rather than read out of it. `lineage_origins` names where the trail genuinely ENDS, which is what makes "the walk stopped here" a checkable claim rather than a description of wherever it happened to stop -- with the CONTROL that a base-table column nothing writes is an ORIGIN WITH NO STEPS, without which "the trail ends at trading.deal" is equally satisfied by a walker that stops everywhere. Added with it: `p_restate_fact`, a SECOND WRITER of a column another module already fills, READING THE VERY TABLE IT WRITES -- both ordinary in a real warehouse, both fatal to a walk that assumes one writer and no cycles. `estate.catalog(spec, source)` is what `discovery.scan` would read back, built from the declaration instead: NOT A SUBSTITUTE FOR THE LIVE TIER (it cannot see what a driver does to a name, which is the class of defect the live tier exists to catch) but it lets the lineage checks -- which are about SQL, not drivers -- run with NO DATABASE, so the part of this gate that can go quiet stays small. Live tier skips without GBASIC_ESTATE_CONNECTION; SQLite and MariaDB are REFUSED (no procedures / no schemas). THE DIALECT MAP CONVERTS TOWARDS T-SQL, NOT AWAY FROM IT (2026-09-11, reported by estateforge): it used to rewrite `+` to `||` for PostgreSQL, and `+` IS BOTH CONCATENATION AND ARITHMETIC, so `f.deal_id * 2 + 1` became `f.deal_id * 2 || 1` which PostgreSQL refuses -- guarding on the spaces does not help, since the arithmetic has spaces too. The source is ANSI now and the map goes the other way, because `||` is NEVER arithmetic and so says exactly one thing. NO BODY HERE HAD ARITHMETIC, so the defect was LATENT and every test passed; `p_post_gl.entry_id` carries `f.deal_id + 100000` on purpose now, so the live PostgreSQL tier refuses the estate outright if the map is turned back. And the SQL Server PROCEDURE path emitted the RAW BODY, skipping the map entirely -- the mirror of the same defect in the one branch with no conversion at all. Also carries the multi-schema COLUMN COMPLETENESS oracle, which run_discovery's own fixture cannot run: that one builds everything in ONE schema and so passes on a per-schema fetch that keeps only the FIRST, where this estate spans four (with a control asserting it really does). Live-verified on BOTH 2026-09-09: PostgreSQL 17 (10 checks) and SQL Server 2025 (11 -- one more, because the dynamic-SQL module is declared T-SQL only and its GAP is asserted where it exists)
./tests/run_discovery.sh   # `discovery` -- what a database ESTATE says about itself (stdlib/discovery.bas, docs/discovery_design.md). FIRST INCREMENT: DECLARED FACTS ONLY, and that is the DESIGN rather than a stopping point -- an inferred relationship is the result of a SEARCH, and a search over a 500-table estate is ~50 MILLION CANDIDATE PAIRS where coincidences are a CERTAINTY not a risk (every `status` overlaps every other `status`, every surrogate key is 1..N). Recipe 1's finding one domain over; inference arrives with a null model or not at all, and a TRIPWIRE TIER fails if it arrives without one -- reading the source, because the fixture cannot see it: a tool that ALSO reports the real edge passes every behavioural check. SELF-CHECKING NOT GOLDEN, forced TWICE: every defect is a PLAUSIBLE CATALOG (an id missing its qualifier still looks like an id) AND the expected values DIFFER BY DATABASE (`sales.disc_orders` on SQLite, `sales.gbasic_test.public.disc_orders` on Postgres), so a golden could only ever pin one of the four. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS IDENTITY, asserted PORTABLY because the qualifier is not in the same place on any two databases -- MariaDB puts it in TABLE_CAT and leaves TABLE_SCHEM empty, Postgres/SQL Server populate both, SQLite neither -- so `schema.table` yields `.disc_orders` on MariaDB SILENTLY, in a library whose whole job is identifying columns. What must hold everywhere: the id STARTS with the source, ENDS with the object, DIFFERS between two tables, and CONTAINS NO EMPTY SEGMENT (`sales..disc_orders` is exactly what a naive join produces where a qualifier is absent). RUNS AGAINST ALL FOUR via GBASIC_ODBC_CONNECTION, 26/26 on each 2026-09-08, which is the only reason those rules are claims rather than guesses. A WEAK ASSERTION CAUGHT BY PERTURBATION and worth remembering: the ordinal-order tier DID NOT BITE -- removing the sort left every live check passing, because the driver returns ordinal order and keys() returns insertion order so the two agree BY ACCIDENT; it asserts the CONTRACT now, against a hand-built catalog whose columns are inserted BACKWARDS. Nullability is recorded from the NUMERIC NULLABLE and explicitly NOT TRUSTED (IS_NULLABLE is empty on Postgres, SQLite calls a primary key nullable). Named `scan` not `read` because `read` is a built-in and a name needing explanation at each call site is the wrong name. PLUS `projection`/`predicates`/`explain`, THE INCREMENT THAT ANSWERS THE QUESTION THE LIBRARY EXISTS FOR: two reports both show a number called `total_volume`, they differ, and today that costs a day of manual research -- BOTH ARE CORRECT and what is wanted is WHERE THE DERIVATIONS PARTED. THE PREDICATE IS PART OF THE LINEAGE, not metadata about it: a `where` clause IS the reason two numbers differ, so where/having/join-on are all captured. THE ORACLE is that the estate records the same answer IN PROSE, written when it was designed, while `explain` derives its answer FROM THE SQL ALONE having never seen that prose -- agreement between two independently written statements is evidence where a golden of either is a transcript, and BOTH fixtures assert it. THREE DECISIONS: string literals KEEP THEIR CONTENTS (never read as object names, the token kind prevents that, but in a predicate the literal IS the explanation -- `status = 'ACTIVE'` answers the question and `status = '...'` does not); `select *` is REPORTED NOT EXPANDED, since expanding needs a shape the function was not given; and it reports DIFFERENCES ONLY with the CONTROL that two identical derivations report NONE, because a tool that always finds a reason is indistinguishable from one that guesses. A FIXTURE DEFECT WORTH REMEMBERING: it dropped its views at the END, so a second run scanned the ones the first left behind -- once VIEWS became part of the catalog that turned "both tables are found" into 4. A fixture passing only on a CLEAN database is flaky, and it fails in the direction that looks like a defect in the library. Six perturbations proven red; one made the fixture CRASH ON AN INDEX rather than report its mismatch, hiding the assertion that had already named the problem, and is guarded now. PLUS `statements`/`derivations`/`lineage` (2026-09-09), WHICH ANSWER "WHERE DID THIS COLUMN COME FROM" ACROSS HOPS. A VIEW IS ONE SELECT AND A PROCEDURE IS NOT, which is where the ETL lives: `truncate table x; insert into x select ... from y` writes x twice for two reasons, and a reader that found "the first select" described the second and dropped the first. SPLITTING IS NOT SOLVED AND THE DOC SAYS SO -- T-SQL makes the semicolon OPTIONAL, so the rule is about VERBS at depth zero with FOUR EXCEPTIONS, EACH OF WHICH WAS A WRONG ANSWER BEFORE IT WAS AN EXCEPTION: `then insert` (a merge arm), a MERGE owning its arms (missing it produced a statement whose TARGET WAS `set` -- a phantom table with the confidence of a real one), the select that FEEDS a write, and a CTE (split off, `with c as (...) insert into t select x from c` leaves an insert reading a table called `c`). PAIRING IS THE WHOLE DIFFICULTY for an INSERT, since the names are on one side and the expressions on the other: it is POSITIONAL, and the two shapes that CANNOT be paired -- a count mismatch, and no column list at all -- are REPORTED rather than guessed, because both yield a perfectly ordinary set of expressions WITH THE WRONG NAMES ATTACHED and nothing downstream can detect that. THE OBJECT LEVEL IS ONE LEVEL TOO COARSE and that is the argument for the whole increment: `fact_volume` reads `stg_deal` says NOTHING about whether `avail_after_pvr` came from `gross_vol_mmbtu`, from `pvr_pct`, from both, or FROM A CONSTANT -- and a constant is one of the commonest reasons a number is wrong and nobody can see why, so a column with a writer and NO SOURCES is a step with an empty source list, which is a DIFFERENT FACT from having no writer. THREE REFUSALS: it will not guess (a source that could belong to two of the objects a statement reads is `unresolved` BY NAME, since the wrong attribution is indistinguishable from the right one downstream), it does not assume ONE WRITER PER COLUMN (a restatement is ordinary and reporting only the first hides it), and it does not assume the graph is ACYCLIC (`update t set x = y * 0.97` reads the very table it writes). A VIEW IS THE WRITER OF ITSELF -- a view has no `insert` and its select list is the derivation of every column a consumer sees, which is the only reason a report's number can be traced PAST the view it was read from. TWO PRE-EXISTING DEFECTS FOUND BY BUILDING IT, both invisible until something tried to RESOLVE what had been reported: a NUMERIC LITERAL was read as a column name (digits are legal inside an identifier, so `0.97` arrived as `0` `.` `97` and looked exactly like a qualified column -- `_sources_in` had been reporting `1` as a source of `x * (1 - y)` since it was written, harmless until it became an entry in the UNRESOLVED list, the one field a caller has to trust), and `cast(x as int)` TRUNCATED THE EXPRESSION and named the output column `int`, because only a depth-zero `as` is an alias. THE SECOND ONE CARRIES THE LESSON: with an alias present the LAST `as` wins and the answer comes out right BY ACCIDENT, so the first test written for it PASSED ON THE BROKEN READER and the fixture now uses the unaliased form an insert-select produces by construction. An UPDATE and a MERGE now report their target as a READ as well as a write (`set x = y * 0.97 where status = 'A'` takes both from the target itself, and reported as a write alone a restatement looks like a module with no inputs), with the CONTROL that a DELETE is a write and ONLY a write. THE PURE-SQL TIER RUNS BEFORE THE DRIVER GATE, in its own fixture: the parser used to be exercised only from inside the catalog fixture, so on a machine with no ODBC driver the whole of it was skipped along with a connection it did not need -- a gate you can turn off by not installing something is a gate that shrinks. A TARGET THAT BINDS TO NOTHING IS STILL A HOP: `select ... into #tmp` then `insert ... select ... from #tmp` is how a great deal of real ETL is written and a temp table is NEVER in a catalog, so without this the chain dead-ends at the first one -- which in T-SQL is most procedures. NOTHING IS GUESSED (the statement that FILLS it says which columns it has, and the resolution is confined to the module that wrote it), the id carries `::` which no catalog id ever does, and `intermediates` NAMES every local object a walk passed through -- with the CONTROL that a name which DOES bind is not treated as local, or every hop would become an intermediate and the distinction would say nothing. Nine perturbations proven red, and the over-correction (every target treated as local) is caught by the estate tier as well, which is honest overlap. THE VALGRIND TIER SHARES tests/odbc.supp with run_odbc: pointed at MariaDB it would otherwise redden on uninitialised reads inside libmaodbc's own SQLTables/SQLColumns/SQLPrimaryKeys/SQLForeignKeys, driver-internal and reproduced from plain C before a line of the file was written. TWO COSTS MEASURED BY estateforge AND FIXED 2026-09-11. `modules` TOOK NO SCHEMA FILTER where `scan` takes three, so it returned EVERY ROUTINE IN THE DATABASE -- measured on a fresh PostgreSQL 17 holding an 11-table estate, 119 rows of which 114 were `vector_*`, because pgvector lives in that machine's `template1` and every new database inherits it into `public`. That is NOT a system-schema failure (an extension legitimately lives there); it just means every caller re-filters and one comparing counts is green or red for reasons unrelated to what it tests. The pattern is BOUND not pasted, one pattern not a list, and SQLite REFUSES a filter rather than ignoring it. AND A SCAN FETCHED EVERY SYSTEM COLUMN TO DISCARD IT: on SQL Server `%` returns 10,006 columns of which 9,927 are sys/INFORMATION_SCHEMA, and POSTGRESQL PAYS IT TOO (2,278 of 2,679 here) -- which CORRECTS the report that raised it, since psqlODBC filters TABLES server-side and NOT columns. Columns are fetched ONE SCHEMA AT A TIME from the list the TABLE scan just produced, so nothing that scan found can be missed by construction, and THE TABLE SCAN'S `%` IS UNTOUCHED because it is the fix for psqlODBC's search-path defect and narrowing THAT reintroduces a complete-looking answer missing most of the database -- worse than being slow. Same answer measured identical before and after on both databases; the oracle is the single-`%` form (every column of a catalogued table must be in the catalog and nothing else may be). TIMES ARE NOT QUOTED: estateforge's absolutes are deployment-confounded (unix socket against a VM over TCP, a 14x baseline penalty on `select 1`), so the ROW COUNTS transfer and the times do not. Hermetic by default, skips only the CATALOG tier without an ODBC driver -- but THE WHOLE SUITE SKIPS WITHOUT ODBC IN THE BUILD, which is a SHRINK THIS SUITE'S OWN COMMENT ARGUES AGAINST and is therefore NAMED rather than taken quietly (2026-10-09). `stdlib/discovery.bas` opens with `load odbc`, and `load` is a DECLARATION -- pre-registered, so it cannot be conditional -- so a build made without unixODBC cannot load the library AT ALL and the three tiers that need no database (the SQL reader's ~60 checks, the supplied notes' ~24, the no-inference tripwire) die at line 13 of it along with everything else. MEASURED by suppressing odbc detection locally: 3 of 4 tiers failed and the valgrind tier reported a missing file, over a correct build -- and the same cause reddened run_estate (whose OFFLINE tier is the half that keeps THAT gate from going quiet) and two tiers of run_nlq. CI's "all optional modules" job had never installed unixODBC, so this was its state on every push; it does now, and asserts every module is really in rather than merely printing what it found. REMOVING THE SHRINK NEEDS `load` TO STOP RAISING FOR A MODULE THAT IS COMPILED OUT, which is a LANGUAGE-VISIBLE decision and not a suite's to take, so the skip says in as many words what did not run. run_nlq is gated PER TIER rather than per suite for the same reason read the other way: eleven of its thirteen tiers read a COMMITTED JSON catalog and need nothing, so skipping the suite would throw ~90 checks away to avoid a dependency two of them have
./tests/run_odbc.sh         # the `odbc` module -- one connection string, every database unixODBC can reach. Hermetic by default (the SQLite3 ODBC driver over a scratch file: a REAL driver-manager round trip, not a stub), and the SAME fixtures run against SQL Server or MySQL by setting GBASIC_ODBC_CONNECTION. SELF-CHECKING rather than golden, because the failure this module exists to prevent is not a crash but an ordinary-looking number wrong in its last digits -- a DECIMAL(19,4) narrowed to a double, or a BIGINT past 2^53 off by one -- and a golden would record the damaged value AS expected and defend it. The EXACTNESS tier inserts 2^53+1, reads it back as a string, and asserts BOTH that the digits survived AND that the same value through a double would have changed (9007199254740993 -> ...992); the second half is what makes the first more than a tautology, and BIGINT/DECIMAL/NUMERIC answering as STRINGS is the design decision it guards, the same one `pg` already made for oids 20 and 1700. THE INJECTION TIER PROVES ITSELF NON-VACUOUS: the obvious payload (`'); drop table x;--` bound, witness table still standing) passes on a module that pastes every parameter, because the SQLite3 driver refuses multiple statements anyway -- measured, not assumed -- so the load-bearing payload is a TAUTOLOGY that subverts a SINGLE statement, run BOTH ways in the fixture: pasted it matches every row, bound it matches only the row whose value really is that text. Refusals split by what they need: 7 pinned-message negatives that need no connection (they live here rather than run_negative.sh, since `load odbc` itself raises in a build without unixODBC and would redden the whole negative suite), and 12 live-connection refusals asserted IN LANGUAGE through PLAT-ERR's `on error goto next` -- parameter-count mismatch either way, a non-array parameter list, exec over a row-returning statement, an unrepresentable parameter, duplicate result columns, bad SQL carrying the driver's SQLSTATE, use-after-close, double close. Plus CATALOG (odbc.drivers must list the driver the connections use, or it is answering from somewhere other than the driver manager) and VALGRIND, which asserts NO INVALID ACCESS rather than no leak, since the driver manager dlopens libraries that leak by design -- and both real defects found building this were memory errors that produced no wrong value (a cleanup walk over an array a refusal path never allocated). Skips cleanly without ODBC in the build or a driver installed. VERIFIED AGAINST SQL SERVER 2025 AND MARIADB 11.8 on 2026-08-29, and that run is the argument for the whole parameterised design: it found THREE defects invisible to SQLite plus one introduced fixing them. (1) booleans bound as the CHARACTER "1" -- a real BIT column refuses it, MariaDB said "Data too long for column"; (2) booleans READ as text and compared to '1', where MariaDB returns byte 0x01, so A COLUMN HOLDING true READ BACK AS false with no error at all; (3) parameters declared SQL_VARCHAR, so FreeTDS routed them through a single-byte charset and non-Latin-1 text VANISHED into SQL Server -- `é` and an em-dash survived while `日本語` and `☃` arrived EMPTY, which is the CP1252 repertoire exactly; (4) MINE, from fixing 3: SQL_WVARCHAR maps to nvarchar whose non-max limit is 4000 CHARACTERS, so a 5000-char value came back as 4000, now SQL_WLONGVARCHAR above that threshold. THE CHARSET TIER IS THE INTERESTING ONE because it has NO ERROR TO ATTACH A HINT TO: even with SQL_WVARCHAR, FreeTDS needs ClientCharset=UTF-8 or it stores UTF-8 bytes one per character (server LEN() says 13 where the text is 5) -- and it ROUND-TRIPS through gBASIC, since our reader reverses the same mangling, so a write-then-read test passes while the database holds mojibake. odbc.connect therefore asks the driver its own name via SQL_DRIVER_NAME and WARNS at connect time, with a control tier asserting the warning goes away once the option is supplied, or it is noise authors learn to ignore. Portability facts now pinned in the fixtures: SQL Server's `timestamp` is a ROWVERSION not a datetime (`datetime` is the portable spelling); bad SQL is rejected at PREPARE by SQLite/MariaDB and at EXECUTE by SQL Server; SQLSTATE for an unknown column is 42S22 on MariaDB/SQL Server but HY000 on SQLite's driver, so the test asserts the driver's text NAMES THE COLUMN rather than pinning a code. tests/odbc.supp carries DRIVER-INTERNAL valgrind defects only, each ISOLATED FIRST by reproducing it from plain C with no gBASIC in the stack -- narrow entries naming an object and entry point, since a blanket "anything inside a driver" rule would also hide uninitialised data we passed in. THE ISOLATION IS NOW A COMMITTED PROGRAM (tests/odbc_driver_probe.c) AND A TIER, added 2026-09-09 when `discovery` made the four CATALOG calls the first time and libmaodbc read an uninitialised value on every one of them. Before this the isolation was done BY HAND AND THROWN AWAY, so the claim was true when made, unverifiable afterwards and unrepeatable on the next driver version. A suppression file rots TWO WAYS and the ordinary tier sees neither: it can stop MATCHING (a driver upgrade moves the frames and every suite sharing the file goes red for something that is not a gBASIC defect) or start matching TOO MUCH. THE LOAD-BEARING HALF IS THE SECOND, and it is a CONTROL: `--dirty` hands SQLTables an UNWRITTEN STACK BUFFER -- a caller's defect, deliberately -- and it must STILL BE REPORTED with the suppressions in force. MEASURED and better than expected: a caller's uninitialised data surfaces at `strlen (vg_replace_strmem.c)`, valgrind's own interceptor OUTSIDE the driver, so an `at: obj:*/odbc/libmaodbc.so` anchor cannot match it; a blanket entry is proven red against exactly that. The "still matches" half SAYS WHEN IT HAS NOTHING TO MATCH rather than passing vacuously, since a clean driver makes it assert nothing. FOUR PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED including one that is not about suppressions at all: a probe that CANNOT RUN must say so rather than be read as "the suppressions no longer match", the reports-the-wrong-cause class this module has now produced three times. THREE DEFECTS IN THE PROBE ITSELF, each measured not reasoned: `drop table if exists` is REFUSED by FreeTDS (so the tables survived and `create` said the object already existed); a FAILED statement POISONS a FreeTDS connection, so speculative cleanup runs on a connection of its own; and SQL_NO_DATA IS SUCCESS for DDL, which FreeTDS returns -- treating it as failure is what produced "create table failed with no diagnostic", no diagnostic because there was no error. Verified on all four drivers, twice each (the probe is idempotent). AND IT EXPOSED A BLIND SPOT IN run_valgrind_policy's OWN TRIPWIRE: its regex required `valgrind` to follow only `if` and env assignments FROM COLUMN ONE, so the first draft of this tier -- `vgp() { valgrind ... ; }` -- went straight past it. It anchors at any COMMAND POSITION now and requires an argument-looking token after, since `printf 'SKIP (valgrind unavailable)'` appears in a dozen suites and a tripwire that fires on prose is one somebody turns off. PLUS THE SCHEMA CATALOG (odbc.tables/columns/primary_keys/foreign_keys over SQLTables/SQLColumns/SQLPrimaryKeys/SQLForeignKeys, 23 checks) -- the first groundwork for the discovery library. READ THROUGH THE DRIVER MANAGER, NOT `information_schema`, and that choice is the whole point: information_schema is a DIALECT MATRIX (Postgres, SQL Server and MySQL each spell parts of it differently, SQLite has none of it) while ODBC normalises these four calls across every database with a driver -- and ONE ORGANISATION OFTEN RUNS SEVERAL DATABASES AT ONCE, so a catalog read working against only one cannot describe the estate. They reuse odbc_rows_from_statement, so every type and exactness rule governing a query result governs these too. COLUMN NAMES ARE THE DRIVER'S, NOT OURS (ODBC specifies them -- note TABLE_SCHEM without the A), so the raw names ARE the portable interface and renaming would add a mapping that can drift while discarding a driver's extra columns; friendly names belong to the library above, the ldap/smtp split. AN ABSENT OPTION MEANS ANY, NOT EMPTY -- ODBC distinguishes a NULL pattern from "" and conflating them returns NOTHING on a database that qualifies its objects. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS THE FILTER, asserted as a DIFFERENCE: with `table` ignored every column in the database comes back and cat_orders' columns ARE ALL STILL THERE, a result that looks exactly like a catalog -- only asserting a DIFFERENT table gives DIFFERENT columns catches it (proven red). foreign_keys answers TWO DIFFERENT QUESTIONS and refuses to guess: `table` gives the keys DEFINED ON it, `referenced_table` the keys POINTING AT it. A DEFECT THE FIXTURE CAUGHT IN THE FIRST DRAFT and it is the reports-the-wrong-cause class: odbc_connection_from_value ALREADY raises "connection is closed" and the new code raised "expects an odbc connection" over the top of it -- false, about a value that IS one. NOW VERIFIED FOUR WAYS (2026-09-08): SQLite 42, MariaDB 11.8 41, PostgreSQL 17 42, SQL Server 43 checks, 0 failed, and the catalog fixture 23/23 on ALL FOUR. THAT RUN FOUND A REAL DEFECT AND A BLOCKER. (1) psqlODBC reports a `boolean` column as SQL_VARCHAR not SQL_BIT, so it arrives as the STRING "1"/"0" -- and that is A WRONG ANSWER IN THE PERMISSIVE DIRECTION rather than a type surprise, because "0" is a NON-EMPTY STRING and gBASIC makes any non-empty string TRUE, so `if row.is_active` is taken for a row the database says is INACTIVE with nothing raised. MariaDB and SQL Server report `bit` as SQL_BIT and are unaffected, which is exactly why a suite that had never met PostgreSQL could not see it. Warned at connect time, the treatment FreeTDS's ClientCharset already gets and for the same reason (the failure is silent so there is no error to attach a hint to); a WARNING not a refusal and NOT a TYPE_NAME="bool" special case in the reader, since the driver said varchar and we returned a string faithfully -- the fix belongs in the connection string the operator controls. Asserted as a DIFFERENCE with two controls: silent once BoolsAsChar=0 is set, and silent on the three drivers that get it right. (2) THE SUITE COULD NOT RUN AGAINST POSTGRES AT ALL -- odbc_test declared `datetime` and `bit`, the portable spelling for SQL Server/MariaDB/SQLite (on SQL Server `timestamp` means ROWVERSION), and Postgres has no `datetime` while its `bit` is a BIT STRING; the runner supplies both spellings now and the defaults leave every existing invocation unchanged. MEASURED FACTS A MULTI-SOURCE TOOL MUST CARRY, now in the reference: the qualifier is in TABLE_CAT on MariaDB, TABLE_SCHEM on Postgres/SQL Server and NEITHER on SQLite, so `schema.table` yields `nothing.orders` on MariaDB; the same varchar(20) is DATA_TYPE 12 on three drivers and -9 on MariaDB, and BOTH Postgres drivers agree on 12 so it is not an ANSI/Unicode split; IS_NULLABLE is EMPTY on Postgres; and SQLite reports a PRIMARY KEY as NULLABLE, which is simply wrong. THE STANDING LESSON: SQLite is DYNAMICALLY TYPED, so a suite that runs only against it cannot see a type error at all -- it accepted a boolean as text and a 5000-char value in varchar(200), and has no wide character types to exercise
./tests/run_webclient.sh    # loopback fixture; skips if env unavailable. CARRIES THE BODY-IS-BYTES TIER since 2026-09-25: `webclient` used to RAISE `binary responses are not supported` on any body containing a NUL, so it could not fetch an image, a PDF or a zip -- in a language whose strings have carried arbitrary bytes since they were designed. THE REFUSAL WAS NOT A FACT ABOUT HTTP: `http.read`, over the same libcurl and the same buffer, already handed those bytes back correctly with `value_string_n`, so two readers of ONE transfer disagreed about what a body is -- and the write side truncated as well, measuring the body with `strlen` for POSTFIELDSIZE. THE TWO DIRECTIONS ARE ASSERTED SEPARATELY, because a client that truncates on the way out and a reader that truncates on the way in agree with each other PERFECTLY, which is how this survived: the read is checked against a body the FIXTURE chose (`/nul`) and the write against the length THE SERVER reports (`/length`), and each perturbation reddens only its own assertion. `json` is withheld from a NUL-bearing body deliberately -- the decoder walks a C string and then checks it reached the end, a test any interior NUL satisfies, so a valid prefix followed by arbitrary bytes would have been reported as a whole valid document, which is a WRONG answer where offering nothing is a true one. docs/webclient_design.md's "keep Phase 1 text-only" is struck rather than quietly edited
./tests/run_steward.sh     # STEWARD -- the AI reference application's first slice (examples/steward/steward.bas; AI proposal step 8), and the SKELETON its Part 3 item 13 asked for before more phase documents were written. WHAT IT IS FOR: every layer under it has its own suite and each passes; this is the ONLY thing that exercises them JOINED -- an HTTP request becoming a run, a run becoming a model call in a worker, a reply becoming a tool call, an approval arriving minutes later on a DIFFERENT request, and a conversation ending. BOTH DEFECTS IT FOUND WERE INVISIBLE TO EVERY LAYER'S OWN TESTS, which is the whole argument for building it. (1) `if req.headers["x-user"] = ""` LET AN UNAUTHENTICATED REQUEST THROUGH: a missing record key reads back as `unknown` and `unknown = ""` is FALSE, so the application answered 202 to a request with no identity at all and created a run for nobody, raising nothing -- the shape of a real authentication bypass, and the perturbation reproducing it is the exact line that was written. (2) A TOP-LEVEL `watch` REGISTERS NOTHING when a `program` block exists and says nothing about it, so every pool reply went undelivered and every conversation sat in `calling_model` forever; it cannot be hoisted like `load` because a watcher FIRES on registration and would run its body before anything it reads is assigned -- what is missing is the diagnostic, not the hoisting. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS TWO RUNS SUSPENDED AT ONCE while the server serves a third to completion, which only works because the run is TEXT in a store between steps (encode in, decode out, on the ordinary path rather than in a unit test). The refused-approval path must end with the MODEL SAYING SO rather than with an error, because a person declining one action is an answer. ONE POOL FOR BOTH KINDS OF WORK is what this settled and the design had not: a model call is simply another thing that blocks, so the non-streaming case needs no llm.start/poll/read at all -- `tools.serve` handles only tool calls, so the worker loop lives in the application over the SAME `tools.dispatch`, and whether tools.serve should be extensible is a real question this raises and does not answer. Three perturbations proven red. No valgrind tier (every library beneath has one; what is under test here is the joining). No network: answers from recorded fixtures. Skips without python3
./tests/run_retrieval.sh   # `retrieval` -- permission-filtered nearest-neighbour search over pgvector (stdlib/retrieval.bas, docs/retrieval_design.md; AI proposal step 7, §1.7). THE DEFECT IT EXISTS TO PREVENT is the obvious implementation: rank first, then drop what the caller may not see. IT FAILS IN A WAY THAT LOOKS LIKE AN ORDINARY EMPTY RESULT -- a user with narrow permissions asks a question, the globally nearest chunks all belong to someone else, every one is dropped, and they are told nothing matched; nothing errors, and their own best matches were never considered. Putting the ACL predicate in the WHERE makes the database narrow first and rank what remains. ASSERTED AS A DIFFERENCE against a real pgvector: a corpus where the three nearest chunks are ones the asking user MAY NOT SEE, and they still get their own top-2 -- WITH THE CONTROL that a user who MAY see them gets those three instead, without which "the narrow user got two rows" is equally satisfied by an ACL that does nothing. The fixture also asserts its own PREMISE (that the restricted chunks really are nearest), or the difference could be an accident of the data. `search` CALLS `query_text` rather than rebuilding the SQL, and that is not tidiness: written as two separate string builds, a perturbation that changed only `search` left the structural tier PASSING -- two representations of one query drift, which is the defect a tripwire guards elsewhere in this tree and which one source removes the need for. AN OFFLINE TIER ALWAYS RUNS, because an entirely skippable suite is one that can go quiet; what it establishes is STRUCTURAL (the predicate precedes the ordering, the acl is GIN-indexed), which is weak alone and is why the live tier proves the same thing behaviourally. Live tiers need GBASIC_POSTGRES_TEST=1 and a PGDATABASE with pgvector, the same switch run_postgres uses. `store` is keyed by CONTENT HASH, which is what makes an indexer resumable. Identifiers are validated and REFUSED rather than escaped (the dbframe rule): escaping an identifier is a decision about a quoting dialect, refusing one is a fact. FOUR PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED including the leak -- with the predicate defeated, "a caller with no overlapping group sees nothing at all: got 5, want 0". NOTE a bug this work found in itself: `pg.exec` returns { command, rows_affected } and the first draft wrote `if r > 0`, which PLAT-EQ's ORDERING REFUSAL caught -- before that fix a record compared as 0 and every write would have been counted as skipped, silently
./tests/run_mcp.sh         # `mcp` -- publishing a toolset over the Model Context Protocol (stdlib/mcp.bas, docs/mcp_design.md; AI proposal step 6, §1.11). TWO TRANSPORTS, ONE DISPATCHER: `mcp.handle` turns a JSON-RPC request into a reply and does NO I/O, so the stdio loop and an HTTP `server` block are thin wrappers over it -- which makes the PROTOCOL testable with NEITHER transport present (the run_web_routes lesson one library over) and leaves the transport tiers testing only what a transport can get wrong. THE HTTP TIER ASSERTS BYTE-IDENTICAL REPLIES to the stdio transport for one request, because "one dispatcher" is not implied by both merely working. THE DEADLOCK TIER IS DEMONSTRATED, NOT ASSERTED: a desktop client launches the server as a subprocess and waits on its stdout, and block-buffered the reply sits in the pipe buffer while the server waits for the client's next request -- so the same session is driven TWICE and every request times out without `--line-buffered`. That tier ALSO FAILS IF ANY REQUEST IS ANSWERED, since that would mean the transport was never buffer-bound and the tier had been proving nothing. THE DISTINCTION THE PROTOCOL TIER EXISTS FOR is between a TOOL that failed (a RESULT with isError -- the model asked for something reasonable and can react by choosing another tool) and a PROTOCOL error (the CLIENT is malformed and cannot fix that by retrying); collapsing them either way yields a well-formed reply that means the wrong thing, so BOTH are asserted, and an unknown TOOL is a result while an unknown METHOD is an error. A NOTIFICATION GETS NO REPLY, with the control that the same method WITH an id does answer -- answering a notification is the commonest way a JSON-RPC server confuses a client, because the reply arrives as the answer to whatever it asks next and every exchange after is off by one. THE MAPPED PRINCIPAL IS THE LEAK SURFACE: a read-only tool published over MCP is answered as whatever principal the configuration names, so a `principal` may only be declared with a `max_groups` ceiling and the server REFUSES TO START when exceeded; a ceiling with nothing to bound is refused too, and serving with NO principal is legal and is the NARROWEST state -- the argument `ldap` made for refusing referral chasing. Four perturbations proven red. NOTE a blank line ends a stdio session, because `input()` returns "" for both a blank line and EOF with no way to tell them apart (DOGFOOD); JSON-RPC never sends one. CONSUMING SHIPPED THE SAME DAY (`mcp.connect`/`tools_of`/`call`/`disconnect`, 20 checks over BOTH transports, gBASIC consuming gBASIC): a spec's `expect` list is checked against the server's ADVERTISED TOOLS AT CONNECT TIME rather than at the first call hours later inside whatever the agent happened to be doing, and a malformed spec is refused BEFORE anything is launched, so a load-time mistake and a connect-time disagreement stay distinct diagnostics. A REMOTE RESULT IS THE SAME SHAPE `tools.dispatch` RETURNS, so nothing downstream can tell a remote tool from a local one, and a protocol error from the far end becomes an error RESULT because from the caller's side "that call did not work" is one outcome however the server phrased it. THE LIMIT IS STATED RATHER THAN PAPERED OVER: our own publisher is the server, which needs nothing external and cannot see a protocol disagreement BOTH HALVES SHARE. `via: {mcp:}` in a tool entry is a DECISION not a deferral -- it would make `tools` load `mcp` so every program holding a toolset carried the client whether or not it consumed anything, and it is two lines in the application. Skips the transport tiers without python3/curl
./tests/run_agent.sh       # `agent` -- a conversation in progress is a VALUE and the loop is a PURE STEP (stdlib/agent.bas, docs/agent_design.md; AI proposal step 5, §1.8). THE NAIVE DESIGN IS A LOOP and the APPROVAL GATE makes it unwritable: when the model asks to change the world a person has to say yes, they may take a minute, the wait spans HTTP requests, and the server is single-threaded and watcher-driven -- nothing may block. So `agent.apply(run, event)` performs no I/O and returns the new run plus the ACTIONS its caller must perform. TWO STRUCTURAL FACTS CAME FROM `encode` RATHER THAN FROM THE DESIGN: a TOOLSET cannot be in the run (it holds function values and encode refuses them) so the run keeps `tools.schema` -- which carries `mutates`, exactly what the approval decision needs -- and `expires_at` is a NUMBER because encode refuses a datetime too; both are the rule §1.8 already stated for the live model handle, reached from the same place, that a run which cannot be stored is not a run. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS STORAGE, asserted as a DIFFERENCE between the original run and one that went through encode/decode: "it encodes" alone is satisfied by a run that encodes and then steps differently, so the tier requires the revived run to produce THE SAME ACTIONS, stage and idempotency key. THE GATE IS ALSO A DIFFERENCE -- a mutating tool ASKS and a read-only one does not, since asserting only the first passes on a run that asks permission for everything -- with a CONTROL (a context carrying `approve_mutations: false`) that says the gate is a decision rather than a hard rule. A DECLINED CALL IS AN ANSWER, NOT AN ERROR: it appends a tool_result part marked is_error and naming who declined, so the model is told in the transcript and can propose something else; a refusal that raised would end the conversation, which is not what one person declining one action means. PURITY IS ASSERTED STRUCTURALLY, by reading the function body for a clock, a socket or an id generator, because a BEHAVIOURAL test cannot notice a clock read that happens to return the same value twice -- and a run that read the time would resume differently after a restart, which is exactly what §1.8 needs not to happen; the perturbation proving it is a single added `epoch(now())`, caught by that tier and NO OTHER. Three perturbations proven red. NOTE `apply` not `step`: `step` is a KEYWORD (`for i = 1 to 10 step 2`), the same wall `new` and `stop` put up elsewhere in this stdlib, and `lending.apply`/`credit.apply` already mean folding an event into a state. One thing came FREE: a gBASIC record is a value, so `apply` cannot mutate its caller's run even by accident. Headless, no network, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_llm_transcript.sh # `llm`'s CANONICAL PART-BASED TRANSCRIPT and KEYED REPLAY (docs/llm_design.md §10; AI proposal step 4). A MESSAGE IS NOT {role, content}: after a tool call the assistant turn carries text AND tool-call parts, and providers shape those differently -- Anthropic nests blocks inside a user turn, OpenAI gives a tool result its own `tool` role and puts the arguments in a JSON STRING -- so until now the transcript WAS the provider's shape and a conversation built for one could not be replayed against the other. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS THAT ONE TRANSCRIPT PRODUCES TWO DIFFERENT WIRE SHAPES: asserting each translation is "valid" is satisfied by a translator that emits the same thing twice, which is precisely the bug, and one of the two providers would reject it -- so the fixture asserts the shapes DIFFER as well as what each is. Every part keeps `raw`, the provider block it came from, because an assistant turn must be echoed VERBATIM for tool-call ids to line up. KEYED REPLAY IS A DIFFERENT THING FROM `llm.offline`, NOT A BETTER ONE, and that is ASSERTED AS THE CONTROL rather than claimed: `offline` returns one fixed file for EVERY request, so the same two turns get the same answer, where `replay` gives each its own. BOTH FAILURES THE DESIGN NAMES ARE FIXED AS DIFFERENCES: a volatile system prompt (today's date) still matches its recording, WITH the control that a change OUTSIDE the volatile path does NOT -- or "volatile" would just mean the hash ignores things; and the OCCURRENCE INDEX IS THE RETRY ATTEMPT, so a retried request replays the SECOND recording, which needed no state outside the call because the retry loop already counted attempts (occurrence is attempts WITHIN a call, not calls within a run -- a later turn carries the earlier ones in its messages and already has a different key). The key is a CANONICAL rendering with SORTED KEYS so it cannot depend on the order a caller built a record in, and it hashes the CANONICAL request rather than the wire body, so a conversation recorded against one provider replays against the other. THE HASH IS ONLY A FILE NAME -- 32-bit FNV-1a written in the library because `crypto` needs libcrypto and this library's whole test story is dependency-free -- and since a collision would SILENTLY serve another request's answer, each fixture records the canonical text and replay REFUSES a mismatch. The canonical rendering is PINNED BY VALUE, because the committed fixtures are named by it and otherwise a change fails every fixture with "no recorded response", which is true and points at the wrong thing. Five perturbations proven red. ALSO CARRIES `llm.embed` (batch embeddings, added for retrieval): BATCH IS THE PRIMITIVE since an embedding API charges per request, and THE ORDER IS WHAT GOES SILENTLY WRONG -- the API returns rows carrying an `index` and does NOT promise they arrive sorted, so read positionally every chunk is paired with ANOTHER CHUNK'S VECTOR and NOTHING RAISES: the store fills, retrieval answers, and the documents are wrong forever. The fixture feeds a DELIBERATELY SHUFFLED response, which is the only way to tell a placement by index from one by arrival, with the control that an ordered response gives the same answer (or "placed by index" is satisfied by a reversal that happens to undo this shuffle). Two refusals rather than guesses: an embedding model is a DIFFERENT model from a chat model, and Anthropic has no embeddings endpoint at all. NOTE FIVE negative_llm_*.err GOLDENS USED TO PIN stdlib/llm.bas LINE NUMBERS, so any insertion into that file rebaselined them -- THREE TIMES IN ONE DAY, for changes none of them was about. Fixed rather than accepted: a `tests/NAME.nolineno` sidecar (the pattern the runner already used for `NAME.libxml2min`, and for the reason its own comment gives -- the requirement lives next to the golden it constrains) normalises `stdlib/<lib>.bas:N:N` to `:LINE:COL` on both sides. The FILE and the MESSAGE are still pinned, both proven by perturbation; only the digits are let go, and they identified nothing since all five messages are distinct. This is the trade run_web_routes.sh already made in as many words -- "the text is the contract" -- and it is deliberately NOT a blanket rule: run_chart.sh still pins chart's positions, because a plausible wrong picture is worse than an error and there the position is how you tell which of several similar refusals fired. Headless, no network, never skips (bar valgrind)
