ONE FIELD PER LINE in the probe's output, which the runner's extractor forced -- two values on a line is run_ksyntax.sh's recorded trap and it bit here within a minute. Twelve perturbations proven red across this increment. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about the clipboard was measured; the doors tier skips without a C++ compiler, saying the two halves were NOT measured separately.
./tests/run_qt_which.sh # WHICH THING WAS THIS EVENT ABOUT? (§0.33) -- `qt.which_tab`, and `qt.right_click` aimed at a tree row and at a tab. THE SHAPE IS ONE THE gBASIC STUDIO BENCH NAMED RATHER THAN THIS BACKEND, and the naming is the contribution: a handler here takes NO ARGUMENTS, so every event that is ABOUT something needs a verb to ask which -- `qt.selected` answers which table or tree row, `qt.gutter_click` which line, `qt.wheel` which way, and this adds which tab. Their own words: *"a reader coming to this backend fresh would find that rule easier to apply than to rediscover per widget."* A TAB IS THE ONE CASE Qt DOES NOT ANSWER BY ITSELF, which is why it needs a verb where a row does not: MEASURED, a right-press MOVES a table's or a tree's current row (so `qt.selected` already says which was clicked) and a right-press on a QTabBar correctly does NOT move the current index -- so `currentIndex` answers the tab that was ALREADY ACTIVE. STU-21's rule is the opposite (*"right-clicking a background tab to close it must not first switch you to it"*), so without this a port acts on the wrong tab IN SILENCE and Studio shipped no tab menu at all rather than one that does. TWO ROUTES, ONE ANSWER: the QPoint a context-menu signal carries, mapped through `QTabBar::tabAt`, and the int `tabCloseRequested` carries -- the other half of the same problem, that index being unreachable from a zero-argument handler so `tabsClosable: true` is unusable without it. Both reach `GbQtRelay`, which gained two slots beside `fire()`, the generic relay having always thrown a signal's arguments away: right for `clicked()` and the loss of the answer for exactly these two. THE TABS ARE AT THE BOTTOM ON PURPOSE AND IT IS THE LOAD-BEARING DETAIL: the point arrives in the EMITTING WIDGET'S coordinates and the bar is not at its origin, so measured with `tabPosition: South` an unmapped `tabAt` answers **-1** -- "no tab" for a click squarely on one -- while with tabs at the TOP the bar IS at the origin and the two agree, so comfortable test data would have hidden the mapping entirely. THE SLOT IS CHOSEN BY THE SIGNAL'S TYPE NAME, NEVER BY ITS ARGUMENT COUNT: `QMetaObject::connect` by index does NOT check argument types, it hands the slot a void* to reinterpret, so `toggled(bool)` reaching `fireIndex(int)` reads FOUR BYTES OUT OF ONE. I EXPECTED VALGRIND TO CATCH THAT AND IT DOES NOT -- measured, the count-based perturbation leaves every behavioural check green AND valgrind silent, the bool sitting in an initialised aligned stack slot and the value being discarded for anything that is not a tab widget -- so the rule is held by tests/qt/slot_choice_scan.py with a planted-copy control, §0.16's arrangement where the native-dialog forcing is a tripwire because offscreen cannot tell the two dialogs apart. That was the one GREEN perturbation and it produced the tripwire. WHAT IT RECORDS IS NARROWED THOUGH WHAT IT CONNECTS IS NOT: the int slot takes ANY single-int signal (one rule rather than a list of names a new Qt version falls off) and records only for a TAB WIDGET, since `stateChanged(int)` on a check box is an int about something else and answering it would be a wrong answer wearing a plausible number -- the fixture declares such a handler as the over-correction guard. AND THE VERB IS NOT `tab_click`, WHICH WAS ITS FIRST NAME AND WAS A LIE: the int route takes any single-int signal, so `currentChanged` fills it too, and that fires when the PROGRAM calls `setCurrentIndex` where nobody clicked anything -- a name has to be true of every route that fills it. `currentChanged` is also what makes the int route DRIVABLE and is how it is asserted; driving the close BUTTON needs the QTabBar's own child widget, which gBASIC cannot name, so what is exercised is the RULE through a signal a program can drive, stated rather than implied since a claim nobody has run is not a claim. `qt.right_click` AIMS AT A TAB THROUGH THE BAR, the one place that verb changes where it delivers, because that is where a real right-click lands -- the context event PROPAGATES to the tab widget and arrives in its coordinates, which is the path the mapping reads back through, where delivering straight to the tab widget would reach the same handler by a route no mouse takes and leave the mapping untested. ITS TREE ROW WAS §0.20's OWN SENTENCE OUTLIVING ITS WIDGET: that section justified the row argument with *"right-click the third file"* and the browser is a `tree` since §0.28, so the one shape it was written for could not be driven -- Studio reported it as *"a tier faithfulness, not a feature"*, which is right and is also why it is here, since without it the tab path would be the only one with an oracle. Eight perturbations proven red. TWO SUITE FAILURES OF MINE WERE FALSE, both the same trap: `pgrep -f "gbasic tests/..."` matched MY OWN SHELL, because the runner was written with a heredoc and run in the same command so the pattern was in that shell's command line -- the check was right both times. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about which tab an event is about was measured -- except the slot-choice tripwire, which reads the source and runs anyway.
./tests/run_qt_keys.sh # SEVERAL SHORTCUTS AND PRESSING A KEY (§0.34) -- `shortcut: [...]` on a menu or toolbar item, `qt.shortcuts`, and `qt.key`. Asked for by gBASIC Studio after Matthew measured by hand that `shortcut: "Ctrl++"` ignores plain Ctrl+= on a US layout. A LIST MEANS ALTERNATIVES (QAction::setShortcuts) and a comma inside one text is still a chord; a misspelled key, which Qt turned into Key_unknown and accepted in silence, is refused by name. `qt.key` sends a real key event through Qt 6's shortcut map with no Qt Test dependency, making the window active SYNCHRONOUSLY (QApplication::setActiveWindow, deprecated but present) because processing events would fire gBASIC's pump inside the call. THE LOAD-BEARING CHECK IS A DIFFERENCE: Ctrl+= does nothing against the single shortcut and fires against the list. THE LIMIT IS STATED: a synthesized key is the key Qt delivers, not a keyboard layout. Four perturbations proven red. Skips without Qt, saying nothing was measured.
./tests/run_qt_align.sh # A COLUMN'S ALIGNMENT (§0.35) -- `align:` on a table's or tree's `field`, and `qt.alignment`. Asked for by gBASIC Studio: its grid is an asking table whose model answered only DisplayRole, so a column could not be right-aligned. One option across held tables, asking tables and trees, applied to every cell as it is made, so a `qt.refresh` row is aligned too. AND A DEFECT FOUND ON THE WAY: a column's options were read for label/type/depth and nothing else, so `alignn: "right"` did nothing in silence; a column's option set is CLOSED now in the shared load-time pass, refused by name with the near miss, which the DECLARATION tier asserts with no Qt at all. THE ORACLE ASKS Qt (the model's alignment role, the item's alignment), never the declaration. Five perturbations proven red.
./tests/run_qt_gutter.sh # A MARGIN ON `source` CARRYING A MARK PER LINE (§0.29), and the click that makes a breakpoint real -- the SECOND of the two asks docs/qt_studio_requests.md names as blocking a feature gBASIC Studio already has (`tree` was the first; that document's own words are "Two of these are gaps that block a feature Studio already has"). IT IS NOT A RULER AND THE USER SAID SO TWICE: *"it's not just line numbers"* on first hearing the word, and then *"I didn't think the gutter was essential because I was only thinking line numbers and forgot about break points and other things"* -- which is the whole argument for the shape, since a margin that could only number lines would be rebuilt the first time anything else wanted to sit in it. THE CATEGORY SET IS DERIVED RATHER THAN CHOSEN, which is what lets it be CLOSED unlike a leaf's properties (75 on a button): two were asked for (`section`, `break`) and the other three ARE gBASIC's own diagnostic severities, `gb_severity` being exactly {error, warning, note}, so a program showing diagnostics has one category per severity instead of flattening three into one. An unknown one is refused BY NAME with a near-miss hint through §0.16's matcher, because a category decides a GLYPH and a PRIORITY and the backend has to know both. THE ENUM ORDER IS THE PRIORITY so there is no second table to drift, and it is two rules rather than a preference: a mark the PERSON placed outranks one the program COMPUTED (`break` wins everything -- a breakpoint is the one mark somebody set deliberately and must see in order to remove, and hiding it behind a computed error makes it unclickable for a reason nothing explains), and among computed marks the more urgent wins, with `section` lowest but ABOVE `note`, which preserves the order Studio's own request states (section 1, error 2 "wins") and keeps the run affordance from being hidden by the least urgent thing there is. LINE 1 IS THE FIRST LINE AND THAT WAS MEASURED, against an ask whose example reads `[0, 12, 31]` and looks 0-based: `source_outline` reports `start_line = 1` and a parse diagnostic carries a 1-based line, so BOTH of the consumer's sources are 1-based and the gutter displays 1-based -- WHICH MEANS THERE IS NO CONVERSION ANYWHERE, and a conversion is exactly where the off-by-one would live, an off-by-one here putting a mark one line from the error it points at, which reads as a margin that works. Line 0 is REFUSED rather than dropped, a 0-based caller being the plausible mistake and a silently dropped mark reporting success while drawing nothing. REPLACE, NEVER ADD OR REMOVE, which is the consumer's shape rather than a simplification -- Studio recomputes the whole list on every redraw and the GTK version removes every mark in the range before adding, so an add/remove pair would make it keep a record of what it had already drawn, a cache to get wrong, and it already has two of those; an empty list clears a category, which falls out of replacing. AND `qt.marks` ANSWERS WHAT WAS SET, NEVER WHAT IS DRAWABLE: a program may mark line 40 and then shorten the document, and a read filtered to the lines still in the file would disagree with the write for a reason the caller cannot see, making `qt.mark` then `qt.marks` non-idempotent -- the drawing is the widget's business, the list is the program's. SEVEN MEASUREMENTS FIRST AND TWO CHANGED THE IMPLEMENTATION: the protected members Qt's own Code Editor example subclasses for (`firstVisibleBlock`, `blockBoundingGeometry`, `blockBoundingRect`, `contentOffset`, `setViewportMargins`) FORCE a QPlainTextEdit subclass, so a `source` is one and a `memo` is still a bare QPlainTextEdit -- not an oversight but what makes `qt.mark` on a memo a REFUSAL rather than a silent no-op; and because that subclass declares no Q_OBJECT (it needs none, its connects being the functor form and `paintEvent` a virtual) ITS METAOBJECT IS QPlainTextEdit's, so `qobject_cast` would SUCCEED FOR A MEMO AS WELL and the refusal is made with `dynamic_cast`, which knows the real type -- the one place here where the obvious code would have been quietly wrong. A CORRECTION TO MY OWN EARLIER NOTE: it said "no moc is needed", and the stronger fact is that A MOC PASS ALREADY RUNS ON THIS FILE, with its `#include` at line 232, which is why a Q_OBJECT in a class declared below it would not compile at all. THE GLYPH IS NOT CONFIGURABLE AND THAT IS §0.23's MEASUREMENT AGAIN: the ask itself records that `dialog-error` and `dialog-error-symbolic` are both standard freedesktop names and NEITHER RESOLVED on the consumer's machine -- Breeze has the first and not the second, Adwaita the second and not the first, and what GTK drew was a grey missing-icon disc wide enough to sit on top of the code -- so four of the five come from QStyle standard pixmaps, which are part of Qt Widgets and need no theme (all five measured non-null 16x16 here with NO icon theme installed), and `break` is PAINTED, a filled circle needing neither style nor font and the one glyph with no dependency at all. A font glyph like U+25B6 was rejected for the same class of reason, needing a font that happens to carry it, which Qt substitutes for in silence. That removes the reason the ask mentioned icons at all; the cost is stated rather than hidden, and if a real need to change one appears that need is the argument. THE CLICK IS IN THIS INCREMENT BECAUSE A BREAKPOINT YOU CANNOT CLICK TO SET IS DECORATIVE, and it needed NO NEW HANDLER SHAPE, which is what made it affordable: §0.21 already established that a handler here takes no arguments and the event is ASKED FOR instead, so `on <part>.gutter` is the THIRD event rather than a signal (after `closed` and `wheel`, the gutter being a plain child widget that emits nothing, so the by-name route would refuse a well-formed handler with "a QPlainTextEdit emits no `gutter` signal") and `qt.gutter_click()` answers { line } exactly as `qt.wheel()` answers the turn, with `unknown` outside a handler -- a different claim from line 0. THE FILTER SITS ON THE GUTTER ITSELF so an ordinary press in the text is still a caret move, installed ONLY WHERE A HANDLER WAS DECLARED, and the control that catches the over-correction is a `source` with no handler firing none. The line is asked of the DOCUMENT through `cursorForPosition` rather than computed from a y coordinate and a line height, a hidden block making those two disagree, with the x pushed into the text area since at the gutter's own x the cursor clamps to the first line whatever was clicked. `qt.gutter_press` is what makes any of it drivable, the `qt.right_click`/`qt.scroll` shape and in the product surface for the reason stated there; a line NOT ON SCREEN is refused, because a mouse cannot land there. WHAT A CHECK COULD OBSERVE IF THIS DID NOTHING -- the question that produced five verbs in §0.20-§0.26 -- HAS AN UNCOMFORTABLE ANSWER HERE, ALMOST NOTHING: `qt.marks` answers what was set, so a backend that stored five lists and painted nothing satisfies every check the language can make, and a QWidget's PIXELS are not reachable from gBASIC at all, unlike a QTextLayout's format ranges which is what made §0.14's colour claim checkable. So the claim is SPLIT and both halves are necessary. `qt.gutter(win, part)` answers { line_numbers, marks, width } or UNKNOWN for a `source` with no margin -- the `qt.pane`/`qt.dialog`/`principal()` distinction, since `width: 0` would say a margin exists and is empty -- and its `width` FOLLOWS THE DIGIT COUNT, which is the one part of the reservation gBASIC can see: a 1200-line document reserves more than a 12-line one, and a gutter that ignored the count passes every presence check and fails that. AND tests/qt/gutter_probe.cpp MAKES THE PAINTING CLAIM, LINKING THE REAL BACKEND OBJECT rather than reimplementing the setup -- the difference from tests/qt/source_probe.cpp and strictly stronger, since what it measures is the painter the product runs through the same `gb_qt_*` seam src/modules/qt.c uses rather than a second copy that could be right while the first is wrong. THREE THINGS MAKE ITS NUMBERS EVIDENCE: the counters are ZERO before any turn of the loop (§0.25's lesson measured once more -- nothing paints offscreen without one, a bare `repaint()` having painted 0 gutter lines where `update()` plus `processEvents()` painted 24), it paints 13 OF 200 lines so it walked real block geometry rather than looping the document, and six marked lines of which one carries TWO categories paint SIX glyphs, seven meaning the priority rule is not applied. ITS FIRST RUN MEASURED FOUR AND THE FIXTURE WAS AT FAULT: the shared line sat at 20 with thirteen lines visible, so the priority case was BELOW THE FOLD -- a check whose subject is off-screen measures nothing, recorded because the number looked perfectly plausible. TWO FURTHER PERTURBATIONS WENT GREEN AND EACH PRODUCED AN OBSERVABLE, the seventh and eighth time in this backend, and both were invisible for ONE reason -- every check was taken at the TOP of the document, where a margin that never repaints still looks right: removing the `updateRequest` connect leaves the numbers sitting still while the text moves under them (all 30 semantic checks and all four paint checks stayed green), so the probe now scrolls to the bottom and requires the margin to have repainted from line 190; and making the display 0-based puts every number in the margin one out, which is the same off-by-one the mark API was designed to have no room for, so the recorded first line is THE NUMBER THE PAINTER DREW rather than a recomputation from `firstVisibleBlock()`, which would agree with a 0-based painter and say nothing. Ten perturbations proven red in all. AND ONE WENT GREEN FOR A REASON THAT WAS NOT ABOUT THE CODE, which is the lasting lesson: with `qobject_cast` substituted the BUILD FAILED, this suite's `make` was unchecked, and it ran the PREVIOUSLY BUILT binary and reported green -- `run_all.sh` guards its own up-front build (line 166) so the gate is safe, and a suite run BY HAND had nothing, which is exactly how a perturbation is exercised; the build is checked here now. That same perturbation CORRECTED A COMMENT OF MINE: it claimed that with no Q_OBJECT the subclass's metaobject IS QPlainTextEdit's so `qobject_cast` would SILENTLY match a memo, and MEASURED IT DOES NOT COMPILE -- Qt 6 answers with a static assertion -- so the wrong code cannot ship and the hazard was overstated, though the refusal itself is still load-bearing since an implementation taking the parts entry at face value accepts a memo and quietly does nothing to it. PLUS A SOURCE TRIPWIRE that runs with no Qt: the five names and their priority live in ONE place and the module ASKS through three accessors rather than spelling them again, two copies of a closed vocabulary being the drift this tree tripwires against -- and this very vocabulary was written out three times before `win_nearest` was derived from the tables, with the newest word missing from the third. Its CONTROL plants a copy and requires the scan to find it, since "no duplicates" is equally satisfied by a scan that read nothing -- PLANTED IN A COPY UNDER THE WORK DIRECTORY, WHICH THE GATE'S OWN LANE SCAN FORCED: the first version planted it in `src/modules/qt.c` itself and restored it, and src/ IS A BUILD INPUT, so the gate reported this suite as one that REBUILDS THE TREE, which in a parallel run means another suite's plain `make` compiles the planted file. Correct, and caught within minutes of the suite existing -- the third time a tripwire in this tree has caught my own brand-new work (run_portable on `set -e` arming, run_qt_split's guard tier on §0.25, this), and a scan reads a file so it can read a COPY. The run it fired on was DISCARDED rather than explained away, the lane verdict being taken at pre-flight from the file as it then was. Both options are refused on anything but a `source`, and each wants a LITERAL true or false rather than being read for truthiness, since every non-empty string in gBASIC is true and `line_numbers: "no"` would turn them ON. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about the margin was measured -- except the grammar and one-vocabulary tiers, which read the grammar and the source and run anyway; the paint tier skips without a C++ compiler, saying the painting was NOT measured.
./tests/run_qt_children.sh # A CONTAINER WHOSE CHILDREN CHANGE (§0.27) -- `qt.refresh(win, name, items)`, plus `qt.children` and `qt.tabs`. ASKED FOR BY THE CONSUMER WITH MEASUREMENTS, which is why this is not a gap I found: docs/qt_dynamic_children_proposal.md was written by the gBASIC Studio side from converting its shell, and it established the SHAPE of the gap rather than describing it -- `qt.read(win, "book", "count")` answers 2 for a two-tab `tabs` so the set is OBSERVABLE, `qt.refresh(win, "book")` refuses by naming tables, and `qt.invoke` passes NO ARGUMENTS, so `removeTab(int)` and `setTabText(int, QString)` are out of reach by construction rather than by omission. Studio's notebook is one tab per open document, which is what the application IS, and the fallback (close, rebuild, reopen) costs per-document undo history AND THE WINDOW'S POSITION ON SCREEN, `x`/`y` being read-only on a QWidget. SIX MEASUREMENTS IN C++ FIRST AND THREE DECIDED THE DESIGN: `removeTab` does NOT delete the page; a kept QPlainTextEdit keeps its text AND undo stack across removeTab+addTab and across a box layout's removeWidget+insertWidget (`isUndoAvailable` 1, visible again with no `show()`), which is the whole reason a reconcile is worth building rather than a rebuild dressed up; and A CHILD DROPPED FROM A LAYOUT AND NOT DELETED STAYS PARENTED AND **VISIBLE**, floating at its old geometry -- so "absent" has to mean DELETED with the name purged, or the window shows a widget the record no longer mentions with nothing raised (§0.17's rule for a dismissed window, one level in). A `tab` CARRIES A NAME IN THE RECORD, which the DECLARATION cannot spell, and that is the one rule added rather than reused: §0.8's one slot is a fact about the GRAMMAR, not about the record, and THE CAPTION IS THE THING THAT CHANGES -- a notebook tab's `* ` unsaved marker -- so keying on it would rebuild the editor on the first keystroke in any document. THE DECLARATION IS UNTOUCHED AND THE BINARY TAUGHT ME THAT: I expected `tab one` to be admitted once the name rule relaxed, and it is refused by a DIFFERENT rule -- a tab needs a caption, because that is what a person reads on it -- so both rules hold and nothing was loosened for the declaration at all. THE NAMING RULE THEN WIDENED BY ITS OWN JUSTIFICATION rather than by analogy: a `column`, `row` and `group` were refused a name on the grounds that a box has no state worth asking about, and the verb gives every one of them some, so the message states the rule now instead of enumerating four words. `qt.children` IS THE ORDER ORACLE AND IT HAD TO BE BUILT BEFORE THE CLAIM COULD BE CHECKED: `qt.at` reads coordinates in a `cells` grid ONLY (§0.8 says so, and the fixture's first draft was told `a` is not in a `cells`), so a box's order was observable through NOTHING; `qt.tabs` answers captions, which `tabText(int)` makes unreadable otherwise. THREE DEFECTS FOUND BY BUILDING IT: a named `tab` never reached the registry at all, because that branch RETURNS EARLY (so a page is not also added to its parent's layout) and skipped the registration twenty lines below -- invisible until something asked a container for a child by NAME, and the symptom was every page rebuilt and the editor holding the record's text instead of what was typed; a child replaced because its WORD changed was dropped TWICE, the prune pass finding it still in the was-there list (§0.18's idempotence guard again); and A USE-AFTER-FREE IN `qt.read` OLDER THAN THIS INCREMENT, its refusal formatting `which.as.string` after `value_free(which)` -- §0.22's own defect one door along, and it survived because A REFUSAL PATH NOTHING EXERCISES IS A REFUSAL PATH NOTHING CHECKS, this fixture being the first thing in the tree ever to ask `qt.read` for a name that is not there. EIGHT PERTURBATIONS, SIX RED AND THE TWO GREEN ONES ARE WORTH MORE: replacing the `delete` with a `hide()` leaves EVERY check green (the name is purged either way and a widget nobody can name is unobservable, the leak excluded by the access-only Qt policy), so that claim is held by a SOURCE TRIPWIRE with a vacuity control; and skipping the NAME PURGE also leaves every check green while valgrind reports 21 INVALID READS, so that half is covered by the tier whose stated reason is exactly this. NOT DONE AND SAID SO: it does not recurse into a KEPT child, and it admits `tabs`/`column`/`row`/`group` only -- a `split` cannot be named (§0.19) and a `cells` grid has declared positions, both refused BY NAME with the reason. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about the reconcile was measured -- except the grammar, declaration and drop tiers, which read the load-time pass or the source and run anyway.
./tests/run_qt_cookbook.sh # docs/qt_cookbook.md -- the Qt tutorial on the shared cannot-lie harness (tools/sync_qt_cookbook.sh wraps the parameterised sync). RUN/CODE/OUTPUT/COVER over examples/qt_cookbook/*.bas. WHAT MAKES A GUI COOKBOOK CHECKABLE AT ALL is that `QT_QPA_PLATFORM=offscreen` is NOT A TEST-ONLY ARRANGEMENT -- it is how a gBASIC GUI program renders on a headless server -- so every recipe builds REAL Qt widgets, interrogates them and prints a golden with nothing on screen, the technique run_gui_cookbook.sh already uses for GTK. AND SIXTEEN OF THE SEVENTEEN DO NOT CALL `qt.run()`, which the page says rather than leaving to be noticed: that verb hands Qt the thread until something calls `qt.stop()`, so a recipe reaching it would HANG (measured, killed at 60s under offscreen, and the harness would report a timeout rather than a failure anybody could read) -- each one opens its window, fires what a person would have clicked with `qt.invoke`, prints and stops, which is also the `check` idiom every program in examples/qt/ carries. SEVENTEEN RECIPES covering EVERY VERB AND EVERY VOCABULARY WORD -- checked by derivation from src/modules/qt.c and src/window_check.c rather than by reading, which is what found the three gaps the first ten left: `tabs`/`tab`, `qt.theme` and `qt.run` itself. Ordered by what bites first rather than by the surface: a first window; a form bound to a record where each field KEEPS ITS KIND (`limit` returns money because the field declared `type: {USD}`); WHERE A HANDLER KEEPS STATE, which is the trap every author meets (gBASIC has no closures and a handler fires beneath `qt.run()`, so it can READ a variable in `main` and CANNOT REBIND one -- warning 2104 names the remedy, and the answer is ONE RECORD whose fields handlers mutate); layout with no coordinates, where `qt.at` asks the LAYOUT rather than any record of where things were put and so reveals that a labelled `field` takes TWO cells; a table feeding a form; menus, which cost no grammar at all; a `choice` translating names to ids; `qt.refresh`; a `source` code editor; the builder; a book of `tabs` pages; theming, whose first half needs NO DISPLAY since a sheet set before any window is remembered until there is a QApplication to put it on; THE EVENT LOOP, the one recipe that DOES call `qt.run()` -- it can terminate because a timer watcher cancels the timer and then calls `qt.stop()`, and THE CANCEL IS THE PART THAT MATTERS, since `qt.stop()` deliberately does not cancel timers and a repeating one left live keeps gBASIC's own post-main loop running so the program never exits (197 further ticks, measured); ASKING BEFORE ACTING; and CHOOSING A FILE, which found a gap of its own -- `temp_dir()` answers a `{dir}`, so the most natural `folder:` there is was refused as "not text", and the path options plus `qt.answer` take a reference now, the two being separate doors and a fix reaching one being the shape webclient's read and write halves had; and CLOSING A WINDOW, where a close is BOOKKEEPING rather than a hide (Qt leaves a dismissed window alive and still readable) so `on <win>.closed` is the one place a form can still be read, and `qt.stop()` is NOT a close. AND A WINDOW SHOWN MODALLY, which is the same window shown a different way -- `qt.open_modal` blocks and answers true or false, `qt.accept`/`qt.reject` are what a button handler calls, and the recipe shows the `closed` handler firing on a CANCEL too so the reader sees that what decides whether to USE the form is the answer rather than the handler. TWO OF THE RECIPES WERE WRITTEN BY GETTING THEM WRONG, which is why they teach what they do: recipe 5's handler first set `g.chosen` while `qt.refresh` looks up the VARIABLE `chosen`, so the form kept showing row 0 -- a handler cannot rebind the subject, and what works is mutating its FIELDS, which reach main's copy; and recipe 8 took a SNAPSHOT (`tasks = g.tasks`), so the handler grew `g.tasks` while the refresh re-read main's unchanged copy -- `append` mutates IN PLACE and resolves through the frame chain, so appending to the SUBJECT ITSELF is what makes a handler able to change the data at all. Both are stated on the page as the rule rather than hidden by the fix. RECIPE 09 SKIPS WITHOUT KSyntaxHighlighting, asked of the BINARY via `qt.can_highlight()` rather than probed on the machine (tests/build_has.sh's argument), because a build without it still renders a `source` and still edits text -- the output is merely different and correct. THE SUITE EXPORTS GBASIC_SYNTAX_PATH=editors/kate, which is not housekeeping: without it recipe 09 reports "none installed" and its golden becomes a fact about the machine, which is precisely §0.14's recorded trap where a copy hand-installed in a developer's home made the whole feature appear to work. Recipe 09 deliberately prints only the LANGUAGE and not the definition's FILE, since which copy wins is the machine's business and a path in a golden would be unportable.
./tests/run_qt_lifecycle.sh # THE WINDOW LIFECYCLE -- `qt.close`, `qt.is_open`, `on <win>.closed`, and the window addressable BY ITS OWN NAME (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.17). WHAT WAS MISSING WAS NOT "A SECOND WINDOW": measured first, that already worked -- two open at once, parts scoped per window, a dialog parenting to whichever is named, `qt.stop()` with both up, and opening the same declaration twice already refused by name. THIRD TIME IN THIS BACKEND that measuring a gap before filling it found it closed (menus needed no grammar; the builder's door was already open). What was missing was the LIFECYCLE, and the four gaps were measured one throwaway program at a time: `qt.close` undefined, `qt.invoke` reaching PARTS ONLY so the window itself was unaddressable, `on a_win.closed` naming nothing, and what closing the last window does to `qt.run()` UNMEASURABLE because nothing could close one. THEN SIX THINGS WERE MEASURED IN C++ AND THE FIFTH DECIDED THE DESIGN: quitOnLastWindowClosed is TRUE (Qt's default, never set by us); `close()` offscreen delivers QEvent::Close to an event filter; closing one of two leaves `exec()` running; closing the last one RETURNS from it; AFTER `close()` THE WIDGET IS STILL ALIVE AND STILL READABLE; and deleting right after `close()` returns is safe. THE FIFTH IS WHY A CLOSE IS BOOKKEEPING AND NOT A HIDE -- Qt does not set `WA_DeleteOnClose`, so a dismissed window goes on answering `qt.read` with what it was showing, which is a program reading off a screen nobody is looking at and raising NOTHING, the failure class this backend is tested against arriving from Qt's own defaults. So a close removes the window from gBASIC's registry and every verb needing an open one refuses by name, WHICH MAKES `on <win>.closed` LOAD-BEARING RATHER THAN A NOTIFICATION: it fires BEFORE the registry forgets, so it is the one place a program can still read what the person typed -- asserted BOTH WAYS, since either half alone passes on an implementation that got the order backwards. THE WINDOW IS A PART OF ITSELF: one `parts.insert` opens FOUR DOORS none of which knew anything new (`qt.read(win,"app","windowTitle")`, `qt.set`, `qt.invoke(win,"app","showMaximized")`, `on app.closed`), measured first -- `windowTitle` is a Q_PROPERTY of QWidget at index 43 and `close()`/`showMaximized()` are invokable by name at 24 and 21 -- which is §1's by-name argument paying off a FOURTH time. ITS COST IS A COLLISION AND IT IS REFUSED RATHER THAN SILENTLY WON: a part called `app` would register SECOND, over the root, so `on app.closed` would connect to a button and the window's own title would be unreadable; it gets its own sentence, because "declared twice" is the wrong advice when one of the two is the window, and the same rule reaches a BUILT record (one checker, two adapters). `closed` IS ANSWERED BEFORE THE BY-NAME SIGNAL LOOKUP because a QWidget EMITS NOTHING WHEN IT CLOSES -- it RECEIVES an event -- so that lookup has nothing to find and would refuse a well-formed handler; the special case is NARROW and a tier says so, `on save.closed` on a button falling through to `a QPushButton emits no closed signal`. ONE PATH FOR ALL THREE WAYS IN: `qt.close` calls `widget->close()` rather than doing the work itself, so the program's verb, an invoked `close` and the window manager's button are the SAME CODE -- the `would`/`execute` rule in `automation`, since if they could disagree a suite driving one would describe the other; the X button is the one that cannot be driven here (offscreen has no window manager) and reaches the same call through Qt's platform layer, so that much is CONSTRUCTION rather than measurement and is said rather than implied. `qt.is_open` ANSWERS WHERE THE OTHERS REFUSE, the `qt.can_display` rule, and it is NEEDED because a window can close WITHOUT THE PROGRAM ASKING -- once a person can dismiss one, every other verb starts raising for a reason that is nobody's mistake. A CORRECTION THIS INCREMENT EXPOSED RATHER THAN INTRODUCED: `gb_qt_stop` called `QCoreApplication::quit()` and MEASURED on Qt 6.10.2 THAT CLOSES EVERY TOP-LEVEL WINDOW (it calls closeAllWindows) where `exit(0)` ends the loop and leaves them alone -- so `qt.stop()`, which means GIVE ME THE THREAD BACK and nothing more, was DISMISSING THE PROGRAM'S WINDOWS, and nothing could see it: they were merely HIDDEN, every part stayed readable, and no fixture asked whether the window was still up. It surfaced only once a close became bookkeeping, when a program that reads its window after `qt.run()` returned started being told it was not open. MEASURED RATHER THAN GREPPED, and the two answers differ: eight programs in the tree read a window textually after `qt.run()` and exactly ONE actually broke -- the cookbook recipe whose subject IS the event loop -- because in the others the reads sit inside handlers that run before the stop, or `qt.run()` is not reached in the mode the suite drives; BOTH HALVES are asserted now because each catches a different wrong implementation -- `is_open` catches a stop that runs the close bookkeeping, `visible` catches one that merely HIDES the window, which is what the defect actually did and which `is_open` alone would go on missing. REOPENING IS BOUNDED AND THAT HAD TO BE MEASURED: before this a second `qt.open` met "already open" so open-and-close in a loop could not be written, and the obvious implementation grows for ever, one node per part per open. `deleteLater()` WAS THE OBVIOUS ANSWER AND IS NOT RELIED ON -- a deferred delete needs a loop to spin and the commonest shape here closes the LAST window, which returns from `exec()` -- so a close noticed in the event filter waits and is deleted at the next `qt.open`, half the cycles exercising each path. Measured 200 vs 2000 cycles: retired 41.9 -> 42.3 MB (1.01x), not retired 45.1 -> 81.6 MB (1.81x), so the gate at 1.25 separates them as a RATIO rather than an absolute size. ELEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED and TWO ARE WORTH RECORDING. (1) THE RE-ENTRY GUARD WAS IN THE WRONG PLACE AND ONLY A PERTURBATION SHOWED IT: written as `!n->widget` alone it caught the `qt.close` path -- where nulling the widget before closing already stops a second entry -- so removing the flag left the whole suite GREEN, and the case it misses is the REALISTIC one, a person clicking X and the handler tidying up by closing the window, where the widget is still ours to delete so deleting it destroys the event filter currently executing, the node the outer frame is about to hand to `g_closed`, and the widget both are parented to. It segfaulted with NO OUTPUT AT ALL, block-buffered stdout going with the crash, which is §0.3's shape arriving from my own code; the fixture drives both paths now. (2) THE HANDLER-LESS WINDOW IS THE TIER TO READ FIRST: the bookkeeping must be UNCONDITIONAL and the handler is the optional part, so a filter installed only where `on app.closed` appears would leave exactly the programs that declared no handler reading a dismissed window -- and nothing else in that fixture could see it, every other window there having a handler; worse than a wrong answer, the registry then holds an entry pointing at a DELETED window, which is why that perturbation reports exit 139 after the mismatch that names it. Honest overlap: the growth fixture's window has no handler either. AND THE TWELFTH BACKTICK: this suite wrote three and the scanner caught TWO, both on `pass`/`fail` lines, missing the one on its own `refuse` helper -- nearly every suite here has such a helper and its first argument IS the message, so the four names were never the boundary of the class, only the names that had been noticed; `tools/scan_shell_backticks.py` reads every function THE FILE ITSELF DEFINES now, DERIVED rather than listed, with that half asserted separately in the selftest (disabled, the `refuse` line goes unflagged) and measured across all 190 suites with no new findings. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about closing a window was measured -- except the DECLARATION tier, which needs no Qt and runs anyway, being a claim about the load-time pass.
./tests/run_qt_chooser.sh # A FILE CHOOSER -- `qt.choose_file`, `qt.choose_save`, `qt.choose_folder` and `qt.dialog()` (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.16). THREE VERBS RATHER THAN ONE WITH A MODE, because they ask three genuinely different questions -- which existing file, what shall I call it, which folder -- which is also how Qt splits them and which lets each refuse the options that mean nothing to it instead of ignoring them; `qt.open_file` was REJECTED AS A NAME, reading as though it opens the file, where `f = qt.open_file(app)` looks like it answers contents or a handle. FOUR THINGS WERE MEASURED BEFORE ANY OF IT WAS WRITTEN AND THREE CHANGED THE DESIGN. (1) THE DESKTOP'S OWN CHOOSER CANNOT BE DRIVEN: with the native dialog (the XDG portal, which is what Qt reaches for on Wayland) `selectFile` does NOTHING -- the selection is empty before exec -- and accepting does nothing either, 41 pump ticks and rc 0 and no file, so a program could not preselect a path and `qt.answer` would be a SILENT NO-OP, the failure class this whole backend is tested against; `DontUseNativeDialog` is therefore FORCED rather than offered. (2) AND OFFSCREEN CANNOT TELL THE TWO APART, because with no portal to reach Qt falls back to its own widget dialog -- so the native path measures as DRIVABLE under the one platform every suite here runs on, which is comfortable test data hiding the difference, so the forcing is held by a SOURCE TRIPWIRE with its own vacuity control rather than by a tier that would pass either way. IT IS THE TIER THAT EARNS ITS PLACE: the perturbation allowing the native dialog back leaves EVERY behavioural check green and is caught by the tripwire alone. (3) `selectFile` IS IGNORED WHILE THE DIALOG IS VISIBLE and its name field has focus, deliberately, so Qt never overwrites what somebody is typing -- so the obvious implementation of `qt.answer(path)` left the selection EMPTY and the accept silently refused. What works is WHAT A PERSON DOES: type into the chooser's `fileNameEdit`, press the AcceptRole button (labelled Open, Save or Choose by mode, found BY ROLE since the label is translated) -- the rule `qt.answer` already followed for a message box, and it pays again, because QT DISABLES THAT BUTTON for a path the mode cannot take, so the refusal is the chooser's own rule rather than a reimplementation of it AND the warning box a direct `accept()` would raise never appears. That box was a HANG rather than a failure, opening a second nested loop from inside the first one's pump tick. (4) A REJECTED QFileDialog STILL REPORTS ITS SELECTION, so reading the file list without checking the result code answers a path for a chooser the person CANCELLED -- not an error but a wrong answer. THE ANSWER IS TEXT and that was decided rather than defaulted: `{file}` and `{dir}` both accept a path that is not there (measured), so a typed answer WAS available, but three sibling verbs would then answer TWO kinds and the rule this backend follows is that a kind comes from the DECLARATION and not from Qt (§0.5) -- a chooser has no declaration and what Qt holds is a path, so `{file}qt.choose_file(app)` where a typed value is wanted, which is one inline modifier. CANCELLING ANSWERS `unknown`, NEVER "": the empty string is the plausible wrong answer, `if path = "" then` happening to work while `read({file}path)` does not, and `if path then` on `unknown` RAISES, which is loud. `filter:` takes text OR A LIST, joined with Qt's own `;;` by the backend because two semicolons meaning "or" is a Qt-ism nobody should have to know. THE OPTION SET IS CLOSED AND REFUSED BY NAME with a near-miss hint -- four options is a vocabulary that CAN be closed, unlike the 75 properties a QPushButton exposes, and an ignored misspelling is a chooser that opens in the wrong folder with the wrong filter and says nothing; `filter:` and `name:` are refused on `qt.choose_folder`, where Qt would ignore both. `qt.answer` GREW A THIRD ARGUMENT SHAPE: a path answers a chooser, `false` is a CANCEL (what a timeout actually wants to say), and `true` is REFUSED because it would have to mean "accept what?" -- with a path on a message box refused the other way round, the two kinds told apart in the backend so each refusal names the dialog that is actually open. `qt.dialog()` IS THE ORACLE AND WAS BUILT BEFORE THE FIXTURE, which is the discipline §0.8 arrived at the hard way: `title:` and `filter:` would otherwise be ACCEPTED AND UNOBSERVABLE, the hole a leaf's `label:` sat in for four increments while every check passed. It answers the innermost dialog as a record or `unknown` when none is open -- the `principal()` distinction, since "nothing is open" and "a dialog carrying no fields" are different claims. THE NEAR-MISS MATCHER IS EXTRACTED, NOT COPIED: the option names are the SECOND closed vocabulary here, so `gb_nearest_word(word, set)` is the matcher and `gb_window_word_nearest` is three lines of it -- that comment already recorded the window VOCABULARY having been written out three times with the newest word missing from the third, and two copies of the MATCHER is the same drift one level up. Nine perturbations proven red, each caught by the tier written for it -- AND ONE FOUND A FALSE CLAIM IN THE FIXTURE'S OWN COMMENT: a cancel with nothing preselected answers `unknown` EITHER WAY, the selection being empty either way, so the check that claimed to catch a result-code-ignoring backend did not and an unrelated tier caught it; it opens the chooser ON A FILE now, which is also the realistic shape. A GAP THE COOKBOOK FOUND: `temp_dir()` answers a `{dir}`, so the most natural `folder:` there is was refused as "not text" -- every path builtin in this language already takes a string, a `{file}` or a `{dir}` alike, so the path options AND `qt.answer` take a reference now, asserted for both because they are two separate doors and a fix reaching one is the shape webclient's read and write halves had. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about choosing a file was measured -- except the native tripwire, which reads the source and runs anyway, since a suite that skipped it with everything else would leave the forcing unguarded on exactly the machines that ship the chooser.
./tests/run_qt_split.sh # `split` AND `scroll` -- A ROW OR COLUMN YOU CAN DRAG, and a region that SCROLLS (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.19). DERIVED FROM gBASIC STUDIO'S OWN SOURCE RATHER THAN GUESSED, which is why these two came first of §0.18's remaining gaps: Studio's shell is `gtk.paned` THREE TIMES NESTED and `gtk.scrolled` FIVE TIMES, and nothing in this vocabulary could express either -- `row` and `column` are BOXES nobody can drag, and while `source`, `memo` and `table` scroll themselves an ordinary column of widgets does not. One increment because they are one layout. AND IT CORRECTED MY OWN GAP LIST: I had named toolbar, status bar, progress bar, slider, spin box and date picker; measured, Studio uses NONE of them (its toolbars are boxes of buttons, which `row` + `button` already covers) and needs these two instead. FIVE THINGS MEASURED IN C++ FIRST AND THE IMPLEMENTATION IS SMALL BECAUSE OF THE SECOND: a QSplitter has NO LAYOUT (like a QTabWidget, so the layout guards had to learn about it); PARENTING IS ENOUGH -- `count()` went to 2 from two `new QLabel(..., sp)` calls with no `addWidget` anywhere, so the construction that already existed does the work; `orientation` is a Q_PROPERTY (Horizontal=1 Vertical=2, and it accepts the string "Vertical"); `widgetResizable` DEFAULTS TO FALSE; and ONE PANE HAS ZERO HANDLES (n panes give n-1). THE RESIZABLE DEFAULT IS THE TRAP: left alone the inner widget keeps its original size for ever, so content never reflows to the viewport and a RESIZED window shows a stripe of widgets beside a grey expanse with nothing raised -- measured working at 40 rows giving an inner height of 932 against a 258 viewport. `sizes` IS NEITHER A PROPERTY NOR INVOKABLE (it takes a QList<int>), so saving and restoring pane widths needs its own verb and is NOT in this increment, stated rather than discovered. THE DIRECTION SPENDS THE ONE FREE SLOT: §0.8 measured that a container carries a name OR options and never both, and a splitter with no direction is not a splitter where a name is something only some splitters ever want -- so `split across` / `split down` at ZERO grammar cost, refused by name with a near-miss hint. THE COST IS REAL AND STATED: a `split` cannot be named, so `on x.splitterMoved` and `qt.set` cannot reach it; a `scroll` needs no direction so its slot IS free, it may be named, and that is what makes `widgetResizable` and the scrollbar policies readable by the ordinary route. WHICH LEFT THE SPLIT UNOBSERVABLE, SO THE ORACLE CAME FIRST (the discipline §0.8 arrived at the hard way): `qt.pane(win, part)` answers { kind, direction, panes, index } for a split and { kind, resizable } for a scroll, and `unknown` for neither -- a record saying `panes: 0` would read as a splitter holding nothing, the rule `qt.dialog` and `principal()` already follow. ASKED OF THE LAYOUT, walking up from a part that CAN be named, which is `qt.at`'s own justification; it keeps THE LAST WIDGET IT CAME THROUGH because a pane is usually a CONTAINER, and the perturbation that asked the splitter for the named widget's own index LEFT EVERY CHECK PASSING since every other part in the fixture happens to BE a pane -- a nested-pane window was added for it. THE NESTING CHECK FALLS OUT: the outer split must report TWO panes, so an inner split hung off the window instead of its parent is caught, the shape the menu oracle learned the hard way. TWO THINGS FOUND ON THE WAY. (1) A NAMED LEAF WITH NO OPTIONS WAS A PARSE ERROR -- `field who()`, `source code()` -- and a PANE needs no label so that is exactly what an author writes; measured at ZERO conflicts and kept. (2) A PANE HAS NOWHERE TO PUT A LABEL AND THE REFUSAL IS NOT IN THE VALIDATOR, which is where it went first AND COULD NOT WORK: `label()` reads a TOP-LEVEL label (what a `group "Credit"` carries) while a leaf's `label:` is an OPTION, so it read NULL for every leaf and the check silently never fired -- §0.8's own recorded defect reproduced one increment later. It lives in `gb_qt_part` now, narrow BY DERIVATION rather than taste: a `button`, `check`, `text`, `memo` and `source` all consume their own caption at construction, so only a `field` and a `choice` (whose label the PARENT LAYOUT places) are refused, with a `check` keeping its caption as a pane asserted beside it. ELEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED. THE GUARD TRIPWIRE IS THE ONE TO READ: a splitter has no layout, so THREE separate doors ask a parent for one (`gb_qt_container`, `gb_qt_part`, `gb_qt_table`) and each was found BY RUNNING THE THING -- a table as a direct pane was refused with "that container cannot hold a table", a true sentence about the wrong thing -- and a FOURTH door would refuse panes in silence, so the tripwire balances the parentheses of every `if` mentioning `!p->layout` and asks whether THAT CONDITION names a splitter. Counting LINES instead reported two perfectly well guarded conditions, because they name the local the function already made rather than an inline cast; and a perturbation that DELETED a condition made the failure message blame the wrong thing, so it names both ways to fail now. THE GUARD TRIPWIRE FIRED FOR REAL ON 2026-10-19 and the lesson is about the TRIPWIRE: §0.25's model-backed table is a FIFTH door, its placement an if/else chain whose SPLITTER ARM COMES FIRST and whose last arm is a bare `else if (!p->layout)` fall-through for a tab page -- correct, and flagged, because the scanner asked each condition in isolation. MEASURED BEFORE THE SCANNER WAS TOUCHED, which is the order that matters: the asking table really is a working pane, so the code was right and the check was wrong. A condition now counts as guarded when ANY EARLIER BRANCH OF ITS OWN CHAIN names a splitter, walked back through `} else` rather than by a character window, which would be a guess. AND THE CHAIN WALK CREATED A NEW WAY TO MEASURE NOTHING -- a walk that hopped too far would find a splitter somewhere and call everything guarded -- so there is a CONTROL now, two planted doors in a scratch copy (a standalone `if (!p->layout)` refusal, and this chain with its splitter arm removed) both of which must be flagged, since "no offenders" is equally satisfied by a scanner that read nothing. The behavioural half lives in run_qt_ask.sh, because a source scan alone is what let this gap exist. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about dragging or scrolling was measured -- except the grammar, guard and declaration tiers, which read the source or the load-time pass and run anyway.
./tests/run_qt_modal.sh # A WINDOW SHOWN MODALLY -- `qt.open_modal`, `qt.accept`, `qt.reject` (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.18), the last item on §0.15's open list. WHAT IT IS FOR: `qt.confirm` answers a boolean because TWO BUTTONS IS THE WHOLE OF WHAT A MESSAGE BOX OFFERS, so anything richer -- a form to fill in, a list to choose from, a thing with a layout of its own -- is an ordinary `window` declaration shown modally. IT IS THE SAME WINDOW AND THAT IS THE CLAIM THIS SUITE IS BUILT AROUND: `qt.open` and `qt.open_modal` differ in exactly two things (whether the root is a QDialog, and what happens once it is built) and everything else -- the record check, the already-open refusal, the display refusal, starting Qt, the syntax paths, the options, the build, the connect, the bind -- is THE SAME CODE, because two copies of it would drift, which is the shape this tree tripwires against (`would`/`execute` in `automation`; one checker two adapters in §0.9). HELD TWO WAYS, since the claim is structural: a TRIPWIRE counting the places that build a window (exactly one, with a vacuity control) and a PARITY tier that opens ONE DECLARATION BOTH WAYS and requires every observable to agree -- parts, the window's own title, a column heading, a cell, the menu bar, and the grid position `qt.at` reads back from the layout. A QDialog ROOT WAS MEASURED BEFORE BEING CHOSEN: everything this backend builds works on one (a menu bar -- which here is a plain QMenuBar in the root's box layout rather than QMainWindow's -- a form, tabs, a table, buttons), and `accept` (39), `reject` (40) and `done` (38) are INVOKABLE BY NAME on a QDialog while being ABSENT from a QWidget (-1), so the answer mechanism is Qt's own and an ordinary window genuinely has nothing to accept. MAKING EVERY ROOT A QDialog WOULD ALSO HAVE WORKED and was rejected as a wider change for nothing, since with a QWidget root the refusal is a fact rather than a policy; a QDialog shown with `show()` is an ordinary window (`isModal` is false until `exec()`). FOUR WAYS IT CAN END, and the fourth measurement shaped the implementation: `qt.accept` -> true, `qt.reject`/`qt.close`/the window manager's button -> false, and the `closed` handler fires for ALL FOUR. ACCEPTING DELIVERS NO QEvent::Close AT ALL -- measured, and finer than the guess that prompted it: `close()` delivers one on a VISIBLE widget AND on a merely HIDDEN one, and NOTHING on a QDialog that `done()` has already finished with, so a backend whose bookkeeping hung off the event would skip the commonest ending IN SILENCE and `qt.is_open` would answer true for a window the program had finished with. The ending is therefore ONE FUNCTION CALLED FROM TWO PLACES AND IDEMPOTENT (`node_closed`), not a side effect of an event. AND `qt.open_modal` CLOSES THE WINDOW BEFORE RETURNING, because `accept` leaves the widget alive while the X button closes it -- so `false` would otherwise mean two different states and every `qt.close` after a modal window would need an `qt.is_open` guard; closing here makes the handler fire EXACTLY ONCE however the window ended, so §0.17's single rule (the handler is where a dismissed form is read) holds with no exception, at the stated cost that the subject is read in the handler rather than after the call. ITS EXTENT IS THE DIALOG'S AND THE CONTROL IS WHAT MAKES THAT PRECISE: §0.15 measured that a dialog raised FROM A HANDLER suspends gBASIC's sources, and this is the other case and the common one -- "show this form and wait" is opened from `main`, and there the sources KEEP RUNNING, measured from another process (the webserver answers `pong` while the window is up) and from inside (a `timer` fires in the modal loop), which is also what makes the window answerable without a person and therefore what makes any of this drivable. WITHOUT THAT CONTROL §0.15 READS AS "a nested Qt loop starves gBASIC", WHICH IS FALSE and would be the wrong lesson to carry into the Windows port: what starves it is re-entering the INTERPRETER, not the loop. `qt.accept` RATHER THAN `qt.answer`, which exists to drive a dialog FROM THE PROGRAM when nobody is there -- this is the person's own answer arriving through a button, a different thing wearing the same word -- and its refusal names the verb that would have made the window modal, because Qt's own would say "a QWidget emits no..." about a method and name the wrong thing. SEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED and FOUR DEFECTS OF MINE CAME OUT OF THIS WORK, the first of them caught by THE GATE rather than by any Qt suite: the shared open path was written just after `#endif /* HAVE_QT */` and so OUTSIDE the guard, which compiled on a full build and broke `make QT_AVAILABLE=0` with `QT_ERRBUF undeclared` -- this file's convention cuts BOTH ways and I had only the half it states, that code which is NOT optional must stay out of those guards; code that IS optional must stay IN them, and none of the ten Qt suites could see it because they all run on a full build. The cheap check named in this very file (`make QT_AVAILABLE=0` before trusting a green gate) is the one I skipped. THE OTHER THREE: (1) THE ENDING WAS HUNG OFF THE EVENT and the accepted case silently did nothing -- found by the first program written against it, which reported `returned true` beside `still open true`. (2) THE IDEMPOTENCE GUARD WAS IN TWO PLACES AND SO NEITHER COPY WAS LOAD-BEARING: the same `closing` test sat in `node_closed` AND in the event filter, so removing the one that owns the invariant left the suite GREEN; it is in one place now and the filter asks, which is also what keeps the retire list from being appended twice. (3) THE TRIPWIRE'S OWN CONTROL COULD NOT FAIL -- `grep -c` counts matching LINES and the planted second call site went on the same line, which is exactly how somebody would write a second path by hand (`modal ? a : b`); it counts OCCURRENCES now, and the control is what found it. AND THE VALGRIND TIER WAS UNBOUNDED, which a perturbation proved rather than suggested: with the pump not armed around `exec()` the fixture waits for an answer that can never come and valgrind makes the wait an order of magnitude longer, so the tier ran for ever and the whole suite was killed from outside WITH NO VERDICT PRINTED -- red, silent, and indistinguishable from still running. `timeout` CANNOT WRAP IT because `vg_run_access_only` is a shell FUNCTION (run_odbc.sh's recorded trap, hit once already this session), so it runs against a deadline in the background, and a KILLED valgrind exits 137 rather than $VG_EXIT so the killed case needs its own branch or a hang reads as a clean run. AND THAT BOUND LEAKED A PROCESS FOR 19 HOURS, found 2026-10-19 by sweeping the machine rather than by any check: `$!` is the SUBSHELL bash forks to run a shell function in the background, so `kill -9` on it left valgrind and the interpreter underneath it ORPHANED, holding a modal dialog with nothing waiting on it -- IDENTICAL IN CAUSE to the leak run_qt_dialog.sh records (`timeout ... &` makes `$!` the bound, not the child), which is the same trap twice in two days in two suites I wrote. The deadline kills the CHILDREN BY PID first, while the parent is still there for `pgrep -P` to ask, then the waiter, then sweeps by name as a backstop -- and THE ABSENCE IS ASSERTED, because the leak is invisible from inside: the tier passed, the suite passed, and docs/reference.md's bold promise that nothing this interpreter starts outlives it was being broken by the SUITE, which run_process_lifetime.sh structurally cannot see. Proven red with the old kill (both the hang and the leftover reported). THE BY-NAME BACKSTOP IS THE PART TO HANDLE CAREFULLY and it is not hypothetical: a pattern matches any process whose command line merely MENTIONS the fixture, and the EXIT trap killed the very shell that was verifying this fix -- so the perturbation's restore never ran and the perturbed file sat there looking restored, which is why the pattern names the INTERPRETER's invocation and why a restore is verified by reading the file rather than by the `cp` having been typed. NOT ASSERTED AND SAID SO: a modal window opened from INSIDE a handler is legal and undrivable here (§0.15's limit means only a person can answer it), and so is a modal window over a modal window -- a claim nobody has run is not a claim. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about a modal window was measured -- except the one-open-path tripwire, which reads the source and runs anyway; the extent tier skips without curl.
./tests/run_qt_icon.sh # ICONS -- `icon:` on a leaf or a menu item, `qt.set(... "icon" ...)` and `qt.has_icon` (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.23). Studio uses Gtk.IconTheme and nothing in this vocabulary could express an icon at all. SIX MEASUREMENTS AND FOUR DECIDED SOMETHING, the first being what makes this an increment rather than a documentation line: `setProperty("icon", QString(...))` RETURNS FALSE for a theme name AND for a file path, with the icon left null, because QVariant will not convert a string to a QIcon -- so `icon` is THE ONE PROPERTY THE BY-NAME ROUTE CANNOT CARRY. Its branch lives in `set_property`, the ONE door the declaration's options and `qt.set` both go through, so the two cannot disagree about what a source is; tripwired with its vacuity control. THREE SOURCES, TOLD APART BY FACTS AND NOT GUESSES: a `SP_` name is one of QStyle's 81 StandardPixmap keys -- QT'S OWN NAMESPACE, and measured those work with NO ICON THEME INSTALLED, the only source that always does; an EXISTING FILE is asked of the filesystem, so a fact rather than a shape; anything else is a freedesktop theme name. AND THE FAILURES ARE ANSWERED DIFFERENTLY, WHICH IS THE DESIGN. A path that is not there, a file that is not an image, and a misspelled `SP_` name are all REFUSED -- each a fact we can check, with an unambiguous intent, and `SP_` being Qt's own namespace so a name it rejects is a MISSPELLING. A theme name that resolves to nothing is a fact about THE MACHINE: measured, this one has NO THEME AT ALL (`themeName()` empty, `hasThemeIcon("document-save")` FALSE), so refusing would make `icon: "document-save"` fail on exactly the HEADLESS MACHINES THE RELEASE TARGETS -- it WARNS (2116), the position §0.14 takes for a missing syntax definition and for the same reason, that the program is usable without the picture and the failure is otherwise INVISIBLE, a button with no icon looking exactly like one never meant to have one. All three asserted, and the warning tier ALSO asserts THE PROGRAM CARRIED ON, which is the whole difference from a refusal -- plus its own branch for a machine that HAS a theme, where the name resolves and nothing must warn, said rather than failed. `qt.has_icon` IS THE ORACLE AND IT WAS NOT FORESEEN: it came from asking what a check could OBSERVE, since a QIcon has NO TEXT FORM and measured, a button carrying a real PNG and one carrying nothing BOTH answered "" through `qt.read` -- so without it every check would pass on a backend that resolved nothing, and worse, `qt.read` was answering a WRONG answer rather than no answer, the class this backend refuses. It refuses now and names the verb; that verb is also what A PROGRAM needs, an icon the theme lacks being a warning, so falling back to a text-only button means being able to ask. A part that cannot carry one (a `text`, a `table`) is refused NAMING ITS KIND, because "no icon" would read as an answer rather than a category mistake. CLEARING IS LEGAL AND SILENT, a program swapping a play icon for a pause one having to be able to take one away -- and falling through would ask the theme for an icon called "". THE PNG THE FIXTURE NEEDS IS GENERATED BY THE RUNNER rather than committed, a binary fixture nothing can reproduce being one that tests itself, AND THE RUNNER ASSERTS ITS OWN PREMISE (a broken generator would make "a path was refused" pass for the wrong reason). SEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED, including both over-corrections -- a theme miss made to refuse (caught by the warning tier, which is the one that would break every headless machine) and `has_icon` always saying yes. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about icons was measured -- except the one-door tripwire, which reads the source and runs anyway.
./tests/run_qt_ask.sh # A TABLE THAT ASKS FOR ITS CELLS -- `table x to ask fn`, `qt.rows`, and a model behind a QTableView (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.25, which §0.6 deferred). gBASIC STUDIO IS THE CONSUMER RATHER THAN A HYPOTHESIS: its results grid switches to `datagrid.create_virtual(count_fn, cell_fn)` ABOVE 500 ROWS and `datagrid` is GTK-ONLY, so this was the ONE thing its Qt conversion could not express -- found by counting Studio's whole dependency surface rather than its widgets. ONE NUMBER DECIDED WHETHER IT IS POSSIBLE AT ALL, a model's `data()` having to call back into the interpreter: measured in C++ FIRST, 27 calls for 100 rows, 27 for 10,000, 27 for a MILLION, 87 for a 900px viewport, and `scrollToBottom` on a million cost 54 IN TOTAL, jumping straight to row 999,999 rather than touching anything on the way. THE COST IS THE VIEWPORT, NOT THE TABLE. AND IT NEEDED NO NEW SEAM, which CORRECTS §0.25's own first assessment: `invoke_function(stmt, args, argc, receiver)` ALREADY takes arguments and returns a Value -- the handler path simply passes `NULL, 0, NULL` because handlers take none, and `qt_invoke_handler` is already a caller -- so this is one more callback beside `GbQtInvokeFn` rather than a new kind of crossing, which moved it from a large increment to a medium one. `ask` COSTS ZERO GRAMMAR: `IDENT IDENT TO IDENT IDENT` is the production a named container with a subject already uses and the direction is recognised BY POSITION rather than by a token, so `ask` joins `show` and `edit` for nothing; the subject is a FUNCTION NAME rather than a list, which is what the word says. A FORM IS STILL REFUSED ONE and that is a CONTROL rather than caution: a form shows or edits ONE record whose fields it can read, so there is nothing for it to ask FOR and `form x to ask f` would be a declaration whose subject could never be bound -- without it, "ask is a direction" is satisfied by letting every word that carries a subject take it. THE ROW COUNT IS A VERB because it CHANGES (a results grid is refilled by every query, so a count fixed at open would be wrong the first time anybody ran a second one), setting it resets the model which is how Qt is told to re-ask -- so there is NO `qt.refresh` for an asking table, the count IS the refresh -- and reading it back with no count is the only way to observe that a set took. EVERYTHING ELSE ANSWERS THROUGH THE MODEL (`qt.cell`, `qt.heading`, `qt.select`, `qt.selected`, the row count), so a program never has to know which kind of table it declared, which is the point of `to ask` being a DIRECTION on `table` rather than a word of its own. A FAILED CALLBACK DRAWS AN EMPTY CELL rather than stopping the program, and that is FORCED rather than chosen: `data()` runs during a PAINT with Qt's own frames between us and anything that could unwind, so there is nowhere to raise to; the raise is reported through the ordinary contained-raise channel instead. THE ORACLE WAS FREE FOR ONCE -- the callback is gBASIC code, so it COUNTS ITS OWN CALLS -- and asserting the claim in the LANGUAGE took one idea beyond the C++ measurement: nothing paints offscreen without a real turn of Qt's loop, so `repaint` answered 0 cells, and the fixture hands the thread to `qt.run()` and takes it back with a timer (the event-loop recipe's pattern). Asserted as a DIFFERENCE across four orders of magnitude AND as the other side of the same claim (far fewer calls than rows), since a model that walked the table would be CORRECT AND UNUSABLE and nothing else in the file would notice. A HANG IS NAMED SEPARATELY FROM A FAILURE because this is the fixture most able to hang in the whole Qt set, handing the thread over twice. SIX PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED. ONE WENT GREEN AND THE FIXTURE WAS AT FAULT, NOT THE IMPLEMENTATION: a model made to prefetch five hundred rows on every `qt.rows` passed EVERY check, because the fixture reset its counter AFTER `qt.rows` so cost incurred while setting the count was invisible -- the boundary is before it now and what is measured is the WHOLE cost of showing N rows. AND A PRE-EXISTING DEFECT FOUND ON THE WAY, not this increment's: `qt.run()` called from inside a FUNCTION silently starves EVERY loop-delivered source, because `watcher_trigger_change` returns at once unless the GLOBAL frame is current -- so a function frame left current for the whole of Qt's loop means every timer tick is appended to `timer.ticks` and NEVER DELIVERED, the timer's own schedule advances so it looks like it is working, and the program HANGS WITH NO OUTPUT. Traced through `pump_arm`, the loop's early return and `timer_loop_service` to that one guard; it reaches timers, the actor inbox, `http` events and a webserver's requests. RECORDED RATHER THAN FIXED, the guard being there because a watcher body must run in the global frame, so the repair is to make the pump establish that frame for the drain -- a change to scope handling and its own increment. AND IT GAINED A PANE CASE THE DAY AFTER, found by a TRIPWIRE RATHER THAN A FAILURE: a splitter has no layout, so every door that asks a parent for one must admit it (§0.19), and the model-backed table was a FOURTH such door that no check in this file could reach -- every table here lived in a `column`. run_qt_split's guard tier reported it ON THE GATE BEFORE §0.25 WAS PUSHED, which is what that tier exists for. MEASURED FIRST: the backend was right all along (an asking table as a direct pane reports `kind=split index=1 of 2` and its cells read back), so what moved was the SCANNER, which could not see an if/else chain whose splitter arm comes first -- and the claim is now asserted BEHAVIOURALLY here as well, with three checks because "it opened" is satisfied by a table that was built and never enrolled: the LAYOUT reports it as a pane, its CELLS still come from the callback, and THE EDITOR BESIDE IT IS STILL THE OTHER PANE, without which a table that had replaced the whole splitter would pass the first two. Proven red by removing the splitter arm, which refuses the window outright. A gBASIC TRAP HIT WRITING IT: the local I bound for the row count was called `g`, which is this fixture's STATE RECORD, so the callback read a field of a number -- the cells came back EMPTY and the program died at the counter, two symptoms of one clobbered name. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about an asking table was measured -- except the grammar and declaration tiers, which read the load-time pass and run anyway.
./tests/run_qt_panes.sh # PANE SIZES -- `qt.pane(...).sizes` and `qt.resize_panes`, which is how "restore my layout" is written (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.22, and §0.19's own open item). TWO REASONS IT WAS OPEN AND BOTH STILL HOLD: `setSizes` takes a QList<int>, so MEASURED, `sizes` is NOT a property (-1) and `setSizes` is NOT invokable by name (-1) -- the by-name route this backend reaches everything else through cannot see either -- and a `split` CANNOT BE NAMED, its one slot being the direction, so there is nothing to look up. SO IT IS ADDRESSED BY WALKING UP FROM A PART, the rule `qt.pane` already follows, which is why it needed NO new spelling: `sizes` joins what that answers and the writer takes the same part. STUDIO NEEDS IT, which is why this came before icons and completion: its shell reads `split.position` for THREE splits out of a saved session and writes them back on exit, so a converted Studio without this FORGETS ITS LAYOUT EVERY TIME -- a feature it already has. A LIST AND NOT A NUMBER, which is a difference from GTK rather than a preference: a Gtk.Paned has exactly two children and one divider where a QSplitter holds any number. THREE MEASUREMENTS AND THE FIRST IS WHY NOTHING HERE ASSERTS AN EXACT SIZE: `setSizes` IS NOT EXACT (asked 260/540 of an 800-wide splitter, got 252/522 -- the handle and the margins), and a WINDOW RESIZE RE-PROPORTIONS (194/580 became 244/730), so what a program stores is a RATIO in effect and every check is a proportion or a difference -- a value check would fail on a correct build. AND A WRONG COUNT FAILS SILENTLY, which is the load-bearing refusal: [100] on a two-pane split gave [774, 0], COLLAPSING the second, and [200,300,400] gave [310,464], honouring NONE of the three -- both perfectly ordinary-looking layouts that are not what was asked for, the class this backend is tested against. Refused naming BOTH numbers, because the author has to know which way they were wrong; a NEGATIVE size is refused rather than clamped, Qt quietly treating it as a collapse. ONE WALK serves the reader and the writer, since two would be two things that can disagree about which split a part is in -- and THE TIER TO READ FIRST is that resizing the INNER split leaves the OUTER alone, because every size check in the file passes on a walk that resized whichever splitter it met first. It stops at the NEAREST REGION like `qt.pane`: a part inside a `scroll` that is itself a pane reports the SCROLL, asserted both ways with the CONTROL that a `table` which IS a direct pane is still found, without which the refusal is satisfied by a walk that refuses everything that is not a plain widget. ZERO IS LEGAL (how a shell hides a panel without forgetting it) and a collapsed pane comes back; THREE PANES are asserted separately, a two-pane-only implementation passing everything else and a Gtk.Paned not even being able to have three. SEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED. A DEFECT OF MINE THE FIXTURE CAUGHT ON ITS FIRST RUN and the presentation is the lesson: the sizes are read AFTER `value_free(which)`, so the name was dangling -- a USE-AFTER-FREE that showed up as `sizes` SILENTLY NEVER BEING SET rather than as a crash, reported by gBASIC as `unknown record field: sizes`. AND A CHECK OF MINE WAS WRONG BEFORE IT RAN: it asserted `console` is in no split, and `console` is a direct pane of the inner one -- it is the positive CONTROL now, and the genuinely no-split case comes from a window that has none. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about pane sizes was measured -- except the grammar and one-walk tiers, which read the source and run anyway.
./tests/run_qt_wheel.sh # THE WHEEL -- `on <part>.wheel`, `qt.wheel`, `qt.swallow`, `qt.scroll`, and THE ZOOM THAT NEEDED NO VERB (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.21). DERIVED FROM gBASIC STUDIO, which uses a Gtk.EventControllerScroll for EXACTLY ONE THING -- Ctrl + wheel zooms the editor -- whose handler reads `dy`, calls one function and returns TRUE to mean handled: a direction, the modifiers, and a way to say handled, and nothing more. FIVE MEASUREMENTS AND THREE SHRANK IT. A SYNTHETIC QWheelEvent IS DELIVERED (a plain one moved the scrollbar 92 -> 95), WHICH IS BETTER THAN GTK: Studio records its scroll and key controllers as its ONLY untested controls precisely because `Gtk.EventControllerScroll` has no emit, so `qt.scroll` CLOSES AN EXCEPTION THAT PROJECT HAD TO SANCTION. A QPlainTextEdit does NOT zoom on Ctrl+wheel (the font stayed 9.00), so the gap is real. AND `font` IS Q_PROPERTY 27 READING BACK AS TEXT ("monospace,9,-1,5,400,..."), so THE ZOOM NEEDS NO VERB -- split the text, change the number, write it back, asserted THROUGH THE INTERPRETER rather than taken from the probe -- which is why there is no `qt.zoom` and is a MEASUREMENT rather than a deferral, `zoomIn`/`zoomOut` carrying a defaulted argument `qt.invoke` could not reach anyway. ONE MORE for §0.20's named list of gaps that measuring found already closed. WHAT IS GENUINELY MISSING IS THE EVENT: a QWidget has no wheel SIGNAL, it RECEIVES a QWheelEvent, so `on code.wheel` is the SECOND thing here that has to be an event rather than a signal (after `closed`) and is answered the same way, before the by-name lookup that would refuse a well-formed handler with "a QPlainTextEdit emits no `wheel` signal". THE FILTER GOES ON THE VIEWPORT, `mouse_target`'s third caller: an event filter sees only what is delivered to the object it sits on, so one on the QPlainTextEdit itself would NEVER FIRE -- and it is installed ONLY WHERE A HANDLER WAS DECLARED, since one per window or one on QApplication would see every wheel event in the program and a filter that claims is a filter that costs a `source` or `table` its ordinary scrolling (asserted twice: a window with no handler claims nothing, AND an undeclared part in a window that HAS one claims nothing, which is the only check that catches a per-window filter). `qt.swallow` IS NOT OPTIONAL and that is measured: Ctrl+wheel scrolls a QPlainTextEdit FASTER than a plain one (95 -> 111 against 92 -> 95), so a handler that zooms without claiming leaves the editor jumping about while it resizes; swallowing stops it (102 -> 102) and declining leaves plain scrolling intact (102 -> 105). `dy` IS IN NOTCHES, not Qt's 120-unit angle delta -- one click is what a person did, and a program dividing by 120 would carry a Qt-ism nothing else here exposes -- and A SUB-NOTCH TURN ROUNDS AWAY FROM ZERO, because a trackpad sends deltas under 120 and integer division would report the wheel moved by NOTHING, in the one value whose SIGN is the whole of what a zoom reads; positive is away from the user, Qt's sign and the OPPOSITE of GTK's, so Studio's `if dy < 0 then zoom in` INVERTS when ported. TWO PERTURBATIONS WENT GREEN AND BOTH ADDED SOMETHING, which is the part to read. `qt.scroll` answers whether anything CLAIMED the event (the `qt.popup` shape) and A FILTER MADE TO RECORD THE CLAIM AND THEN RETURN FALSE -- so the claim is reported while the view scrolls anyway -- WAS CAUGHT BY NOTHING: that answer agrees with `qt.swallow` having been called whatever Qt then did, so the whole of what swallowing does was asserted against the program's own word for it. AGREEMENT IS NOT CORRECTNESS (PLAT-NUMFMT) arriving inside my own increment. The only honest observable is the POSITION, which a program could not ask for -- a QAbstractScrollArea reaches its scrollbar by a METHOD, so the by-name property route cannot see one -- hence `qt.scrolled` and `qt.scroll_to`, BOTH DIRECTIONS because a reader without a writer is the shape webclient's read and write halves had, and NOT a test hook either since reopening an editor where somebody left it is what Studio already does for its panes; it answers `unknown` for a part that does not scroll, a different claim from "scrolled to nowhere". The second green perturbation was the SUB-NOTCH ROUNDING, undrivable while `qt.scroll` took whole notches -- it takes a FRACTION now and a quarter-notch is asserted in both directions. ELEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED. AND AN ASSERTION OF MINE THAT BROKE THE FIRST TIME A CHECK WAS ADDED is now a DIFFERENCE rather than a literal: the zoom counter moves with every Ctrl+wheel above it, so a number written there was a transcript. A RESERVED WORD CAUGHT BY THE GATE RATHER THAN BY ME: the fixture used `step` as a variable, which `for i = 1 to 10 step 2` claims, and run_qt_builder's AGREEMENT tier -- which lifts every window block in the tree mechanically -- found the parse error in a fixture nobody had added to anything, the glob-discovery argument working on an unbuilt file. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about the wheel was measured -- except the grammar, no-zoom-verb and declaration tiers, which read the source or the load-time pass and run anyway.
./tests/run_qt_context.sh # A CONTEXT MENU -- `popup`, `qt.popup`, `qt.right_click`, and a Qt DEFAULT THAT SILENCES A HANDLER (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.20). DERIVED FROM gBASIC STUDIO'S OWN SOURCE like `split` before it, and reading it SHRANK THE INCREMENT TWICE: Studio's browser context menu is built ONCE holding every item and HIDES the ones a row does not offer, and `visible` is Q_PROPERTY 14 of QAction so `qt.set` already reached it -- no new capability; and its `context_actions(rows, index)` takes an INDEX, so the one genuine gap looked like "which row was right-clicked" and MEASURED, Qt MOVES THE CURRENT ROW ON A RIGHT-PRESS BY ITSELF, so `qt.selected` already answers it. ANOTHER GAP MEASURING FOUND ALREADY CLOSED, and the instances are NAMED rather than counted in §0.20 because the earlier sections' ordinals do not agree with each other -- the count depends on whether "measuring changed the design" belongs in the same series as "the gap was already closed", so naming is checkable where a number is not. THE WORD EXISTS BECAUSE THE SLOT DOES: §0.8 measured that a container carries a NAME OR OPTIONS AND NEVER BOTH, `menu "File"` spends its one slot on the caption a person reads on the bar, and a context menu has NO BAR TO BE READ ON -- so what it needs is a NAME, `popup row_actions`, at ZERO grammar cost (the production `tabs settings` already uses, and a submenu inside one is that production recursing exactly as `menu "Recent"` inside `menu "File"` already was). Both halves refused, so neither word can be written the other's way. ITS PLACEMENT IS THE BAR'S RULE REACHED BY A DIFFERENT ROUTE rather than by analogy: measured, a QMenu parented to a widget that HAS a layout is NOT taken by it (count 2 before, 2 after), so a `popup` inside a `row` is a declaration the layout SILENTLY IGNORES. THE TIER TO READ FIRST IS POLICY, the silent class this backend is tested against arriving from a QT DEFAULT: every widget kind starts at `DefaultContextMenu` and under that policy `customContextMenuRequested` IS NEVER EMITTED, so a handler declared for it without a second line setting the policy is CONNECTED TO A SIGNAL NOTHING EMITS -- the right-click does nothing and nothing is raised.
