So DECLARING THE HANDLER IS THE REQUEST, the author having already said what they want by naming it; THE COST IS STATED rather than hidden, since this REPLACES the menu Qt would have put up itself, so a `memo` or `field` with a context handler loses its own cut/copy/paste -- which is why the policy is touched only where a handler EXISTS, asserted as a DIFFERENCE with the CONTROL that a part with NONE keeps Qt's, without which "the policy is set" is satisfied by setting it on every widget in the language (that over-correction is caught by the control AND BY NOTHING ELSE). `qt.right_click` IS THE ORACLE AND WAS BUILT BEFORE THE FIXTURE, the discipline §0.8 arrived at by getting it wrong: a `table`, `memo` or `source` does NOT answer a context-menu event sent to the widget -- delivered to the QTableWidget it fired 0 times and the event propagated to the PARENT, delivered to that widget's VIEWPORT it fired 1 -- and a real right-click lands on the viewport, so the feature works for a person on all five kinds while gBASIC cannot NAME a viewport, leaving the path drivable only on `field` and `button`, the two kinds that happen to have none. It sends a PRESS AND A RELEASE and then the context event, in the order a mouse produces them, because the selection is part of the answer: a bare event would fire the handler over a STALE SELECTION, a verb that drives half of what a mouse does. `qt.popup` ANSWERS WHETHER ANYTHING WAS SHOWN rather than refusing -- Studio's own comment says an empty context menu "reads as a control that failed", and measured Qt agrees (an all-hidden menu is `isEmpty()` and `popup()` leaves it INVISIBLE), so a row that offers nothing is an ORDINARY OUTCOME and a boolean is the truthful report; both halves asserted, since `true` alone passes on a verb that always says yes. AND THE COUNT RECURSES, WHICH examples/qt/browser.bas FOUND RATHER THAN ANY REASONING: hiding every item INSIDE a submenu does not hide the submenu, and measured Qt then reports `isEmpty()` FALSE and SHOWS a 120x25 menu holding NOTHING BUT AN ARROW LEADING NOWHERE -- "reads as a control that failed" one level in, which Qt's own test cannot see (it declines only once the submenu's own action is hidden too). A submenu counts for WHAT IT LEADS TO, told by `menu()` being non-null against a plain item's nil; asserted BOTH ways, because a count that stopped treating a submenu as anything at all satisfies the first half. `qt.menus(win, "name")` IS THE ORACLE A FLAT REGISTRY ALWAYS NEEDS, taking TEXT where a bar position is a NUMBER (a popup is not on the bar, so it HAS no position): an `item` keeps its name wherever its menu ends up, so one built into the wrong popup of two passes EVERY by-name check there is -- the same measurement the submenu oracle rests on, where that perturbation produced ZERO MISMATCHES -- and it also says a popup has NOT quietly been added to the bar, since `gb_qt_menu_count` cannot see one. ONE ENTRY WALK serves a bar menu, a submenu and a popup, tripwired with its vacuity control counting OCCURRENCES RATHER THAN LINES, which is how the equivalent control in run_qt_modal.sh was found unable to fail. ELEVEN PERTURBATIONS PROVEN RED. TWO DEFECTS OF MY OWN: the fixture's first draft counted into PLAIN SCALARS in `main` and every counter read 0 while the handlers had plainly run (the selection had moved, the item visibility had changed) -- warning 2104 and §0.12's own rule, that a handler cannot rebind an outer scalar, which is exactly what the cookbook's recipe 3 exists to teach and I walked into anyway; and RESTORING A PERTURBATION DESTROYED AN HOUR OF UNCOMMITTED WORK, because `git checkout -- .` is safe only when EVERYTHING ELSE in the tree is committed too -- the increment was and the docs, the submenu fix and a backtick fix were not. The discipline is `git checkout -- <the one perturbed file>`, or commit before each round. ALSO the BACKTICK TRAP for the THIRTEENTH time in this tree, in this suite's own `fail` message, and the tripwire added at the eleventh CAUGHT IT BY NAME -- the guard worked and the author did not, for the second time. PLUS `under` SINCE THE SAME DAY: `qt.popup(win, menu, part)` pops BENEATH a part rather than at the pointer, which closes §0.20's own open item and whose caller is gBASIC Studio's TOOLBAR MENU BUTTON -- a menu button's menu belongs under the button, not wherever the mouse happens to be (measured, a widget's bottom-left lands exactly there). AND THE FIFTH GREEN PERTURBATION OF THE DAY PRODUCED ITS ORACLE: ignoring the third argument and popping at the pointer left `qt.popup`'s boolean TRUE and was caught by NOTHING, the boolean saying only that a menu appeared -- and a menu's own `x` is in a DIFFERENT COORDINATE SPACE from a button's, so comparing them would compare two unrelated numbers. What is honest is a DIFFERENCE: popping under one button and under another puts it in different places, with the pointer as a THIRD. A name that is not there is refused, because popping at the pointer instead would look like a bug in the program rather than a name that was wrong. AND THE EXAMPLE FOUND A USAGE NOTE NO CHECK WOULD HAVE: a popped menu STAYS UP and a visible QMenu GRABS THE MOUSE, so adding the menu button to examples/qt/shell.bas broke the Ctrl+wheel check TWO TIERS BELOW IT with the menu still open and swallowing the wheel -- a person clicks or presses Escape, a program calls `qt.invoke(win, menu, "hide")`, and auto-dismissing was rejected outright since a menu that vanished the instant it appeared would be no menu at all. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about a context menu was measured -- except the grammar, one-walk and declaration tiers, which read the source or the load-time pass and run anyway.
./tests/run_qt_dialog.sh # DIALOGS -- `qt.inform`, `qt.confirm`, `qt.answer` (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.15). A program could not ask a question, and the irony was sharp: §5.1 chose direction A BECAUSE modal dialogs starve the other arrangement (a 651ms gap over a 600ms dialog, which is a webserver answering nothing) while nothing could open one. MEASURED BEFORE DESIGNING, which decided the shape: a C++ probe in gBASIC's exact arrangement -- a QTimer inside Qt's loop, which is what `gb_qt_run` uses -- shows the tick firing DURING a modal QMessageBox (3 of 3) and ABLE TO PRESS ITS BUTTON, so a program can answer its own dialog and a suite needs no human (a QFileDialog behaves the same once DontUseNativeDialog asks for Qt's own rather than the desktop's). AND THE CLAIM IS NARROWER THAN §5.1 READS, which is this suite's reason for existing: §5.1 is about QT's loop surviving the nested one, and what cannot happen is gBASIC BEING SERVICED WHILE A WATCHER BODY IS ON THE STACK -- a dialog raised from `main` leaves the sources running (a `timer` fires and can answer the box), one raised INSIDE A WATCHER BODY suspends them until it is answered. THE SECOND IS THE NORMAL CASE AND IS WIDER THAN IT LOOKS, a `timer` body, a webserver handler, an actor mailbox watcher, an `http` event watcher and a button clicked from any of them all running inside one, so a modal dialog is modal to the whole PROGRAM and not only to its window -- defensible for a desktop application, surprising for one that is also a server. THAT ROW USED TO SAY "FROM A HANDLER" AND NAMED THE WRONG CAUSE, corrected 2026-10-18 by the `stall` tier and its fixture, WHICH HAS NO HANDLER IN IT AT ALL and stalls identically: TWO INDEPENDENT GUARDS HOLD IT, EACH SUFFICIENT ALONE -- Qt will not re-deliver the ONE reference-counted QTimer gBASIC's sources ride while its handler is on the stack (0 pump ticks against 3 for a fresh timer, measured in C++), and `watcher_drain` returns at once while a watcher body is running, so the loop polls, finds work and declines to dispatch it. REMOVING EITHER CHANGES NOTHING, which is what made this worth measuring rather than reasoning: given a timer of its own the pump reached gBASIC's loop 397 TIMES during one dialog and the stall did not move, and with gBASIC's guard alone removed the pump still never ticked; WITH BOTH OFF IT DISSOLVES. SO THE FIX IS NOT A TIMER CHANGE, which is the half worth recording since a timer change is what anybody reaches for first -- it needs watcher dispatch to become RE-ENTRANT, and that guard is deliberate and right, a watcher body re-entered mid-statement being the hazard rather than the remedy; a language decision, not this backend's to take. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER ASSERTS THAT WITH A REAL `server` BLOCK on an OS-assigned port and needs THREE checks: the route answers with no dialog up (the CONTROL, without which "stalled" is satisfied by a server that never worked), it does NOT answer during one, and the dialog is STILL OPEN afterwards so what was measured was a stall and not a crash. IF THAT TIER EVER PASSES THE LIMITATION IS GONE and §0.15, the reference and the cookbook all overstate it, which is run_limitations.sh's negative-control shape -- so it fails with that sentence rather than a bare mismatch. TWO DEFECTS FOUND BY RUNNING IT: the pump lived INSIDE `gb_qt_run`, so a dialog opened before the program handed over the thread had NO pump at all -- `exec()` opened a nested loop with nothing servicing gBASIC, and the first `qt.confirm` written outside `qt.run()` HUNG until a 30-second timeout -- it is reference-counted now and a dialog arms it too, so the promise holds wherever the box was opened from rather than only in the arrangement the pump happened to be written for; and the no-display refusal said "window `app` is not open", TRUE and the WRONG CAUSE (the window is shut because there is nowhere to put it), the class this tree has recorded five times, so it names the display now WITH THE CONTROL that an unopened window still gets the other sentence, or naming the display would just be what this verb always says. DECISIONS: `confirm` answers a BOOLEAN rather than a button code, two buttons being the whole of what it offers and anything richer a window shown modally; NO IS THE DEFAULT BUTTON, because a question answered by pressing Return without reading should not be the destructive one; and `qt.answer` REFUSES when nothing is open rather than being a silent no-op, which would leave a program waiting for somebody who is not there -- a HANG and not a failure. A QMessageBox is heap-allocated behind a QPointer, because a stack-allocated QObject GIVEN A PARENT is a double free and the parent is wanted so Qt centres the box on it and makes it modal to that window. Three perturbations proven red, each caught by the tier written for it (confirm always answering yes; `answer` made a silent no-op; the no-display refusal naming the wrong cause). AND THIS SUITE LEAKED A gBASIC PROCESS PER RUN, found 2026-10-19 by sweeping the machine rather than by any check: SIXTEEN orphaned `./gbasic tests/qt_dialog_modal_fixture.bas` processes, one per run since the suite shipped the previous morning, each holding a modal dialog for ever. TWO CAUSES AND BOTH ARE GENERAL. (1) `timeout -k 5 40 ./gbasic ... &` makes `$!` THE `timeout` PROCESS, so `kill -9 "$fixture"` killed the bound and ORPHANED its child -- and with `timeout` dead nothing was left to escalate to it. (2) The EXIT trap's `pkill -f` sends SIGTERM, and THIS INTERPRETER INSTALLS A SIGTERM HANDLER FOR POOL DRAIN while the fixture is BLOCKED INSIDE A NESTED QT LOOP, so it records the signal and never acts on it -- measured, a leftover survived SIGTERM and died only on SIGKILL, which is the same reason every bound in this tree is `timeout -k`. `pkill -KILL` in both places now, AND THE ABSENCE IS ASSERTED, because the leak is invisible from inside: the tier passed, the suite passed, and docs/reference.md's bold promise that nothing this interpreter starts outlives it was being broken by the SUITE rather than by the interpreter -- run_process_lifetime.sh guards gBASIC's own CHILDREN and structurally cannot see a harness leaking the interpreter itself. Proven red by reverting both kills. NOT ASSERTED AND SAID SO: a dialog opened OVER another -- the backend keeps a stack so one is not refused, but nothing exercises it, and a claim nobody has run is not a claim. Skips cleanly without Qt, saying NOTHING about dialogs was measured; the modal-extent tier skips without curl, saying how far a dialog reaches was not measured. ALSO: the backtick trap for the SIXTH time in this tree while writing the commit message -- `git commit -m` with a double-quoted string lets the SHELL run anything in backticks, so it ran `server` and `qt.confirm` as commands and git read the fragments as pathspecs; the heredoc form every other commit here uses has no such hole.
./tests/run_qt_examples.sh # examples/qt -- RUN, NOT MERELY PARSED. `run_gui_parse.sh` parses every program in that directory on every gate run, which catches a syntax error and NOTHING ELSE; behaviour was gated only by the FIXTURES in tests/, written by the hands that wrote the backend -- so an example can rot while every fixture passes, and one had: examples/qt/README.md listed ONE OF FOUR examples for a day, because the parse walk globs the directory and the page describing it was checked by nothing. AND THE EXAMPLES ARE WHERE THE GAPS CAME FROM, which is the stronger argument: setting out to write one per aspect of this backend is what found that the leaf vocabulary constructed QLineEdit, QPushButton and QCheckBox AND NO MORE -- so a heading, an instruction or a description had NOWHERE TO GO, and a GUI toolkit that cannot display a sentence is not fully functional. Nothing in the tree said so, because every fixture happened to need only an entry, a box and a button; §0.10 of docs/qt_gui_design.md records it and `text`/`memo` close it. EVERY EXAMPLE TAKES `check`, which is not a test seam bolted on: it is how a person verifies a Qt install WITHOUT LOOKING AT A SCREEN, and it is the only way a program that hands the thread to `qt.run()` can be gated at all. TIERS: ARGS asserts every example accepts it STRUCTURALLY, because one that does not would HANG rather than fail and a hang is not a failure (run_string_nul.sh's recorded trap); RUN runs each offscreen in check mode requiring exit 0, OUTPUT (a check run that prints nothing is indistinguishable from one that did nothing, which is the whole reason the mode exists) and CLEAN STDERR; DEGRADE runs each with NO DISPLAY AT ALL and requires a sentence and exit 0 -- §0.3's claim made by EVERY example rather than by one fixture, since with no display Qt calls qFatal and ABORTS uncatchably and a crash takes block-buffered stdout with it, so a program printing its progress prints NOTHING and the failure reads as line 1; COVER names every example in the README and refuses a row naming a file that is gone, MOVED here from run_qt_builder.sh because this suite owns the directory. THE THIRTEEN EXAMPLES ARE ONE PER ASPECT AND FOUR COMBINED (the count moves with the glob, so a stale number here is the kind of rot run_docs_gate exists for -- `dialogs` and `shell` joined 2026-10-19, the first being the one you can sit in front of and CLICK since everything dialog-shaped is otherwise timer-driven and answers itself in fifty milliseconds, and the second being the IDE layout `split` and `scroll` were built for): layout (the four containers with no data at all, which also prints the HONEST LIMIT that only a grid has coordinates for `qt.at`, and shows a labelled `field` taking TWO cells -- its label and itself), tabs (a page is a COLUMN, which is why a grid and a whole table work inside one; `movable:` set with `qt.set` because a container carries a NAME OR OPTIONS, not both), properties (three doors that must agree what a name is -- the declaration, `qt.read`, `qt.set` -- each refusing a misspelling BY NAME, because `setProperty` on an unknown name SUCCEEDS in Qt and would silently do nothing; and an ENUM answers its NAME, `echoMode: 2` reading back as "Password"), theme (the half that NEEDS NO DISPLAY, a sheet being remembered until there is a QApplication to put it on), clock (§5.1 as something you can WATCH: a `timer.every` still firing after `qt.run()` has the thread, teaching two things learned by getting them wrong -- the `watch` goes INSIDE `program` since at the top level beside one it REGISTERS NOTHING, and `qt.stop()` DOES NOT CANCEL YOUR TIMERS, so leaving a repeating one live hands the thread to gBASIC's own post-main loop and the program NEVER EXITS, measured at 197 further ticks before a timeout killed it), then customer (binding), orders (a table feeding a form), notes (menus + theme + table + form) and invoice (the builder). A FLOOR ON THE GLOB, because every tier loops over the list and a glob that matched nothing would report a clean sweep of nothing. Skips nothing; the RUN and DEGRADE tiers need Qt in the build, and each example says what is missing rather than failing when it is absent.
./tests/run_qt_source.sh # THE `source` WIDGET -- a code editor in the `window` vocabulary (docs/qt_gui_design.md §0.14), and the single biggest piece gBASIC Studio needs from this backend. WHY IT IS A WORD RATHER THAN A PROPERTY, which is what made it an increment at all: everything else a widget carries goes through `QMetaObject` by name, and that is what keeps this backend small -- eight construction words against the 75 properties QPushButton alone exposes -- but A HIGHLIGHTER IS NOT A PROPERTY, it is an object attached to a document, so `indexOfProperty` has nothing to find however it is spelled; the same is true of the QCompleter Studio wants next, which is why the shape here is "attach a thing, then ask what was attached". A `source` IS A `memo` WITH FOUR DIFFERENCES and each is asserted AGAINST A `memo` IN THE SAME WINDOW rather than as a value, since every value check also passes on a backend that built a plain memo and called it a source: it does NOT wrap (a wrapped line of code makes a line number stop matching the file -- the OPPOSITE default from `text`, where wrapping is what stops a paragraph truncating), its tab is four columns rather than eight, its font is the system's monospaced one (ASKED FOR rather than named, a hardcoded family being how a port acquires a fallback nobody chose), and it carries a highlighter. THE DEFECT THIS SUITE EXISTS FOR IS INVISIBLE RATHER THAN PLAUSIBLE, which is unusual even here: an ABSENT definition still applies the theme's normal-text format to EVERY LINE, measured at 3 format ranges against 8, so an editor highlighting NOTHING is not blank, not plain and not obviously wrong -- it is an ordinary editor with ordinary text in it, every assertion of the shape "formats were applied" passes on it, and a golden would record it as expected. THE TIER TO READ FIRST IS `definition source`, because it is the one that would have caught the trap this increment nearly shipped with: the probe that DESIGNED the widget reported `gBASIC` as FOUND with no search path set at all, which read as the path plumbing being unnecessary, and it was A COPY HAND-INSTALLED INTO ~/.local/share/org.kde.syntax-highlighting IN JUNE for Kate -- a fresh machine finds nothing, highlights nothing and looks fine, which is the libcurl floor / mawk / GnuTLS shape for the FOURTH time, invisible exactly where it was written. So the definition comes from a path THE BUILD knows (GBASIC_SYNTAX_PATH for the source tree, a baked-in $(DATADIR)/syntax-highlighting otherwise, mirroring GBASIC_PATH and GBASIC_DEFAULT_STDLIB one line up in the Makefile, with `make` deriving the engine's `<dir>/syntax/` layout from the ONE definition the repo ships so there are never two copies to drift), and the tier runs with XDG_DATA_HOME MOVED ASIDE and requires the file to be OURS. IT CAUGHT A SECOND INSTANCE WITHIN THE HOUR: run_qt_examples.sh ran `editor.bas` with no path set and reported clean stderr ON THIS MACHINE ONLY -- pointed at an empty XDG_DATA_HOME it fails with `editor.bas wrote to stderr` -- and that suite exports the path now. "IT HIGHLIGHTS" IS THEREFORE TWO CLAIMS IN TWO PLACES and neither is sufficient alone: WHICH DEFINITION WAS APPLIED is asked through `qt.highlighting`, answering { language, file } or `unknown`, and it needs BOTH halves because a definition the user installed in their own home SHADOWS ours and reports the IDENTICAL name (measured, 7 ranges against 8) -- that precedence is left alone deliberately, being the rule `load` already follows for a library beside the program, while being unable to SEE it would not be acceptable; and WHETHER THE TEXT IS REALLY COLOURED gBASIC cannot ask, having no way to read a QTextLayout's format ranges, so tests/qt/source_probe.cpp does it in C++ with the control IN THE SAME PROCESS (15 ranges against 5, WHILE BOTH FORMAT EVERY ONE OF THE FIVE LINES, which is the measurement that makes a presence check vacuous) rather than across two runs whose environments would have to be argued identical. LOOKUP IS BY NAME AND THAT IS A RECORDED DECISION: `*.bas` is claimed by FreeBASIC at the SAME priority, extension lookup picks FreeBASIC with no error, and editors/README.md argues deliberately against shipping a priority since a higher one wins for EVERY `.bas` file on the machine including somebody's FreeBASIC -- so by-name is the only route left, and the fixture asks for FreeBASIC and requires FreeBASIC beside gBASIC giving gBASIC, the pair without which every other check passes on extension lookup. AND A SECOND TRAP OF THE SAME FAMILY WAS FOUND BY ASKING WHAT A RELEASE ARTIFACT IS rather than by a failing test: the baked-in path alone would have shipped A DOWNLOAD WHOSE EDITOR HIGHLIGHTED NOTHING, because tools/build-release-tarball.sh says the tree is RELOCATABLE BY CONSTRUCTION and runs where it is unpacked -- which works for the stdlib only because that is resolved RELATIVE TO THE BINARY, so a tarball would have looked in /usr/local/share/gbasic/syntax-highlighting, a directory that is not there, leaving THE FILE IN THE ARCHIVE AND UNREACHABLE BY THE BINARY BESIDE IT -- the shape reported from outside this project three times and the reason run_library_tiers.sh exists. MEASURED BOTH WAYS by installing to a scratch DESTDIR and MOVING the tree somewhere no build knew about with the environment stripped (no GBASIC_SYNTAX_PATH, empty XDG_DATA_HOME): `gBASIC` from its own path with the resolver, `applied=none` and warning 2115 without it. The prefix walk is SHARED with the stdlib's (`gb_exe_relative_share(subdir)`) rather than copied, two representations of one layout being the drift this tree tripwires against, and the `relocated` tier keeps it -- proven red. AN ABSENT DEFINITION WARNS (2115) RATHER THAN RAISING, which is what makes the feature affordable -- the editor is built, editable and useful without highlighting, so stopping the program is a worse answer than saying so, the position `odbc.connect` takes on a driver needing ClientCharset and for the identical reason that the failure is SILENT -- and the message names the PART because it cannot name a POSITION, a window record holding no line, so it is reported at `qt.open`; its control is that a run which FOUND its definition is silent. VALGRIND OBJECTED AND IT IS PCRE2's JIT, isolated the way run_odbc.sh isolates a driver defect by reproducing it with NO gBASIC IN THE STACK and then narrowed by measurement: a QPlainTextEdit with no highlighter 0 objections, gBASIC's pre-existing `memo` fixture 0, a highlighted document 3, the same with QT_ENABLE_REGEXP_JIT=0 back to 0 -- the JIT compiles to ANONYMOUS executable memory, which is why valgrind can name no object (`obj:*`, `???`) and its own glibc suppressions do not match, and its matcher over-reads the subject in 16-byte steps BY DESIGN, which is why PCRE2 ships an --enable-valgrind build of its own. THE TIER TURNS THE JIT OFF RATHER THAN SUPPRESSING, on tests/odbc.supp's own argument: the only suppression available would be "anything in an object we cannot name", which would also hide uninitialised data WE passed in, where that file's whole discipline is narrow per-driver entries -- and THE PREMISE IS ASSERTED, the fixture having to produce the same checks either way, so a setting that changed the program would fail rather than pass quietly. A CORRECTION TO THAT PREMISE TIER FOUND BY PERTURBING: written as "the counts agree AND the no-JIT run is clean", a BROKEN FIXTURE failed here too, saying "with the regex JIT off the fixture behaves differently" when both runs behaved identically badly -- the semantics tier had already named the real defect and this one blamed a setting with nothing to do with it, the reports-the-wrong-cause class again; it compares BOTH counts now. Seven perturbations proven red, each caught by the tier written for it -- the search path never registered is caught by `definition source` AND NOTHING ELSE, which is what says that tier catches the trap, and lookup-by-extension reddens seven checks. AND ONE WENT GREEN FIRST AND MEANT NOTHING: the perturbation removing `source` from `qt_bindable_word` did not APPLY, my pattern having assumed `strcmp(word, "choice")` began a line when it follows "memo" on the same one -- a no-op edit reporting green, found by checking the match rather than trusting the colour, and the redone one reddens three. THE GATE CAUGHT A BLIND SPOT IN ITSELF, which is the best thing in the increment: warning 2115 is the FIRST raised from a module file, and run_docs_gate's warning-code check read src/eval.c LITERALLY -- so it reported a live warning as a GHOST; it follows eval.c's own `#include "modules/*.c"` lines now, DERIVED rather than listed, because a second list of modules is one a new module silently stays off. ALSO the backtick trap for the fifth time in this tree, in this suite's own pass message (`make`'s inside a double-quoted string, which the shell RAN). CARRIES THE CARET SINCE 2026-10-20 (§0.32), asked for by the gBASIC Studio session with the use that justifies it -- *"stopped at line 7 -- put the caret there to see why"*, their own summary being that it is "the difference between an editor you can look at and one you can use". IT NEEDS A VERB AND THAT WAS MEASURED, which is the question §0.23 and §0.29 both had to ask since this backend's economy is that construction is a table and everything else is by name: `textCursor` is NOT a Q_PROPERTY and `textCursor()` is NOT invokable by name, both -1, because it answers a QTextCursor where the by-name route moves QVariants -- so the caret joins the highlighter and the margin as genuinely unreachable rather than as something `qt.read` already does and nobody noticed. 1-BASED, WHICH IS §0.29's RULE RATHER THAN A FRESH CHOICE: Qt's `blockNumber` and `positionInBlock` are both 0-based while gBASIC's diagnostics, `source_outline` and a mark list are all 1-based, so THE +1 LIVES IN ONE PLACE and a `line:column` out of any of them goes straight into `qt.caret_to` with no arithmetic at the call site. THE LINE REFUSES AND THE COLUMN CLAMPS, which is not symmetry but two different facts: a LINE past the end means the caller's model of the document is wrong and clamping would put the caret on a different line while LOOKING like it worked (the silent class this backend is tested against), so it is refused with the document's real length in the message, where a COLUMN past the end of its own line is ordinary -- a diagnostic pointing just past the last character, or a line since shortened -- and clamps to where End would land. AND THE CLAMP IS OURS, WHICH THE FIXTURE CORRECTED RATHER THAN CONFIRMED: the first comment said Qt stops at the line end by itself and MEASURED `movePosition(Right, n)` WALKS ACROSS BLOCK BOUNDARIES, so column 999 of line 2 landed on LINE 7 of a seven-line document -- a plausible position in the wrong place, and the second comment of mine that week a measurement contradicted after `qobject_cast` in §0.29. `selecting` is reported because a caret inside a selection is ONE END OF A RANGE and a bare line and column would be an ambiguous reading (the `principal()`/`qt.dialog` discipline), while the ANCHOR is deliberately not: nobody has asked for it and a verb answering five numbers when two were wanted is a surface to regret. A part that is not a text editor answers UNKNOWN rather than refusing, which composes with `is_unknown` -- and a name that is NOT THERE AT ALL still raises, a distinction that corrected a check of mine whose first version asked about an absent name (a mistake rather than an answer) and whose `or true` made it vacuous besides. AND §0.31's DISCIPLINE IS APPLIED RATHER THAN REDISCOVERED: measured, `setTextCursor` emits `cursorPositionChanged` ONCE, so a handler may run inside `qt.caret_to` and may have reconciled that very editor away -- the function ends on the call and touches nothing afterwards, which is the cheapest form of that rule, one increment old and already paying. Four perturbations proven red: the read made 0-based, the write made 0-based, the column clamp removed, the past-the-end refusal removed. DELIBERATELY NOT HERE and said so: a LINE-NUMBER GUTTER, which Qt has no widget for (a painted side area, a separate increment, and Studio needs it), and COMPLETION, whose data already exists in `source_outline` and src/lsp/ while the popup is a QCompleter -- another object attached to a widget, so it lands exactly where the highlighter did and for the same reason. Skips cleanly without Qt in the build, saying NOTHING about the editor was measured; the colour tier skips without a C++ compiler or the KSyntaxHighlighting headers, saying colouring was not measured.
./tests/run_qt_builder.sh # THE BUILDER (docs/qt_gui_design.md §4) -- a window a program BUILDS, and `qt.check`, the declaration's own rules over a record. §4 called for a second door, the block for fixed layouts and a builder for data-driven lists, because a declaration cannot contain a loop and so cannot generate a row per invoice; it said "the builder's surface is not designed here". MEASURED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS DESIGNED, THE DOOR WAS ALREADY OPEN AND NOBODY KNEW: `qt.open` reads the record a declaration binds and asks only that `kind` is "window", so a record a program builds OPENS, RENDERS, READS BACK AND FIRES HANDLERS exactly as a declared one -- the surface IS the record, the `web.routes` shape §4 itself named, and no verb was needed. Third time in this design that measuring a gap before filling it changed what got built (menus needed no grammar; a named container with options cost four reduce/reduce). WHAT IT LACKED WAS EVERY LOAD-TIME CHECK, which is not a missing nicety but the defect `web.configure` exists to close -- there the record could already be written directly and that path validated NOTHING, so `app.options.workesr = 4` was accepted in silence. MEASURED ON THE BINARY FIRST: a part with NO NAME opened quietly and was unaddressable forever; TWO PARTS SHARING A NAME opened quietly with the second shadowing the first; `word: "feild"` was refused by the BACKEND for having no widget wired, never "did you mean 'field'?"; `item` in a `column` got the right refusal with the wrong sentence; and a handler naming nothing was refused for carrying no function, NEVER "there is nothing called `nobody` in this window" -- which is the refusal load-time validation exists for, so a builder without it is a builder whose mistakes surface as a window that looks fine. ONE CHECKER, TWO ADAPTERS, and that is the load-bearing structural decision: the rules are ~350 lines and a record walker would be a SECOND COPY, the drift this tree tripwires against and which THIS VERY VOCABULARY had already suffered (written out three times before `win_nearest` was derived from the tables, with the newest word missing from the third). They live in src/window_check.c against an ACCESSOR MODEL -- src/frontend.c adapts the AST, src/modules/qt.c adapts a record, neither holds a rule -- which is `would`/`execute`'s arrangement in `automation` for the reason stated there: if the two could disagree, a check would describe a DIFFERENT WINDOW from the one that opens. `qt.check` NEEDS NO QT AND NO DISPLAY, being a check on a DECLARATION, so it sits OUTSIDE the `#if HAVE_QT` guard -- this project's own convention read the other way, that code which is NOT optional must stay out of those guards -- and `qt.open` RUNS IT TOO, because a validator nobody is obliged to call is necessary and not sufficient (`rules.validate` refused a malformed stack all along while `rules.evaluate` still answered `ok: true` for a string). THE RECORD'S FIELD NAMES ARE CLOSED as well, a question about the REPRESENTATION rather than about windows, so `nmae:` is refused by name and an item with no `kind:` is told the three it may have; reported with a PATH (`column[0] > field a`) since a record has no line. TWO DEFECTS OF MINE, BOTH FOUND BY RUNNING IT: the child wrapper was a RING OF SIXTEEN under a comment claiming the checker "holds at most a parent and a child at any moment" -- FALSE, and the sixteen was the giveaway, since at the SEVENTEENTH SIBLING the ring recycled the PARENT'S slot while the walk was still iterating it, segfaulting at nineteen generated parts (which the fixture had, §2.4's rejected keypad being nineteen buttons) and taking block-buffered stdout with it so the run printed everything up to the tier and nothing after, reading exactly like a hang -- the same shape §0.3 describes for a display-less Qt abort, arriving from my own code. ONE SLOT PER DEPTH now, and that exposed the second: a DECLARED window's nesting is bounded by its source and a BUILT one by NOTHING, and the walk recurses -- MEASURED, with the cap lifted it survives 5,000 and segfaults at 10,000, while building the same 10,000-deep record and NOT checking it exits 0, which is the CONTROL that makes it a fact about this walk rather than about gBASIC (a nested value around 22,000 deep separately kills the interpreter at teardown). Capped at 64, some 80x short of the crash, WITH THE MARGIN RECORDED rather than the cap merely asserted. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS PARITY: the same window written BOTH WAYS, every observable required to agree INCLUDING the grid geometry `qt.at` reads back from the layout, because "the builder works" is otherwise satisfied by a builder that renders something PLAUSIBLE, which is this backend's whole failure class. AND PARITY ALONE IS NOT ENOUGH AND THE FIXTURE SAYS SO -- perturbing `gb_qt_span` to ignore spans breaks BOTH halves equally, so all six parity checks stay GREEN and only the companion assertion ("the span really is 2") catches it, which is agreement-is-not-correctness (PLAT-NUMFMT) one level up. Beside it, AGREEMENT puts every one of the 23 `window` declarations in the tree through the RECORD adapter, LIFTED MECHANICALLY rather than listed: a declaration passed the AST adapter at load time, so a refusal there means the two have drifted, and it is the only tier that can see that. AND ITS ONE-COPY TIER WAS PASSING BY ACCIDENT OF A LINE BREAK, found 2026-10-20 when §0.29's `qt.mark` category near-miss turned it red: MEASURED FIRST, the scanner was wrong rather than the code -- `src/modules/qt.c` HAD HELD a near-miss sentence since §0.16 (the chooser's option names) and the check passed anyway, because that one is written `"did "` + `"you mean"` ACROSS A LINE BREAK and a literal grep cannot see it, so it would have gone on passing however many copies arrived in that shape. AND THE TWO SENTENCES ARE ABOUT DIFFERENT THINGS, which is why there are legitimately two: window_check.c owns near-misses about the DECLARATION's vocabulary, where the rules live, and qt.c owns them for a VERB'S ARGUMENTS -- a mark category, a chooser option -- which the declaration pass has no business knowing. WHAT MUST NOT BE DUPLICATED IS THE MATCHER, so the tier asserts `gb_nearest_word` is defined exactly ONCE and that every file offering a near miss ASKS it, with two controls: a planted sentence that asks nothing is flagged, and one SPLIT ACROSS A LINE BREAK is seen -- the half the old scanner missed. THREE OF THIS SUITE'S OWN CONTROLS CAUGHT MY SCANNER RATHER THAN THE TREE and each is a trap already written down in tests/: the one_copy scanner first reported src/modules/qt.c for the sentence `never "did you mean 'field'?"` INSIDE THE COMMENT EXPLAINING WHY THE FINDER LIVES ELSEWHERE -- the ARGUMENT for a rule read as a breach of it, run_libcurl_floor.sh's smtp.c trap; stripping comments with a sed RANGE then deleted to END OF FILE whenever `/*` and `*/` sat on one line, so the scanner read nothing and would have passed on anything, caught by the control asserting it keeps the code; and `code_only | grep -q` fails the whole pipeline WHEN IT MATCHES, grep closing the pipe and awk dying of SIGPIPE -- FIFTH instance in this tree, recorded in run_rules.sh, which is where the helper was copied from. PLUS A COVER TIER ADDED BECAUSE IT CAUGHT TWO: examples/qt/README.md listed `customer.bas` and nothing else while `orders.bas` and `notes.bas` had been there a day, and run_gui_parse.sh parses the DIRECTORY by glob so the examples could not rot while the page describing them silently did; both directions asserted (nothing undocumented, and no ghost naming a file that is gone) and both proven red. DELIBERATELY NOT BUILT and said so: `stdlib/qt.bas` with builder helpers, the record being buildable as it stands and `qt.check` making it safe to build -- whether helpers earn their place is a question a real consumer answers, and Studio (§0.1) is that consumer. Ten perturbations proven red. Headless bar the `built` tier, which SKIPS without Qt and says that NOTHING was measured about rendering a built window.
./tests/run_qt_loop.sh # THE QT EVENT-LOOP PROBE -- the one thing docs/qt_gui_design.md §5 named as unproven before any Qt code is written, kept MEASURABLE rather than written down once. THE QUESTION IS WHOSE LOOP WAITS: gBASIC's own is ONE `poll()` over the webserver's descriptors, libcurl's descriptors and the root mailbox with timers riding the timeout (`webserver_run_event_loop`), and Qt has a loop of its own which needs to be the one waiting because it watches the keyboard and the mouse -- and because Qt opens NESTED loops, a modal dialog and `QMenu::exec()` and drag-and-drop each running an event loop inside the first. BOTH SHAPES ARE MEASURED, which is the whole design of this suite, BECAUSE THE ONE THAT FAILS DOES NOT FAIL IN ORDINARY OPERATION: direction B (gBASIC owns, Qt pumped with processEvents) runs BOTH loops at full fidelity -- 30 polls, 15 Qt ticks, worst gBASIC gap 52ms -- right up until something opens a dialog, and then gBASIC's poll NEVER RUNS for the dialog's whole life (18 polls, worst gap 651ms over a 600ms dialog), so a webserver stops answering and every timer misses while a message box is up. Direction A (Qt owns, a gBASIC source is a QSocketNotifier) serves 4 of 4 source events WITH A DIALOG OPEN, three of them during it, identical to the no-dialog control -- which is the whole argument for A and is asserted as that EQUALITY rather than as two numbers. Same answer offscreen and on a real Wayland display. SO A IS THE ARCHITECTURE AND B IS RULED OUT BY MEASUREMENT, and the B tier ASSERTS THE STARVATION, so it goes RED if a future Qt makes the nested loop cooperative -- which is worth knowing, since that would reopen a decision taken on this measurement. IT LINKS NO gBASIC CODE, deliberately: every source is a descriptor or a deadline, so a stand-in needs only a real poll over a real descriptor, which means the answer cannot be confounded by the interpreter and the probe stays buildable as the interpreter changes. AND A FINDING §5 DID NOT PREDICT, now a stated contract rather than something each source rediscovers: a QSocketNotifier is LEVEL-TRIGGERED, so the first version -- which never read its pipe -- fired 545,066 TIMES FOR ONE BYTE, the same defect class PLAT-HTTP already recorded ("the first delivery loop re-reported undrained bytes EVERY iteration ... a loop that never went idle"); every gBASIC source registered with Qt must be DRAINED by its handler, and the tier asserts a CEILING on the activation count because "it was served" alone passes on the broken version. AND IT CARRIES THE REQUIREMENT FOR THE FIRST BACKEND COMMIT since 2026-10-17, asked for after the question "will gBASIC still run on a text-only computer?" -- IT WILL, COMPLETELY (the lean release builds GTK_AVAILABLE=0, this repo's own test binary carries HAVE_GTK=0, and a program with no `window` block is untouched; only OPENING a window needs a display, and a window block still parses, validates and introspects with no Qt at all, which is why the declaration shipped first). THE HAZARD IS WORSE THAN "IT WILL NOT WORK": with no display Qt calls qFatal AND ABORTS -- measured, SIGABRT, exit 134, core dumped -- and qFatal CANNOT BE CAUGHT, so by the time the process knows it is gone, and a crash takes block-buffered stdout with it so a program printing its progress prints NOTHING and the failure reads as line 1. Exactly the defect GTK produced and run_gi.sh records (a widget built before gtk_init, exit 139). AND THE ENVIRONMENT CANNOT BE READ INSTEAD, which was the obvious guess and is wrong: Qt found Wayland through XDG_RUNTIME_DIR with WAYLAND_DISPLAY UNSET, so its detection is richer than any reimplementation and would drift as Qt changes. So the refusal asks QT ITSELF IN A FORKED CHILD, a process we can afford to lose, and the parent reads a status -- PROVEN rather than planned: no on a machine where Qt aborts, yes on a real display, yes under QT_QPA_PLATFORM=offscreen (so a headless server can still render to an image, which stays a thing A USER may set), and THE PARENT EXITS CLEANLY IN EVERY CASE so a diagnostic can still be printed. All four asserted, since any one alone is satisfied by a probe that always says the same thing. THE REQUIREMENT IS NOW MET AND ITS ENFORCEMENT MOVED: the backend shipped the same day and run_qt_backend.sh asserts the same four things THROUGH THE INTERPRETER, so this tier is the measurement of the MECHANISM and that one is the measurement of the PRODUCT -- both are kept, because a probe that works while the module does not is exactly the state nobody would notice. WHAT IT DOES NOT ANSWER AND SAYS SO: re-entry into the EVALUATOR (the probe proves a real Qt signal reaches a C callback through a moc translation unit via one generic relay, and `invoke_function` from there is still inferred from `gi_signal_marshal`, which ships it for GTK); WINDOWS, where QSocketNotifier works on SOCKETS ONLY and direction A will need QWinEventNotifier for anything else -- named now rather than discovered during the port; and threads, actors and more than one window. NOTE the pipefail trap landed here too, SEVENTH instance in this tree: the probe asserts its own expectations and exits nonzero when they fail, which is EXACTLY what the modal run is meant to do, and under `set -euo pipefail` that status propagated out of the command substitution and ended the suite SILENTLY mid-tier with no FAIL line. Skips cleanly without Qt6Widgets development files, without `moc` (naming it, since Qt6 can be installed without the tools) or without a C++ compiler -- and each skip says the loop question was NOT measured.
./tests/run_portable.sh # GNU coreutils behaviour where the tools are BSD (tests/portable.sh), AND SINCE 2026-10-17 tests/premise.sh, which is the OTHER harness file and answers a different question: `portable.sh` supplies a missing TOOL, `premise.sh` answers whether this machine can BUILD THE PREMISE a tier needs -- does `chmod` govern reading, is there a `mkfifo`, a peak-RSS meter, a real symlink, a settable stack. It exists because SIX SUITES FAILED ON WINDOWS FOR REASONS THAT WERE FACTS ABOUT THE HARNESS (windows_port_status.md §30) and a suite that fails for the wrong reason is one people learn to ignore. IT IS ALSO NOT tests/build_has.sh, which asks what THE BINARY can do and says in as many words why it asks the binary and not the machine; a tier often needs both, one to know the feature exists and the other to know its premise can be built, and both files now cross-reference the other so nobody merges them. THE PROBES ASK THE MACHINE, NOT `platform()`, and that is stronger rather than lazy: `chmod 000` also fails to bite on a Linux FAT or CIFS mount and under root, a container may ship no /usr/bin/time, and MSYS `ln -s` silently COPIES -- so a test on the platform NAME would be right about Windows and wrong about all four. WHICH WAY A BROKEN PREMISE FAILS DECIDES THE TREATMENT, and the two cases are both in the tree: in run_scratch a copy-instead-of-link SATISFIES the conclusion (deleting a copy leaves the original alone, which is what that tier concludes) so a failed premise must FAIL, while in run_glob the same copy makes `through=` answer 1 and the tier reports that the walk DOES follow links -- a false failure about gBASIC from a fact about `ln -s` -- so there it must SKIP. THE HAZARD THE FILE ADDS IS A SILENT SHRINK and is why the tripwire lives here: a suite that CALLS a probe without SOURCING it gets 127, which `if ! premise_x` reads as "cannot", so every guarded tier SKIPS and the suite stays GREEN while measuring nothing -- demonstrated rather than reasoned, with a vacuity control on the scanner. IT CAUGHT ITS OWN STALE PATTERN WITHIN THE HOUR, which is the best endorsement available: the file was renamed from `capabilities.sh` (one character from `run_capabilities.sh`, which is about the unrelated `--capabilities` flag, the `scoring`/`scorecard` rename again) and the sweep could not match the filename spelled `premise\.sh` INSIDE A REGEX, so the one place the rename missed was the check, and the check named it. PLUS THE FORCING SEAM, which is how a skip path gets proven at all: on a full machine every probe answers yes, so every skip is UNREACHABLE and untested, and each probe reads its cache from the environment (`_premise_rss=no ./tests/run_read_at.sh`) so the branch can be exercised -- that is how each new skip was checked to say the right thing rather than merely to compile. AND IT FIXED A PRE-EXISTING DEFECT OF EXACTLY ITS OWN CLASS: the /proc tier extracted `gb_have_proc` with `declare -f` but NOT the helper it calls, so it printed `_gb_portable_forced: command not found` on every run and then answered CORRECTLY BY LUCK (the missing call returns 127, the `&&` short-circuits, and the real test below it runs) -- which also meant `GB_PORTABLE_FORCE=noproc` could not be exercised here, leaving the answers-no half of that function unreachable on every machine in the gate. AND SINCE 2026-10-18 IT HOLDS THE BACKTICK TRIPWIRE, at the ELEVENTH instance of a trap this tree keeps paying for: a backtick inside a DOUBLE-QUOTED shell string is command substitution, and a message quoting a name the way this project quotes names (`load`, `qt.confirm`, `unreachable`) gets run. IT IS INVISIBLE WHERE IT IS WRITTEN -- the substitution usually fails quietly, so the line prints with the quoted word MISSING and reads as an ordinary sentence, and in a `fail` message it only ever surfaces on the day a tier legitimately goes red. FOUR WERE LIVE and none was written by the session that found them: two in `fail` messages (run_ldap, run_library_tiers), two in a tool's `printf` (three words run as commands), plus one that had been WORKED AROUND rather than fixed by spelling the word as `printf '%s' check` so the substitution happened to produce it. tools/scan_shell_backticks.py is NARROW ON PURPOSE, reading only `pass`/`fail`/`printf`/`echo` lines AND EVERY FUNCTION THE FILE ITSELF DEFINES -- widened 2026-10-18 at the TWELFTH instance after a MEASURED MISS, a new suite writing three offenders of which it caught the two on `pass`/`fail` lines and missed the one on its own `refuse` helper, since nearly every suite here has such a helper and its first argument IS the message, so the four names were never the boundary of the class but only the names that had been noticed; DERIVED FROM THE FILE rather than listed, because a list is what a new helper silently stays off, with that half asserted separately in the selftest (disabled, the `refuse` line goes unflagged) and measured across all 190 suites with no new findings -- because a general scan drowns in false positives -- a `'`-prefixed gBASIC comment inside a heredoc, an awk pattern matching a Markdown fence, a deliberate substitution -- and a tripwire whose false positives look like its true ones is one somebody turns off. Its own `--selftest` plants one and requires the two safe spellings beside it to stay silent, since "no offenders" is equally satisfied by a scanner that read nothing. The release script's own backtick tripwire has covered its container string since the third instance and could never see these. AND SINCE 2026-10-19 IT ALSO HOLDS THE `set -e` MODE-CHANGE TRIPWIRE, a sibling trap found the same way: the `set +e` ... `set -e` pair is a SAVE AND RESTORE only in a file whose header really is `set -euo pipefail`, and copied into one whose header is `set -uo pipefail` it does not restore anything -- IT TURNS THE MODE ON. The only thing that then fires is an unguarded command that FAILS, which on a red path is a `grep` finding nothing, so the cost is paid EXACTLY WHEN A SUITE IS GOING RED: it dies mid-tier with no verdict and no FAIL line, which reads as still running. MEASURED across four suites (three written that day) and BITTEN IN ONE OF THEM by a line added to IMPROVE a failure report -- 26 mode changes removed, the `set +e` lines kept because they truthfully say a failure is expected there. The scan reads each DASH-PREFIXED WORD of the header rather than the whole line, which its own control caught: `set -uo pipefail` CONTAINS the letter `e`, in "pipefail", so a `case "$hdr" in *e*)` skipped every file in the tree and the check passed while reading nothing. Two controls, since one alone is satisfied by a scan that flags everything or nothing: a planted copy is flagged AND a suite that really declares `-e` is left alone.
./tests/run_capabilities.sh # `gbasic --capabilities` (docs/library_trust_design.md §1.3) -- the surface a scanner in another project DERIVES instead of hardcoding, whose failure mode is NOT a wrong label but a MISSING one: the day gBASIC gains a twelfth network module and the table does not know, a library using it scans as harmless and every scan after is silently incomplete. Coverage is asserted BOTH DIRECTIONS against two lists the interpreter already maintains for its OWN reasons (`library_is_native_qualifier`, because alias collision refusal depends on it; `dispatch_only[]`, because has_builtin does), which is the same argument as deriving from a list something else needs -- a list kept only for this purpose is a list that rots. CARRIES THE has_builtin TIER since 2026-10-11 and it fixed a defect MEASURED ON THE PUBLISHED 0.7.0 ARTIFACTS: `has_builtin` is documented as the way a program degrades gracefully across releases, and on two of three published artifacts it could not answer the question it exists for -- it affirmed `password_hash`, `sha256` and `compress` on builds that cannot run any of them, so a program that CHECKED FIRST then called crashed on the branch it had checked for. Worse than DOGFOOD 46, the same defect inverted, where a careful program merely took a weaker path. THE REGISTRY IS COMPILED CONDITIONALLY NOW, so twenty names move with the build; `base64_*`, `hex_*`, `base32_*`, `random_bytes` and `secure_token` deliberately do NOT, having been MEASURED to work on the leanest build there is. AND THE INVERSE WAS FOUND THE SAME DAY: `pbkdf2_sha256`, `pbkdf2_sha512` and `scrypt` were never in the registry at all, so has_builtin DENIED three key-derivation functions the build could run -- found by probing every registry name against the published lean archive and then asking what that archive could do which the registry denied. `compress` WAS THE SHARPEST CASE AND THE CAUSE IS WORTH KEEPING: zlib was the ONE optional builtin family whose NAME TEST sat inside its `#if`, so the function vanished from the dispatch entirely rather than refusing -- the inverse of this project's own stated convention that a missing dependency turns the affected feature into a clean runtime error. Hence `has_builtin("compress")` true and `unknown function: compress` from the same binary; and the old diagnostic ended with *`has_builtin("compress")` asks without raising*, so the message pointed at the probe that was lying. THE INVARIANT IS BUILD-AGNOSTIC, which is what makes it testable without a second compile: `has_builtin(n)` is true EXACTLY WHEN calling `n` does not refuse for unavailability, which holds on a full build, a lean one and everything between, and fails in BOTH directions -- which matters because the two defects point opposite ways. A name the dispatch does not know fails outright rather than being classified, whatever has_builtin says. THE ARITY TABLE IS HAND-WRITTEN and the COVERAGE CHECK is what keeps it honest: every name guarded in src/builtins.c must have a probe, so a new optional builtin cannot be added to the registry without one. WHAT IT CANNOT COVER AND SAYS SO: it runs against the binary this suite built and the suite builds everything, so the case the defect SHIPPED IN -- a build with the dependency absent -- is only reachable from a lean compile; both perturbations were proven red against one by hand, and the measured half lives in tools/build-release-tarball.sh's library sweep
./tests/run_limitations.sh # DOGFOOD.md's accepted-limitations ledger, held to the truth by RUNNING it. Every live bullet is a NEGATIVE CONTROL: the probe asserts the limitation STILL HOLDS and goes RED WHEN IT IS FIXED, naming the entry to strike -- the shape run_docs_gate.sh uses for a performance claim. IT EXISTS BECAUSE FIVE OF FOURTEEN BULLETS WERE FALSE on 2026-09-01: "a raise cannot be caught" (retired by PLAT-ERR, and it carried the whole PRE-VALIDATE DOCTRINE with it), `call(args) = value` misparsing (PLAT-BRACE's own RESIDUAL tier), keywords after a dot (all 16 tried now resolve), `find`/`match` missing the sentinels (PLAT-EQ), and no line continuation (PLAT-CONT). Each had been fixed by a shipped phase with a passing suite IN THE SAME TREE, none was catchable by reading, and the entries were cited as design justification elsewhere -- stdlib/consolidate.bas picked a field name over one of them. This is the rot PLAT-DEBT built run_docs_gate for, in a file that gate cannot see: it reads docs/, and DOGFOOD.md is at the root. THE CONTROL TIER IS WHAT MAKES THE PROBES MEAN ANYTHING: several assert an ABSENCE ("the output does NOT say parse error"), which is equally satisfied by a harness that ran nothing, so the five STRUCK bullets get the opposite check -- the limitation must be provably GONE -- which doubles as a regression guard on PLAT-ERR/BRACE/EQ/CONT and the keyword-fields work, none of which knows this ledger cites it. COVER runs both directions: a live bullet must be probed or named in a deliberately tiny NOT_MECHANICAL list with a reason (2 entries: gi static class functions, which run_gi owns, and valgrind on riscv64, unobservable from here), and a struck bullet must have a control, since a struck bullet with nothing behind it is just a deleted one. COVER READS THE LEDGER'S OWN "LIVE LIST" TABLE AS WELL AS THE ACCEPTED-LIMITATIONS BULLETS since 2026-10-19, which closed this suite's own blind spot: DOGFOOD.md calls that table "the part you act on" and it was the one region no probe could reach -- and its own prose records a row that still read as open having in fact been fixed, so the rot this suite exists to prevent was happening inside it. BOUNDED TO THE FIRST TABLE OF THAT SECTION, by where the table ends rather than by a line count, and WHAT THAT LEAVES OUT IS NAMED RATHER THAN SKIPPED QUIETLY: the RANKED table below it is the 2026-10-13 audit's archive, 50 of 52 struck, and the handful still live there have no probes, which is its own increment since each needs a measurement of its own. THE VACUITY GUARD IS ABOUT THE TABLE AND NOT ABOUT LIVE ROWS, which is the whole distinction: the list is LEGITIMATELY EMPTY whenever nothing is open (it was, from 2026-10-14 to 2026-10-19), so requiring a live row would fail on a healthy tree while requiring none would pass on markers that had moved. Two perturbations proven red: the probe unregistered (the live row is reported unclaimed) and the table marker moved (the guard fires rather than reporting a clean sweep of nothing). The first row it carries is `qt.run()`-inside-a-function starving every loop-delivered source, whose probe is a PAIR -- the function form must HANG and the identical program with `qt.run()` at the top level must tick and exit 0, since "it hung" is equally satisfied by a fixture that cannot run at all. All five tiers proven red. Headless, GI-independent, never skips (bar the gi probe without the module)
./tests/run_silent_traps.sh # Failures that reported WITHOUT a diagnostic, promoted to raises (docs/warning_model_design.md §7 rows 1 and 3). Both shared one signature: an UNLOCATED bare line on stderr, a result of `nothing`, and EXIT CODE 0 -- and since `nothing` is a legitimate value, a caller could not tell the failure from a real one while CI saw success. `goto`/`gosub` to a label that does not exist printed and then ABANDONED THE REST OF THE FUNCTION, so a typo'd label silently truncated it; an out-of-range array READ printed and yielded `nothing` while out-of-range ASSIGNMENT called runtime_error_raise properly two lines away in the same file. Extended 2026-08-23 with the five typed-value modifiers (date/datetime/time/file/dir), which had the identical signature and sat FOUR LINES from `USD` in the same dispatch function -- USD raised, they printed, and that neighbouring inconsistency is what made it a bug rather than a policy. docs/text_design.md and stdlib/ari.bas had both CLAIMED these raised for months; the claim was measured, found false, and then made TRUE rather than weakened. None of these were ever warnings -- they were raises nobody had written, which is why they shipped independent of the PLAT-WARN channel. Tiers assert three things the printed line never gave: a NONZERO exit, a LOCATED `runtime error at file:line:col` diagnostic, and that execution did NOT continue past the failure; plus that both are now CATCHABLE with `on error goto next`, and a write-path tier so the half that was always right stays that way. Cost zero test changes: 333 negative and 232 examples passed unchanged, which is the evidence that nothing in the tree relied on the old silence.
./tests/run_warning_model.sh # PLAT-WARN: the warning channel (docs/warning_model_design.md). The feature is not any single warning -- it is that SUPPRESSION makes an aggressive diagnostic affordable and ESCALATION makes it enforceable. Before this, every warning gBASIC shipped had to be near-zero-false-positive because a program had no way to say "I meant that here", which is precisely why the worst traps stayed silent: a discarded return (the mechanism behind web._serve_pool supervising nobody through a tagged release), `contains(s, "b*")` searching for a literal asterisk, a blind shadow of a global. Measured before building: 2215 bare call statements in the tree, 173 discarding a non-`nothing` return from a gBASIC function, ~68 of them deliberate -- unusable as a warning without an opt-out, routine with one. Two tiers carry the design's load: NO_RULE2 (PLAT-ERR's rule 2 re-raises a pending ERROR at frame exit and must NOT leak here, or every unchecked warning becomes an error -- which is why the warning pending flag is SEPARATE, not a severity tag on the error's) and SOFT_NAME (`warning` resolves AFTER the environment walk, so a variable shadows it and `r.warning` still parses -- that one placement is what lets the whole channel exist with ZERO reserved words; `examples/edgar/monitor_harness_test.bas` uses `board.warning` five times and never notices). Other tiers: the four modes; claiming (bare `warning` claims, `warning.field` does not); DYNAMIC mode lookup, deliberately unlike frame-local error mode, because a failure is the callee's business but the noise budget is the caller's; escalation attributed to the warning's OWN site with error.severity; independence (an error and a warning pending at once, read separately); and unused-result with its two exemptions (builtins, so `append`'s 1101 bare calls stay quiet; and `return nothing`, the void convention, exempt BY VALUE). NOTE the raise is `warning(...)`, a BUILTIN CALL: `IDENT expression` as a statement form costs 4 shift/reduce conflicts, measured, and `error` gets the statement form only because it is a hard token.
./tests/run_error_model.sh  # PLAT-ERR: `on error` is FRAME-scoped (docs/error_model_design.md). The old model's defect was proven, not suspected — docs/ai/ERRORS.md showed that under process-global `on error resume next` a function could not catch a raise and return a fallback, because the caller's statement was abandoned by a generation check regardless of what the callee returned, and error.clear() did not rescue it. That single property forced the pre-validate doctrine, which is TOCTOU-broken for anything external, and drove four independent inventions of a catchable-error convention the language never blessed (try_decode, process.which, web.pool's {ok,why}, the webserver's err_out). These fixtures were written BEFORE the implementation, from the design, and the first — catch_return — is exactly the case the old model could not pass. The two anti-silence rules get their own tiers because they ARE the argument for this shape over VB6's: rule 1 (a second raise while one is pending ESCAPES the frame, so the last error cannot shadow the first) and rule 2 (returning, or ending the program, with an unacknowledged pending error re-raises rather than vanishing) — together, no raise can disappear, and forgetting a check produces noise instead of silence. Other tiers: propagation across two unarmed frames (a callee is NOT covered by its caller's handler); disarm-in-handler (a raise inside a goto-label section propagates instead of looping); re-arm; loop fall-through (per-item failure checked per item); `e = error` snapshot and `error e` re-raise preserving code/source/details and the ORIGINAL trace; structured `error {record}`; trace contents; `resume` as an ordinary identifier; error.clear(). NOTE the byte-exactness tier: the fatal stderr line must stay identical to the old format, which is what let all 333 negative cases pass this change UNCHANGED — the compatibility hinge, since the report moved from raise time to whichever frame declines. A THIRD ANTI-SILENCE RULE LANDED 2026-09-23 (DOGFOOD 34, tier first_raise_wins): while a raise is UNWINDING the first one wins, so nothing that happens on the way out can replace it. `decode(read(f))` on a missing file reported `decode expects a string` -- the read's raise destroyed, decode handed a `nothing`, and decode complaining about the symptom it had just been given, naming the one call in the line that is NOT at fault. MEASURED across five argument positions, TWO were right and THREE were wrong and nothing in the source said which you would get: arithmetic and user functions check `error_action_pending` after an operand, three builtins did not. FIXED IN ONE PLACE (`runtime_error_raise`) RATHER THAN IN EACH BUILTIN, because a rule re-established at two hundred call sites is one a new builtin silently leaves out -- which is exactly how those three came to differ from the two beside them. THE LOAD-BEARING CHECK IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE FIX: a builtin with nothing in flight must still complain in its OWN words, or "the first raise wins" is satisfied by a build that stopped reporting type errors at all. The OVER-CORRECTION (guarding on "an error exists" rather than "one is in flight") is caught by the pre-existing `rule1_escape` fixture, which is the old rule acting as a control on the new one. AND THE SHARPEST TIER ASSERTS A FILE'S CONTENTS: `write` truncates before it has anything to put in it, so a failed argument getting through would empty a file the program never meant to touch, and a return value proves nothing about the disk. PLUS THE POSITION TIER (DOGFOOD 27): a raise names the failing EXPRESSION, not the statement containing it. `x = a[5]` and `print(a[5])` both blamed column 1 and `print("x " + string(a[5]))` blamed the `+` -- a reader taught that `:5:1` means line 5 column 1 looks at column 1, finds `print`, and concludes `print` is broken, because A `:1` READS AS A CARET AND IS NOT ONE. Nothing had to be computed: parser.y already places index and field nodes at `@2`, the `[` and the `.`, which is why the PROMPT was the one place that got it right (there the subexpression is the whole statement). THE EXPECTED COLUMN IS COMPUTED FROM THE SOURCE, not written down, since four numbers in a file are a transcript of whatever the binary said; it fails both on a build reporting the statement's column and on one reporting a constant. TWO CONTROLS: a division, whose operator carries its own position, so the tier cannot pass by hunting for brackets; and a statement-wide fault, which must still report column 1. TWO OF MY OWN CHECKS COULD NOT HAVE FAILED and both were found by running them: the first control used a bare identifier, which is a PARSE error rather than a runtime one, so it failed against a correct build; and `got=$(./gbasic ... | sed ...)` under `pipefail` takes the pipeline's status, and the run under test is SUPPOSED to fail -- so the assignment failed, `set -e` ended the suite, and it printed no FAIL line at all under a green-looking scrollback, the same trap run_http.sh records from the other direction.
./tests/run_library_scope.sh # a library's unqualified call resolves to its OWN function first. THE RULE WAS RATIFIED AND ITS DIAGNOSTIC REWRITTEN 2026-09-01, on the gdash session's report: the fix reached the BUILT-IN case too (a library defining `lines` calls its own from inside; outside, the built-in still wins) and Matthew ruled that IS the rule -- your own first -- so the diagnostic became a NOTE rather than a warning (docs/warning_model_design.md §12: prints, silenced by `on warning ignore`, NEVER escalates under `on warning stop`, never sets the pending flag). Two defects fixed with it, both in the diagnostic and neither in the behaviour. Its TEXT said "unqualified calls use the built-in", true OUTSIDE the library and FALSE at the declaration it pointed at. And its POSITION came from the CALLER: registration-time warnings fire while the `load` statement runs, so the line was a line in the CALLING file printed beside the LIBRARY's path -- gdash inferred the count skipped comment lines, but measured, three libraries loaded on lines 1/2/3 reported 1:1/2:1/3:1 regardless of where the function sat. WORSE, AND UNREPORTED: position is the DEDUPLICATION key, so every collision in one library shared that one load line and A LIBRARY SHADOWING THREE BUILT-INS WARNED ABOUT ONE. `runtime_warn_at`/`warn_fmt_at`/`note_fmt_at` thread the declaration's own line through; the LOOKUP-time sites keep `warn_fmt`, where current_line IS the call site they mean. The tier's last two checks -- inside the library its own wins, outside the built-in does -- correctly PASS on the pre-change binary: they are the CONTROL asserting the behaviour the message must match, and that behaviour was deliberately not touched. Proven red otherwise (1 of 3, position 1:1, old text). It used to go through the same backward scan every caller uses -- LAST REGISTRATION WINS -- so a library's internals were rewired by whatever was LOADED AFTER it: with `alpha` and `beta` both defining `helper` and `alpha.outer` calling it unqualified, loading alpha then beta made alpha.outer return BETA's, and the other order did not. A library could not call itself reliably and the answer depended on a load order it does not control. THE FIX NEEDED TWO SITES and the second was INVISIBLE until a root program happened to define the same name: `function_resolve` is not the only path -- an earlier dispatch consults `function_find_local` DIRECTLY and would otherwise hand a root-program function to a library calling itself. The first version fixed only the resolver and shipped that hole; the root case caught it, and instrumenting (rather than reasoning further) is what located it, since no debug line printed at all -- proof the code was never reached. THE CONTROL IS WHAT KEEPS IT FROM SEALING LIBRARIES OFF: a library calling a name it does NOT define must still reach the global table, and the root program must still get its own -- both asserted, plus qualified calls untouched. The load-order tier states the property in one line: the SAME program with the two loads swapped must give the same answer. Self-checking, because the failure returns a perfectly good string from the WRONG function and a golden would have recorded "beta" as expected. No golden moved across 83 suites
./tests/run_keyword_fields.sh # a keyword used as a FIELD NAME is a name, not a keyword. rc6 made keywords usable as field names but only HALFWAY: the lexer classified them as keywords and the grammar then mapped every keyword token to a canonical LOWERCASE spelling (`kw_name("or")`), so `{ OR: 1 }` stored the key "or", `r["OR"]` missed, and `{ OR: 1, or: 2 }` produced a record whose keys() were `or,or` -- two spellings the language otherwise treats as distinct, collapsed silently into one. WHETHER A FIELD NAME KEPT ITS CASE DEPENDED ON WHETHER IT HAPPENED TO COLLIDE WITH A KEYWORD, which is the inconsistency fixed. FIXED IN THE LEXER RATHER THAN THE GRAMMAR, and that is the whole reason it works: emitting TOKEN_IDENT hands the parser the ORIGINAL SPAN which it copies verbatim, where every grammar-side option had only the token's IDENTITY and would have had to reconstruct the text -- the leading one, giving all 46 keyword tokens a semantic value, leaks a string per `if`/`then`/`end` in every program since bison does not free a value a reduction ignores. THE CONTEXT TEST IS EXACT: inside an open `{` (a depth the lexer already tracks, added a week earlier for LINE CONTINUATION) a word followed by `:` can only be a record key, since a brace MODIFIER contains no colon and `consider` recognises branches by column; after a `.` it can only be a field. BOTH HALVES WERE NEEDED -- fixing only the literal left two keys stored and one reachable, which is worse than the original defect. The control tier is what keeps it from being "treat keywords as names": `to = 5` must still be a parse error. Self-checking, because the defect produced a perfectly ordinary record and a golden would have recorded `or,or` as expected. NO GOLDEN MOVED across 82 suites, which is the evidence nothing depended on the lowercasing
./tests/run_brace_modifiers.sh # PLAT-BRACE: modifier clauses are written in braces (docs/brace_modifier_design.md). The paren spelling made `name(caseless) = "joe"` and `kind(x) = "record"` the SAME tokens in the same order, so the parser had to guess -- ninety lines of lookahead (`modifier_lparen_ahead`) whose own comment admitted the identifier-argument case could not be closed at token delivery. A brace cannot open a call, so there is nothing to guess. Tiers: FORMS (every modifier position in the new spelling), RESIDUAL (the case that could not be fixed, pinned for two phases as a permanent defect in negative_clause_residual.bas, now parses as the ordinary call it always was -- this tier is the point of the whole change), PAREN_FORM_REFUSED, GUESSER_REMOVED and MACHINERY_REMOVED (source tripwires: the guesser is gone, and so are the two things it drove -- the lexer's raw `(...)` span mode and the parser's source-wide `function NAME` pre-scan, both of which kept compiling after the caller went. §1 argues the paren spelling's cost by counting the three string scanners a clause passed through, so leaving one in the source left that argument half false. Dead code that still parses is how a retired construct comes back). Headless, never skips
./tests/run_for_each_index.sh # `for each item, i in list` -- the element AND its position. THE SEMANTICS DID NOT CHANGE AND THAT IS THE POINT: gBASIC has no references and is not getting them (an actor is fork+exec so a reference cannot cross `spawn`, and `encode` TOTALITY is what lets an `agent` run sit in a store between HTTP requests -- references admit cycles and that property dies), so a loop body cannot write through the element variable and `item.x = 1` is SILENTLY DISCARDED. MEASURED BEFORE BUILDING ANYTHING: that mistake appears EXACTLY ONCE across stdlib, examples and tests, in examples/array_cow_test.bas, the fixture that asserts the semantics -- so the fix is to make the CORRECT idiom cheap rather than make the incorrect one work, since `list[i] = item` is an lvalue PATH and paths write in place. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS THE WRITE-BACK, asserted as a DIFFERENCE against its CONTROL: the same loop WITHOUT `list[i] = item` must change NOTHING -- "the array changed" alone passes on a language with references and "it did not change" alone on one where the body does nothing, so both halves are required. THE SNAPSHOT TIER is what makes a single-pass rewrite expressible at all: the array expression is evaluated ONCE and a write detaches the variable's store through copy-on-write, so the walk sees the ENTRY values and APPENDING INSIDE THE LOOP DOES NOT EXTEND IT -- a stronger guarantee than Python (which skips elements) or JavaScript (which can loop forever), and asserted rather than assumed. GRAMMAR asserts bison still reports ZERO conflicts, measured before AND after, which is not decoration in a project that rejected `IDENT expression` as a statement form over 4 MEASURED conflicts -- `IDENT COMMA IDENT` in a `for` head is exactly the shape that could collide with the counted loop and the plain element form. The element and the index may not share a name (neither binding is wrong alone, so nothing would raise: the index would overwrite the element and the body would read a number where it expected a record), refused at parse time beside CONTROLS that the plain forms are untouched. ALSO CARRIES WARNING 2107 (`discarded write`): a write to the element that NOTHING READS. THE DISCRIMINATOR IS NOT THE ASSIGNMENT BUT WHETHER THE VALUE IS READ AGAIN, and that is the whole design -- writing to the element is LEGITIMATE and ordinary (`row = normalise(row)` then `print row.name`; `row.label = tag(row)` then `send(row)`), so a syntactic "you assigned to a loop variable" rule flags CORRECT CODE, which is exactly the shape that got the blind-shadow warning built, MEASURED AT 287 FALSE POSITIVES across 103 files, and reverted. MEASURED BEFORE BUILDING: 3 writes exist tree-wide, the rule fires on exactly 1 (the fixture demonstrating the discard) and the write-back idiom stays silent WITHOUT special-casing since `list[i] = item` READS item; RE-MEASURED AFTER by running every example and test, 0 false positives. Asserted as a DIFFERENCE with FIVE CONTROLS outnumbering the positive case, since "it warns" alone passes on a rule warning about everything and "it is silent" alone on one that never fires -- both perturbations proven red. Deliberately CONSERVATIVE and erring towards silence: source POSITIONS not dataflow, so a later read suppresses, a goto/gosub/label suppresses (source order stops implying execution order) and an unmodelled construct suppresses -- a missed dead write costs nothing, a false one costs the channel's credibility. Affordable ONLY because the index form shipped the same day, so the message names a remedy THAT EXISTS. The fixture's own control carries `on warning ignore`, which doubles as the test the opt-out is real. Six perturbations proven red including index_name never freed -- PLAT-OPTPARAM's lesson, where an unfreed parser field turned 22 suites red on their own valgrind tiers. AND THE RULE WAS NARROWED 2026-09-25 ON A REPORT FROM THE gBASIC STUDIO BENCH, which is the first FALSE POSITIVE this channel has produced in the wild: `e.entry.text = "filled in"` on a Gtk.Entry handle held in the element warned that the write was discarded, AND THE ENTRY WAS FILLED IN -- the element is a copy, but a handle inside it refers to the same object either way, so the message was simply false. IT STOPS JUDGING PAST ONE HOP, because what separates that from `e.inner.x = 1` on a nested RECORD -- genuinely discarded -- is the KIND of the value at the first hop, known only at run time, while this analysis is a source-position scan; one of the two readings makes the sentence a lie and the stated policy is that a missed dead write costs nothing where a false one costs the channel its credibility. THE COST IS ASSERTED RATHER THAN COMMENTED: a two-hop write over a nested record goes unjudged EVEN THOUGH IT IS DISCARDED, pinned so that anyone re-widening this is told what they are trading, with the BOUNDARY control that a ONE-hop field write is still reported -- without which the narrowing could quietly retire the whole warning, which is exactly what the too-far perturbation does. The half that says the silence is CORRECT needs a reference-kinded value and lives in run_gi.sh. Three more perturbations proven red. Headless, GI-independent, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_exponent_literal.sh # scientific notation as a LITERAL -- and THE SILENT TRAP BEHIND ITS ABSENCE, which is the half the ledger bullet understated. `1e20` lexed as the number 1 beside the identifier `e20`, which the grammar reads as a DURATION, and the ledger recorded that as "a misleading message". MEASURED 2026-09-23 it was worse in three ways at once: the message was UNLOCATED and went straight to stderr, BYPASSING THE DIAGNOSTICS SINK so `--json-diagnostics` emitted a non-JSON line into a JSON stream (the defect run_parse_exit.sh exists for); the program then ANSWERED `0 seconds`; and it EXITED 0 and carried on. That is the bare-line/plausible-value/successful-exit signature run_silent_traps.sh was built for, IN A THIRD PLACE IT HAD NEVER REACHED -- and not reachable only through `1e20`, since `1 fortnight` had the same shape. So there are two changes here and they are ONE STORY: the literal removes the commonest way into that path, the refusal removes the path. THE LOAD-BEARING TIER IS THE ORACLE: each literal must equal `number("...")` on the same text, which is the route the whole tree has been using instead -- a value comparison alone passes on a lexer consistently off by a factor, because the expected value would be written as a literal too. The CONSERVATIVE block (hex, durations, a variable named `e2`) keeps "it is a literal" from being satisfied by a lexer that swallowed the identifier after every number. AND ONE CHECK CAME FROM A PERTURBATION RATHER THAN FROM DESIGN: with the digit requirement after `e` removed, EVERY OTHER CHECK IN THE FILE STAYED GREEN, because a greedy lexer makes `1e` a perfectly good number 1 -- it cannot be asserted in the fixture, since a fixture that could ask has to contain a line that does not parse, so it lives in the runner. The REFUSAL tier asserts the four things run_parse_exit.sh established (LOCATED, NONZERO, NOTHING RAN, and it reaches the SINK as JSON with nothing else on the stream) beside the CONTROL that a real duration is untouched, plus a SOURCE TRIPWIRE that no bare `fprintf(stderr` returns to the duration path, which no behavioural check can see. VALGRIND runs TWICE, because the fixture never reaches the refusal path and that is the path whose string ownership changed -- proven by the perturbation that leaks the unit, caught there and NOWHERE ELSE. Five perturbations proven red. Headless, GI-independent, never skips (bar valgrind)
./tests/run_lock_signal.sh # a program that has taken a `lock` must still DIE BY THE SIGNAL that killed it.
